  - **Account:** exactly one new `real_accounts` row whose identity equals the attempt's (the Safe included), exactly one new **active primary** `real_passkeys` row, and no other account sharing its sub-org, owner, or Safe. The bounded wallet-account read, bound to exactly the created account, succeeded (the new account's wallet account id came from it).
  - **Unrelated data:** `payment_attempts`, `backup_passkey_enrollments`, `passkey_revocation_attempts`, and `passkey_revocation_resolutions` unchanged (rows and content hashes); the 2 pre-existing attempts, 2 pre-existing Real accounts, and 7 pre-existing passkeys byte-identical; no catalog change; no idle transaction, session, or lock.
  - **Operator poller, dry run (no commit flag) against that dispatch:** `refused` / `already_terminal`, `committed: false`; all 9 tables identical before and after.
  - **The created Turnkey sub-organization is permanent testnet state, and its owner address must never be funded** (the Safe is the user's account; the owner is only signing authority).
- **Paths that live run did not exercise.** It took the **immediate-`COMPLETED`** path: Turnkey's submit response was already terminal, so no polling ran. **Not yet occurred live:** bounded polling, a submit timeout or abort, a transport failure or unparseable answer, a non-2xx answer carrying an activity id, a definitive `FAILED` / `REJECTED` (and the evidence-bound revert), an ambiguous `turnkey_created` write, and any review outcome. Those branches are covered by the offline tests (the fake parent Turnkey and the mutation-checked guards), the offline Neon-wire tests, and the disposable-branch adapter smoke as documented above — **not** by any live Turnkey run.
- **Closed, and what is explicitly outside this slice.** Evidence capture is complete. Still open, each a separate piece of work: the operator provenance resolver for an uncertain create (not built); adoption (none; automatic adoption stays disabled); same-body resubmission (a candidate resolver primitive only); and the **public-deployment prerequisites** — no hosted deployment exists yet, rate limiting (M2) and a full script CSP (M3) are still required before any public deployment, a host-specific RP ID and origin, the platform's function-duration limit (the registration path's Turnkey stage alone can take up to about 23 s, and the timing constants remain provisional with no `maxDuration` set), and the HSTS and cookie decisions. **Option 3 is unchanged:** an uncertain create stays `provisioning_in_flight` / review-only and is never auto-discovered, adopted, retried, or re-sent, and a recorded observation moves nothing.

### Human-Readable Account Identity (Handles)

**Status: COMPLETE/CLOSED** — Human-Readable Account Identity / Handles. The closure covers: a durable, immutable handle registry (never recycled; reserved names seeded as rows); optional display names; a fresh-passkey-confirmed handle claim bound to the exact name; the WebAuthn `user.id` kept per credential; handle and display-name isolation from Turnkey provisioning and requests; the Safe remaining the account and payment destination; the migration and the concurrency/runtime behavior proven on a disposable Neon branch; the migration applied to the real Neon database; and a real browser/WebAuthn claim gate passed against it. **Exactly one real handle is currently claimed: `@smit`**, on an existing real testnet account whose display name is `Smit Patel`. A relabel of that passkey was observed with Google Password Manager, the account's Safe and owner values were confirmed unchanged by read-back, and provisioning evidence remains at version 1. **Paying by handle is future work and not part of this slice;** public claiming still requires rate limiting before any public deployment. This slice did not touch Turnkey parent authority; the separate Turnkey parent/dashboard recovery, still paused awaiting Turnkey Support when this slice closed, has since been completed and closed (see ROADMAP.md). Handles is not an active slice.

- **Three identities, kept apart.** `app_user_id` is still the permanent application identity (sessions, foreign keys). The WebAuthn `user.id` (`real_passkeys.user_handle`) is still a random value **per credential** — a backup passkey has its own — and an existing credential's value is never rewritten. A *handle* is a third thing: a public label with **no authentication authority**. `auth.ts`, the session cookie, login, and every Turnkey request are unchanged and never read it.
- **Canonical handle.** Stored without `@`; lowercase ASCII; 3–20 characters of `a-z 0-9 _`; starts with a letter; no leading, trailing, or consecutive underscores (`^[a-z][a-z0-9]*(_[a-z0-9]+)*$`). `lib/real/handle.ts` is the one validator, used by the routes and the UI. Its order is fixed: trim ASCII whitespace, drop at most one leading `@`, **reject any non-ASCII code point before any case conversion**, lowercase, validate. No NFKC and no Unicode case folding, so nothing (Kelvin sign, dotted capital I, fullwidth Latin) can fold into ASCII.
- **ASCII look-alikes are accepted, not folded.** `pau1` and `paul` are different handles. The mitigation is presentational: wherever a handle will identify a payee it is shown beside the display name.
- **Registry: `real_account_handles`, one row per handle, forever.** `handle TEXT COLLATE "C" PRIMARY KEY`; `kind` is `reserved` or `claimed`; `app_user_id` is `UNIQUE` (one handle per account); `claimed_by_credential_id` records the passkey that authorized the claim. Because only the canonical lowercase form can be stored (a `CHECK` under `C` collation, plus `octet_length = char_length`), the primary key is case-insensitive uniqueness with no locale dependence. A `CHECK` ties `kind` to ownership (reserved: no owner; claimed: owner and claimer).
- **Immutable, never recycled — enforced by the database.** A row trigger refuses every `UPDATE` and `DELETE`, and a statement trigger refuses `TRUNCATE`. Both foreign keys (to `real_accounts`, and the composite `(app_user_id, claimed_by_credential_id)` to `real_passkeys` via the new `real_passkeys_app_user_credential_key`) are `NO ACTION`. The application has no update or delete operation for a handle. A typo is permanent; the UI's double entry and warning are the mitigation.
- **Reserved names are rows.** The migration seeds them as `reserved` with `ON CONFLICT (handle) DO NOTHING`, so the database refuses them even if the application list is bypassed. The list in code and the seed are kept identical by a test (minus `me`, which is too short to be stored at all).
- **The migration is fail-closed**, in the same style as Provisioning Evidence Capture: one `DO` block, `search_path` pinned, missing objects created by name and then everything proven from the catalog (table shape against a reference copy, both foreign keys, no foreign index or dependency, the guard function's exact body, exactly the two triggers). It sits before the evidence block, which stays last in `schema.sql`. Its first step proves `real_accounts.display_name` before anything is created: `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` trusts only the name, so the block checks the actual column (built-in `TEXT`, default collation, nullable, no default, not generated, not identity) and its named `CHECK` (validated, exact definition, depending on nothing but `real_accounts`) against a reference deparsed by the same server, and raises on anything else — never altering or dropping an existing object into compliance. Apply it before deploying the code; without it, registration, sign-in, the session check, and backup-passkey setup all still work (see "Profile metadata never blocks authentication"), but claiming a handle and saving a display name answer 500.
- **Migration smoke — passed on a disposable Neon branch (PostgreSQL 18.6): first 76/76, then re-proven against the CURRENT text at 77/77, 61 of them live** (`test/lib/real/handles-migration.smoke.test.ts`; gated by `REAL_SMOKE_HANDLES_MIGRATION`, reads only `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL`, and refuses to connect unless that endpoint differs from the real database's). In scratch schemas copied from the branch's own tables, with only the target swapped in the exact migration text:
  - the clean apply passed (existing rows unchanged; structure read back independently from the catalog);
  - the format, kind/owner, uniqueness, and foreign-key rules behaved as designed — `23514` for format, `23505` naming exactly `real_account_handles_pkey` / `real_account_handles_app_user_id_key` through the Neon HTTP driver's `.constraint`, `23503` on both foreign keys — and the claim's `INSERT ... SELECT` returned zero rows for a non-active passkey;
  - UPDATE, DELETE, and TRUNCATE each raised the guard (`P0001`);
  - every hostile `display_name` shape (21) and wrong Handles object (25) was refused with the catalog left identical;
  - a partial failure (purpose + part 1 landed, the block refused) was recovered by removing the cause and rerunning;
  - a second full run changed nothing.

  The migration was then applied to that **disposable branch's `public`**: only additive objects appeared, every existing table's content hash was unchanged, the registry held the 43 reserved rows and zero claimed handles, and one idempotent rerun changed nothing. The 77/77 re-proof ran against the current `schema.sql` on that already-migrated branch (the harness strips its scratch copies back to the pre-migration shape, so every case still starts from a pre-migration table) and ended with one more no-change rerun on `public`. **Exact migration text proven** (SHA-256 of each executed piece; the `target_schema` constant is the only thing swapped for scratch runs):
  - purpose-widening block: `22243b845cd58cb88a6dfdfc0af9ced99f75c3d2df3a2a79811e4754f05e260d`
  - `display_name` part-1 `ALTER`: `ba10b027e618e5a9815a2aa1f677e0ace420f5618877bcc8d8c88e428789d364`
  - Handles block: `34c1f258736a9e7ec14e4f6f1f20d8247e06c0890885fedb328ae3259c8d8add`
  Two behaviors worth knowing: a *missing* guard trigger is created again on rerun (create-if-missing, like a first apply), and an unrelated extra built-in column is tolerated. This smoke is evidence about the migration only; the runtime smoke below is what exercised claims through the application's code.
- **Runtime and concurrency smoke — passed on the same disposable branch, 21/21, 17 of them live** (`test/lib/real/handles-runtime.smoke.test.ts`; gated by its own `REAL_SMOKE_HANDLES_RUNTIME`, reads only `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL`, refuses to run beside any other smoke or admin gate or against the real endpoint). It drives the **real** Neon adapters (`createNeonDurableStores`) and the real services (`prepareHandleClaim`, `completeHandleClaim`, `updateAccountDisplayName`, the profile reads, `beginBackupEnrollment`), with fixture authenticators answering the WebAuthn challenges (genuine signatures, verified by the real library) and no Turnkey or browser involvement. Results:
  - two accounts racing for one handle: exactly one winner, one registry row (the raw-insert variant's loser got `23505` naming `real_account_handles_pkey`);
  - one account racing for two handles: exactly one winner, the other handle left free (the loser's raw `23505` named `real_account_handles_app_user_id_key`);
  - a same-handle retry after a lost response is idempotent (`alreadyOwned`), one row, nothing rewritten;
  - an account that already owns a handle keeps it; a second claim returns the owned handle and leaves the other free;
  - reserved names are refused at options exactly like taken ones, with no challenge minted;
  - a revoked, pending, or revoking credential cannot complete a claim — including when the status changes after the assertion verified, which only the `INSERT ... SELECT` recheck can stop;
  - cross-account sessions, forged challenge contexts, and a foreign credential in a direct insert (`23503` on the composite foreign key) are refused;
  - a replayed challenge is refused; two simultaneous submissions of one challenge give one claim and one refusal;
  - a handle other than the challenge's own can never be substituted, and what is stored is the challenge's canonical value;
  - display name: set, read back, NFC-normalized, cleared; invalid names refused by the application; a direct 41-character write refused by the database (`23514`);
  - backup labels: with a claimed handle the new passkey is `@handle` / the display name with a fresh random `user.id`; with a failing profile store the setup still starts, labelled `real-backup-<id8>`, after one step-up; the run stops at registration options, before any Turnkey step;
  - an account with no handle authenticates, has an empty profile, and keeps working.

  **Data integrity.** Every row that existed before the run was byte/content-identical afterward; the 43 reserved rows were unchanged; **13 fixture handles were claimed**, no account owns more than one, and every claim references that account's own credential; no registry `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, or `TRUNCATE` occurred. Those fixture handles (and their 25 fixture accounts) exist **only on the disposable branch and are intentionally permanent there** — the registry cannot be cleaned by design, so the branch itself is the cleanup boundary. The runtime smoke never touched the real database.
- **Real-database migration — PASSED on PostgreSQL 18.6.** The three live-proven statements were applied to the real Neon database's `public` schema by a one-off operator script (`evidence/handles-real-migration.mjs`, kept locally and untracked; not part of the application, no route), after a separate read-only preflight. Before connecting, the script required the SHA-256 of each exact statement to equal the text proven on the disposable branch (above) — all three matched — and it refused to run with any branch, smoke, or admin variable set or against the disposable branch's endpoint.
  - **Preconditions** (all met): PostgreSQL 18.x; role `neondb_owner`, not a superuser, owning the three tables; `session_replication_role = origin`; none of the Handles objects present; exactly one purpose CHECK over the five existing purposes; the challenges table empty.
  - **First apply:** the purpose-widening block, the `display_name` `ALTER`, and the Handles block each succeeded, sent separately, in order, once.
  - **Verification:** the structure (columns, collation, CHECKs, both `NO ACTION` foreign keys, the passkey unique key, the guard function body, both triggers at `tgtype` 27 / 34 in mode `O`) matched the live-proven definitions exactly. Every pre-existing relation, column, index, trigger, function, and constraint was identical by OID and definition, except the purpose CHECK, replaced under the same name as designed; only the intended objects were added. All nine pre-existing tables kept their row counts and content hashes, and **no existing application row changed**. **No display name was written.** The registry holds **43 reserved / 0 claimed**.
  - **One authorized idempotence rerun** passed: identical structure and identities (only the purpose CHECK's OID changed again, by design), identical data hashes, and a registry hash unchanged including `created_at`.
  - **Final state:** no claimed handle, no display name, no `handle_claim` challenge, no idle-in-transaction session, no ungranted lock. No Turnkey call and no browser flow occurred. The machine-readable facts for the run are kept locally and untracked in `evidence/` (see "Evidence policy" below).
  - **What this did not show at the time:** that the application, a real user, and a browser/WebAuthn ceremony work against the real database with this schema. The real browser gate below then did.
- **Real browser gate — PASSED, against the real migrated Neon database.** One operator, on one existing real testnet account, ran the application locally against the real database (no branch, smoke, or admin variable set) and verified the registry read-only after every step. Results:
  - **Before the claim:** sign-in with no handle worked unchanged (session survived a reload; Home, the Cash balance, Pay, and passkey management all worked; the "Choose your @name" card was skippable and nothing forced it; addresses stayed under progressive disclosure). A reserved name (`admin`) was refused without a passkey prompt; mismatched entries blocked submit; a cancelled passkey prompt wrote nothing (0 claimed rows; its one `handle_claim` challenge later expired and was purged). The display name was set, edited, cleared, and set again to its final value `Smit Patel` — four saves, none needing a passkey — changing only that account's `display_name`.
  - **The claim:** exactly **one permanent handle, `@smit`**, on the intended existing account, with **one `options` request and one `claim` request** and no retry. A fresh WebAuthn confirmation by the account's active primary passkey succeeded. The registry now holds **exactly one claimed row** — `smit`, that account, claimed by that account's own active primary passkey — and the **43 reserved rows are unchanged**.
  - **After the claim:** a reload and a fresh sign-in both preserved it; Home shows the display name `Smit Patel` with `@smit` under it, and the claim card is gone. **Google Password Manager honored `signalCurrentUserDetails`** and relabelled the passkey `@smit` — the one browser/provider observed so far; others remain unverified.
  - **What was and was not changed, stated precisely:** no Turnkey-facing route was called. The account's **Safe and owner values were confirmed unchanged by read-back**; the Turnkey identifiers (sub-organization, user, wallet) are unchanged by code-path inspection — no code path in this slice writes them — and the read-back's account-row fingerprint matched the baseline apart from the two columns this gate legitimately changes. Provisioning evidence is still version 1 (one dispatch, unchanged). Payments, registration attempts, enrollments, and revocations are unchanged; every passkey row matched its baseline, including each stored `user_handle`; no passkey was added or removed; all challenges ended at 0. **Session epochs did change** — on all three accounts, because "Sign out everywhere" was used several times while switching between accounts (the intended account's epoch went 1 → 3). Those are authentication/session effects, not handle-registry mutations: they were the only differences besides the new registry row and the `display_name` column.
  - **Not exercised:** the backup-passkey label on the real database was **deferred** because this account already has two active passkeys (starting a new enrollment only to inspect a label was out of scope); that path is covered by the disposable-branch runtime smoke. The server-side "already has a handle" refusal was likewise proven on the disposable branch, not on the real database (the UI offers no second claim).
  - **Two UX defects observed and fixed afterward** (component-only change, no backend, schema, or auth change): a stale "That name isn't available" message used to stay on screen while the user edited the name — it now clears the moment either entry is edited, locally, with no request; and the final "Your name will be @handle" line, which shows the canonical value the claim will submit, now sits in its own prominent block directly above the passkey button, with the permanence warning still visible. Both are covered by component tests.
  - The machine-readable read-backs for the gate are kept locally and untracked in `evidence/` (see "Evidence policy" below).
- **`session_replication_role`.** On that branch the application role (`neondb_owner`, not a superuser) was refused with SQLSTATE `42501` when it tried to set `session_replication_role = replica`, so the application's own role cannot switch the immutability triggers off that way. The triggers are ordinary origin-mode triggers: a role privileged enough to set that parameter (or to disable or drop a trigger, or alter the table) can bypass them. Immutability is therefore a guarantee against the application and its role, not against privileged schema/database access, which remains trusted administrative authority.
- **Claiming needs a fresh passkey confirmation bound to the exact name.** A dedicated challenge purpose, `handle_claim` (never `backup_step_up`), with context `{ appUserId, credentialId, handle }` and a 5-minute expiry. `POST /api/real/account/handle/options` canonicalizes, refuses malformed / unavailable names, and issues sign-in options for the **session credential only** with user verification required. `POST /api/real/account/handle/claim` consumes the challenge, requires the context to match the session's account and credential and the body's handle to canonicalize to the context's, re-reads the passkey (active, same account, credential id compared by decoded bytes, `userHandle` equal to the stored per-credential value), runs the existing WebAuthn verification, updates the counter, and then inserts **the context's handle** — never the body's. A stolen cookie alone cannot name an account.
- **The insert is authoritative; the availability check is advisory.** The claim is one `INSERT ... SELECT` from `real_passkeys`, so the database re-checks that the credential is still active and belongs to the account. Two accounts racing for one handle, or one account racing for two, each produce exactly one winner. On a `23505` the adapter looks at the exact constraint: if the account already owns the same handle it is an idempotent success (a retry whose first answer was lost); a different owned handle is "already has a handle"; otherwise "unavailable". Any other error — including a `23505` on any other constraint — propagates. Reserved and taken names get the same answer.
- **Display name.** `real_accounts.display_name` (nullable; the database guards only length, 1–40 code points). Mutable, not unique, never an authentication or lookup input, and editable with a session alone (`PATCH /api/real/account/profile`). `lib/real/display/account-name.ts` trims, NFC-normalizes, treats empty as "none", allows at most 40 code points, and rejects control characters (`Cc`), **every** format character (`Cf` — zero-width space and joiners, word joiner, soft hyphen, bidirectional controls), Unicode line/paragraph separators, and any name that **begins with `@`** (a display name must never read as a handle; an `@` elsewhere is fine). Rejecting all of `Cf` also refuses emoji built with the zero-width joiner and scripts that rely on the zero-width non-joiner — accepted for now in exchange for unambiguous names. No profanity or moderation logic. It is a different column from `real_passkeys.display_name` (a passkey's own label).
- **Response surface, not the session.** `handle` and `displayName` are returned by `GET /api/real/session`, `login/verify`, and `register/verify`. The signed session payload is unchanged, and `readAuthenticatedRealAccount` does not fetch profile data for the other protected routes.
- **Profile metadata never blocks authentication.** `register/verify` looks nothing up: a just-registered account cannot own a handle or display name, so it answers `null` for both and no profile-store failure exists on that path. `login/verify` and `GET /api/real/session` read the profile only **after** authentication has succeeded (and, for login, after the session is issued), through one helper (`readAccountProfileBestEffort`) that turns any failure — the registry table missing, a transient error, the store not being buildable — into an empty profile. A failed profile read therefore never fails a login, never clears a cookie, and never signs anyone out. Backup-passkey setup follows the same rule: `beginBackupEnrollment` takes the store as a thunk and reads the profile through the same helper, after the step-up has verified, so a profile failure is indistinguishable from "no handle" — the new passkey gets the pre-Handles placeholder label, with no extra step-up and no failure. Every authentication and account read is unchanged and still fails closed (a generic 500, no cookie). The best-effort read is used only for presentation (those two responses and the backup passkey's label): where the profile decides something (the claim and profile routes) the store is read strictly. (The later recipient lookup route, whose whole answer is the handle read, also reads it strictly — see "Handle Pay / Recipient Discovery — Slice A".) The visible cost is that, during such a failure, an account that has a handle is shown without one until the next successful read.
- **WebAuthn labels.** Primary registration is unchanged. A backup passkey enrolled after a claim is created with `user.name = "@handle"` and `user.displayName = display name, else "@handle"`; its `user.id` is still a fresh random value. Without a handle the old naming is kept. The RP presentation name is now `ON Chain Finance` (RP ID and expected origins unchanged).
- **Signal API: `signalCurrentUserDetails` only**, never `signalUnknownCredential` or `signalAllAcceptedCredentials`. After a successful login or claim response, the browser is asked — feature-detected, not awaited, every failure swallowed — to relabel the one passkey that was just verified, using that credential's own `userHandle`. Each passkey is relabelled only when it is itself used on a browser/provider that supports the signal. *Candidate, not verified:* which browsers and passkey providers honor it.
- **Handles never enter Turnkey.** No Turnkey request body, stored request, activity, or provisioning evidence contains a handle or display name; an offline test drives a backup enrollment with a claimed handle and checks every artifact. The evidence version stays 1.
- **A handle resolves to the Safe, never the owner.** The intended contract is `handle -> account -> Safe address`. When this slice closed, neither paying by handle nor any lookup API was built. An advisory, exact-match lookup now exists (see "Handle Pay / Recipient Discovery — Slice A" below; it keeps the Safe on the server and returns only the handle, display name, and an is-self flag). **Paying by handle is still not built.**
- **Accepted as they are (audit).** `/handle/options` is reachable only with a session; a reserved name and a taken name get the same public answer; and no rate limiting is added in this slice.
- **Open before any public deployment.** Squatting is unsolved: one handle per account is the only limit, so rate limiting (M2) must precede public claiming. There is no moderation or operator block.
- **Evidence policy.** The one-off operator tooling and its outputs stay **local and untracked** (`/evidence/` is in `.gitignore`): the real-migration script and its preflight and facts files, and the browser-gate inspector with its baseline and final read-back. They hold no secrets, but they include endpoint, account, and credential fingerprints and row hashes that do not belong in the repository. The durable record is this section.
- **Accepted, non-blocking residuals (future polish; they do not reopen the slice).**
  - The permanence copy says people will be able to pay the user by their name, although paying by handle is not implemented yet.
  - Rare cross-tab case: if another tab claims a *different* handle for the account, the first tab's claim form can stay visually stuck until a reload (the server still refuses the second claim).
  - A display-name save error stays on screen while editing, until the next save or cancel.
  - The real-database backup-passkey label flow was intentionally not completed — it would have required an unnecessary Turnkey mutation on an account that already has two active passkeys; that path is covered by the disposable-branch runtime smoke.
  - Only Google Password Manager has been observed honoring the relabel signal.
  - Public handle claiming requires rate limiting before any public deployment.
- **Other residuals, recorded.** ASCII look-alikes are accepted by design. The display-name policy rejects every `Cf` character, so names that depend on the zero-width joiner or non-joiner are unavailable. Privileged database roles can bypass the trigger protections (above). The migration is applied to the real database and one real handle has been claimed through the browser; the broader concurrency, replay, and failure paths are live-proven on disposable Neon only. Paying by handle is out. The Turnkey parent/dashboard recovery, paused when this slice closed, has since been completed and closed (see ROADMAP.md); this slice did not touch it.

### Handle Pay / Recipient Discovery — Slice A

**Status: COMPLETE/CLOSED.** A signed-in Real account can look up one exact `@handle` and learn whether it belongs to a payable account. This is **advisory discovery only: at this slice's closure users could not pay by handle** (Slice C has since built the Pay UI that calls it), and no payment path uses this lookup to route money. There is no schema change and no UI caller in this slice.

- **The route.** `POST /api/real/recipients/lookup`, body `{ handle }`. POST only — no search, prefix match, listing, or lookup by app user id or address. Order: the Real Mode flag (404 when off), then the session (401, and the handle store is not even built without one), then the body (400 for an unparseable or non-object body), then the resolver. Any failure after that is the generic 500. It sits behind the existing request gate (JSON-only, same-origin).
- **Canonicalization.** The handle goes through the one validator, `lib/real/handle.ts`. A malformed handle is a 400 carrying the validator's own reason, returned before any database read, so it reveals syntax only.
- **Resolution path: `handle -> app_user_id -> real_accounts.safe_address`.** One store read, `findPayableAccountByHandle` on the handle store: a single parameterized `SELECT` joining `real_account_handles` to `real_accounts` on `app_user_id`, restricted to `kind = 'claimed'`. It names exactly four columns (`handle`, `display_name`, `app_user_id`, `safe_address`) — no `SELECT *` — and the adapter maps the row field by field. **The Safe is the recipient account; the Turnkey owner address is never selected or used.** Static tests hold that for the Neon read, the in-memory twin, the resolver, and the route.
- **Server-only versus public.** `resolveHandleRecipient` (`lib/real/server/handle-recipient.ts`) returns the canonical handle, display name, `app_user_id`, and Safe address for server use. The route answers only through `toPublicRecipientLookup`, which builds its result from named fields, never a spread: `{ found: false }`, or `{ found: true, handle, displayName, isSelf }`. **`app_user_id` and `safe_address` never reach the browser.**
- **Self detection uses `app_user_id`** — the resolved account's against the session's — never an address and never anything from the request body.
- **One `not_found`.** A reserved handle, a nonexistent one, and a claimed handle whose account is currently unpayable all produce no row from the same single query and the same `200 { found: false }`.
- **Payability in Slice A (PROVISIONAL — since REPLACED by Slice B; see "Handle Pay Slice B" below).** As Slice A shipped, only a claimed handle whose account has at least one `active` passkey resolves (an `EXISTS` over `real_passkeys`; a backup passkey counts, and `pending`, `revoking`, or `revoked` alone do not). See the residuals: this was never the payment-routing rule, and the lookup no longer applies it — it now requires a claimed handle and a valid Safe address only.
- **Read-only store surface.** The handle store gains exactly one method, a read. It still has no operation that updates, deletes, or recycles a handle; the exact-method test and the approved-handle-store-readers test were extended to name the new method and route, not loosened. `auth.ts` and `session.ts` still read no handle metadata.
- **Offline verification.** Focused Slice A tests passed; the final focused regression group passed 104/104; `tsc --noEmit` and lint passed; the full Vitest suite was 2555 passed, 205 skipped, 0 failed; `git diff --check` was clean. An independent read-only audit (Opus 5.5, high effort) reported no Critical, High, or Medium finding and "ready to close Slice A".
- **Read-only Neon smoke — PASSED against the real database** (`neondb`, `public`, PostgreSQL 18.6). The production store method and the resolver ran through a wrapper that refused any statement other than a single `SELECT`, from a throwaway local script that is not part of the repository.
  - `@smit` returned exactly one payable account, with exactly the four expected fields. The returned Safe equalled `real_accounts.safe_address` for that account and differed from `owner_address` (compared inside SQL; the owner address was never returned). The handle row is `claimed`, and the account had active passkeys.
  - The query as executed selected no `owner_address` and no `SELECT *`.
  - A reserved handle (`admin`) and a nonexistent valid handle returned no payable row, and gave the same public `{ found: false }`.
  - No write occurred: every statement was a `SELECT`, and the row counts and a digest over all handle rows were identical before and after.
  - *Not shown live:* that an account with no active passkey is excluded. That would need a write, so it rests on the in-memory and query-shape tests.
- **Residual — rate limiting.** At Slice A's closure there was **no durable shared rate limiter**. The route is an exact-match existence check, open to any signed-in account, so recipient discovery is acceptable for controlled Base Sepolia testing only. **It is not ready for a public or mainnet deployment.** Durable per-account, per-IP, and global limits (M2) are required first. *(Since: Slice E added the durable per-account limit on this route — "Handle Pay Slice E", below. Per-IP and global limits and registration abuse controls are still not built, so it remains not public/mainnet-ready.)*
- **Residual (RESOLVED by Slice B) — the active-passkey rule was provisional, for Slice A only. It has been removed from the lookup and was never used by payment resolution (see "Handle Pay Slice B" below).** As recorded at Slice A's closure: It is acceptable for an advisory lookup on Base Sepolia. Do not treat it as the final payment-routing rule: a Safe can technically receive funds even when its account cannot currently authenticate. **Before Slice B, explicitly decide whether "can receive money" should depend on "currently has an active authentication credential"**; payment prepare must not use handle resolution until that is settled.
- **Residual (low) — claimed-but-unpayable disclosure.** Combining this lookup with the existing handle-availability surfaces may let a signed-in caller infer the unusual state "claimed handle + no active passkey". According to the independent audit that state is not reachable through normal current product flows. Revisit it together with the Slice B payability decision. *(Since settled for the lookup: Slice B removed the active-passkey condition, so the lookup no longer distinguishes that state.)*
- **Not yet implemented (as of this slice's closure).** The `payment_attempts` recipient-identity migration (since written, audited, proven on a disposable branch, applied to the real database, and closed — see the next section); authoritative `@handle` resolution inside payment prepare; recipient identity binding on payment attempts; a handle input in the Pay UI (since built by Slice C); handle-based history and receipts (since built by Slice D); production rate limiting.
- **Next.** The recipient-identity migration is **COMPLETE / CLOSED** (next section) and Slice B — authoritative handle resolution and durable recipient binding during Real Pay prepare — is **COMPLETE / CLOSED** (the section after it). Slice C (the Pay UI) has since been built and is **COMPLETE / CLOSED** ("Handle Pay Slice C — Pay UI", below).

### Payment Attempt Recipient Identity (migration)

**Status: COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Implemented, independently audited, proven on a disposable Neon branch (PostgreSQL 18.6), applied exactly once to the real Neon database, and independently post-apply audited (no Critical, High, or Medium finding; no remediation required). This is a schema-only, additive migration (a fail-closed `DO` block in `schema.sql`, after the Handles block). At this migration's closure nothing read or wrote the new columns; **Slice B has since been built on it and is COMPLETE / CLOSED (next section)**. The statements below that say "Slice B must" or "not implemented now" are this migration's record as closed — Slice B implemented each of them. At the migration's closure users could not yet pay by handle through the product UI (Slice C has since built it and is COMPLETE / CLOSED).

- **Columns** (on `payment_attempts`; all nullable, no default, no backfill): `recipient_app_user_id TEXT`, `recipient_handle TEXT COLLATE "C"` (the registry's own collation), `recipient_display_name TEXT` (a snapshot for receipts; never an identity or lookup input, and bounded where it is written, so no second length rule).
- **CHECK `payment_attempts_recipient_identity_check`.** An *address* payment has all three NULL (every existing row, and every address payment from now on). A *handle* payment has `recipient_handle` and `recipient_app_user_id` both non-NULL; `recipient_display_name` stays nullable. A handle without an account, an account without a handle, or a display name alone is refused.
- **Foreign keys** (direct, validated, not deferrable, MATCH SIMPLE, `ON UPDATE NO ACTION ON DELETE NO ACTION`): `payment_attempts_recipient_app_user_id_fkey` to `real_accounts (app_user_id)` and `payment_attempts_recipient_handle_fkey` to `real_account_handles (handle)`. A recorded recipient cannot vanish or be re-pointed.
- **Deliberately not added:** `recipient_kind` (it is exactly `recipient_handle IS NOT NULL`, so a second column could only disagree), a composite foreign key, a new unique key on the registry, any index, any trigger, any backfill. The closed Handles block is untouched: its executed text still hashes to `34c1f258736a9e7ec14e4f6f1f20d8247e06c0890885fedb328ae3259c8d8add`, and the registry keeps exactly its own two keys, two indexes, and two guard triggers.
- **Fail-closed block.** Same convention as the Handles and provisioning-evidence blocks: one `DO` block = one transaction, `search_path` pinned, the three tables must be ordinary permanent tables, missing objects are created by name, then every column, the CHECK, and both keys are proven from the catalog against a reference deparsed by the server, and nothing else may depend on the three columns. Anything weaker or different RAISEs and rolls the whole block back; nothing is dropped, altered into compliance, or written. **Block SHA-256 (executed text from `DO $$` to `END $$;`): `72342684c507d51c31d6209c8b8b32aebb58152591ec1d632edf5649507a6824`.** The executed SQL is evidence-bearing: do not change it without re-running the migration smoke.
- **Pair integrity is deliberately NOT enforced by the database.** The keys prove the handle exists and the account exists, not that the handle belongs to that account (a composite key would need `UNIQUE (handle, app_user_id)` on the closed, evidence-bearing registry). The registry is immutable and never recycled, so a pair that is right when written stays right. Therefore **Slice B must derive the handle, the recipient `app_user_id`, the Safe destination, and the display-name snapshot from ONE authoritative database operation (one atomic `INSERT ... SELECT`), never from client-supplied identity.** A *reserved* handle is a registry row, so the handle key alone accepts it; only that derivation (restricting to `kind = 'claimed'`) and the audit below exclude it.
- **Permanent audit query** (read-only; must return zero rows once handle payments exist; kept verbatim in `schema.sql`'s block comment and in `test/lib/real/fixtures/payment-recipient-audit.ts`, which a test holds identical). It catches a reserved or non-claimed handle, a handle/app-user mismatch, and a Safe/recipient mismatch:
  ```sql
  SELECT p.id
  FROM payment_attempts p
  LEFT JOIN real_account_handles h ON h.handle = p.recipient_handle
  LEFT JOIN real_accounts a ON a.app_user_id = p.recipient_app_user_id
  WHERE p.recipient_handle IS NOT NULL
    AND (h.kind IS DISTINCT FROM 'claimed'
      OR h.app_user_id IS DISTINCT FROM p.recipient_app_user_id
      OR lower(a.safe_address) IS DISTINCT FROM lower(p.recipient));
  ```
- **DECIDED for Slice B — payability.** A claimed handle with a valid Real account and Safe is payable **regardless of active-passkey state**: receiving Cash is separate from authenticating. When Slice B is implemented it removes the active-passkey condition from authoritative payment resolution and aligns the advisory lookup (Slice A's provisional rule goes away). **Both changes were made by Slice B** (next section): the lookup no longer has the provisional condition, which settles the Slice A "claimed-but-unpayable disclosure" residual for the lookup.
- **DECIDED for Slice B — address normalization.** `payment_attempts.recipient` is normalized lowercase and `real_accounts.safe_address` is stored case-preserving, so Slice B writes `lower(a.safe_address)` into `recipient`. Implemented by Slice B.
- **Fail-closed without the migration.** Slice B's `INSERT` names the new columns, so on an unmigrated database the statement is refused outright and no payment is reserved; a handle payment can never be stored with its identity silently dropped.
- **Tests Slice B changed deliberately** (`payment-recipient-identity-schema.test.ts`): the one asserting no application code names the new columns (now an exact allow-list: the snapshot is named only in `neon-store.ts` and `payment-attempts.ts`), the one pinning `reserve()`'s INSERT column list (now including the three identity columns as explicit NULLs), and the one asserting the lookup still had its active-passkey condition (now asserting it does not).
- **Evidence.**
  - *Independent audit* (Opus, read-only): no Critical, High, or Medium finding; verdict "ready for disposable Neon branch smoke".
  - *Local dry run:* the gated smoke and the whole of `schema.sql` ran on an in-process PostgreSQL 18 (scratchpad only, not part of the repository).
  - *Disposable Neon branch, PostgreSQL 18.6 — passed* (`test/lib/real/payment-recipient-identity-migration.smoke.test.ts`; gated by `REAL_SMOKE_PAYMENT_RECIPIENT_MIGRATION` (+ `_PUBLIC`), reads only `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL`, refuses to connect unless that endpoint differs from the real database's): **64/64**. The scratch proof passed; the branch's `public` first apply and an idempotent rerun passed; every pre-existing payment row kept all three identity fields NULL and nothing was backfilled; the payment row count and legacy-row digest were unchanged; the handle row count and digest were unchanged; the audit query returned 0 rows on `public`; the Handles block reran successfully after the new inbound foreign key; and there was no mutation of the registry, any index, or any user trigger. The smoke also shows the knowingly-unenforced case (a valid handle paired with another valid account is accepted by the database and is exactly what the audit returns).
  - *Offline after the smoke:* full Vitest 2609 passed / 257 skipped / 0 failed, `tsc --noEmit` and lint passed, `git diff --check` clean.
  - *Real Neon production apply — COMPLETE.* Target: the production **direct (non-pooled)** Neon endpoint (same endpoint id as the pooled production URL), PostgreSQL 18.6, database `neondb`. A read-only preflight and a refreshed live baseline matched exactly, then the migration block was submitted **exactly once** (SHA-256 `72342684c507d51c31d6209c8b8b32aebb58152591ec1d632edf5649507a6824`; the closed Handles block remains `34c1f258736a9e7ec14e4f6f1f20d8247e06c0890885fedb328ae3259c8d8add` and was not rerun). It returned success; **the outcome was never ambiguous**; nothing was retried. Nothing else was written.
  - *Post-apply schema (catalog-verified, read-only):* `recipient_app_user_id TEXT NULL`, `recipient_handle TEXT COLLATE "C" NULL`, `recipient_display_name TEXT NULL` (no default, not identity, not generated); `payment_attempts_recipient_identity_check` present, validated, enforced, not deferrable, with exactly the intended expression; both foreign keys present, validated, not deferrable, MATCH SIMPLE, NO ACTION / NO ACTION. No `recipient_kind`, no composite foreign key, no new application index, no user trigger, no backfill.
  - *Data preservation:* `payment_attempts` 13 rows before and after (8 confirmed, 3 cancelled, 2 failed, 0 non-terminal) with the legacy-column digest unchanged at `13:82a2f9d6436d67805f213c2b2de524ce`; `real_account_handles` 44 rows (43 reserved, 1 claimed) with digest unchanged at `44:853e9df3e450c29ebdba784af396565c`; `real_accounts` 3 and `real_passkeys` 8 unchanged. Rows with any recipient identity value: **0**. The permanent pair-integrity audit: **0 rows**.
  - *Handles surface — closed and untouched:* the independent post-apply audit confirmed the registry's columns, original constraints, exactly two indexes, and both immutability guard triggers (unchanged and enabled) and its data are unchanged; the only additions are the normal PostgreSQL **internal** referential-integrity triggers created by the inbound foreign key. The Handles migration is not reopened.
  - *Independent production post-apply audit:* **REAL NEON POST-APPLY AUDIT PASSED — READY TO CLOSE MIGRATION.** No Critical, High, or Medium finding; no remediation required.
  - *Process finding (historical, LOW; not a database defect).* During the production apply the pre-write safety gates were piped through `tail`, which can mask an upstream exit code. It did **not** invalidate the result: the final target, SQL digest, schema, and data state were verified independently afterward, and the gates did print their pass lines. **Permanent runbook rule:** future security-sensitive database runbooks MUST NOT pipe a safety gate through `tail`, `head`, `tee`, `grep`, or similar when that can mask an upstream exit code, unless `set -o pipefail` is explicitly guaranteed. Preferred pattern: run each safety gate as its own command, check its exit code explicitly, and only then authorize and execute the write. Also match migration objects by **exact name** in absence checks, never by a wildcard such as `payment_attempts_recipient_%` — PostgreSQL 18 already has the legacy `payment_attempts_recipient_not_null` constraint.
- **Testing note — the older Handles live-smoke harness.** `handles-migration.smoke.test.ts` assumes zero claimed handles, so it cannot fully run on a database cloned after `@smit` was claimed. That is a **test-harness limitation, not evidence of a defect** in the Handles migration or in this one, and the completed Handles architecture is not reopened. The recipient migration smoke reruns the Handles block after migrating on its own, and a direct exact rerun of the Handles block on the disposable branch also succeeded with the catalog and data preserved.
- **Not done by this migration.** Slice B (authoritative resolution in prepare, recipient binding on attempts, removing the lookup's active-passkey condition) — **since done, next section**. Not done by it: a Pay UI handle input (since built by Slice C, COMPLETE / CLOSED), handle-based history and receipts (since built by Slice D, COMPLETE / CLOSED), production rate limiting (since built by Slice E, COMPLETE / CLOSED).
- **Next milestone after this migration: Handle Pay Slice B — COMPLETE / CLOSED (next section).**

### Handle Pay Slice B — Authoritative Handle Binding (prepare)

**Status: HANDLE PAY SLICE B — COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Implemented; offline verification passed; an independent pre-smoke audit passed; a disposable Neon PostgreSQL 18.6 smoke passed; a final independent post-smoke audit passed. At Slice B's closure Slice C (the Pay UI) was not started; **it has since been built and is COMPLETE / CLOSED (next section).** This slice is server-side only: Real Pay *prepare* can bind a payment to an `@handle`, but **at its closure the Pay UI had no handle input, so users could not yet pay by handle through the product**. At its closure history and receipts (Slice D) and rate limiting (Slice E) were not implemented; both have since been built and closed. No schema change, no Practice Mode change, no change to submit, reconcile, signing, Turnkey, or Safe ownership.

- **Prepare wire contract.** `POST /api/real/payments/prepare` takes exactly ONE recipient selector: an address payment `{ recipient, amountBaseUnits }` or a handle payment `{ recipientHandle, amountBaseUnits }`. Both, or neither, is `400 invalid_recipient` (never "pick one"; returned after authentication, so an unauthenticated request is still 401). The route parses the body once into an internal discriminated union (`parsePrepareRecipientSelector`, `handle-recipient.ts`) and reads only those keys and the amount — no client-supplied app user id, Safe address, display name, or other recipient identity can reach a payment.
- **Prepare requires the canonical handle.** `"smit"` is accepted; `"@smit"`, uppercase, and whitespace-padded forms are `400 invalid_recipient_handle`, refused before anything is reserved. (The advisory lookup still canonicalizes friendly input; the browser is expected to send the canonical handle the lookup returned.) `payments.ts` still imports no handle module — handle syntax is checked at the edge, which keeps the Slice A guard that handle modules never reach Turnkey-adjacent code.
- **Handle authority: ONE authoritative `INSERT ... SELECT`.** `PaymentAttemptStore.reserveHandlePayment` (Neon adapter) extends the existing reserve CTE — the same payer advisory lock and the same quota/active counts — with `JOIN real_account_handles h` (`h.handle = <canonical handle>`, `h.kind = 'claimed'`) and `JOIN real_accounts a ON a.app_user_id = h.app_user_id`, requiring `a.app_user_id <> <payer>` and a valid Ethereum-address shape for `a.safe_address`. The claimed handle, the recipient `app_user_id`, the recipient Safe, and the display-name snapshot all come from those joined rows in that one statement; the caller supplies only payer/payment fields and the handle. Persisted values:
  - `recipient = lower(a.safe_address)`
  - `recipient_app_user_id = a.app_user_id`
  - `recipient_handle = h.handle`
  - `recipient_display_name = a.display_name` (nullable)

  At most one row is possible (one aggregate row, two primary-key joins). It references neither `real_passkeys` nor `owner_address`: **the Safe is the destination and the Turnkey owner address is never a payment destination.** If the statement inserts nothing, one read-only follow-up only NAMES the refusal (it is not authority and writes nothing), in this order: `recipient_not_found`, `self_payment`, `payment_in_progress`, `quota_exceeded`. The in-memory twin resolves synchronously from the in-memory handle directory (registered lazily on the registry internals, because the runtime builds the payment store before the handle store) and fails closed without one.
- **Not found is one answer.** A reserved handle, a nonexistent handle, a missing account, and an invalid Safe are all `recipient_not_found` (HTTP 404), deliberately indistinguishable. No attempt is created.
- **Recipient payability.** A claimed handle with a valid Real account/Safe is payable **regardless of the recipient's active-passkey state** — receiving Cash is separate from authentication. **The Slice A advisory lookup is now aligned with this rule**: its Neon query no longer consults `real_passkeys` and uses the same valid-Safe predicate; the in-memory lookup requires a usable Safe instead of an active passkey. The lookup's public response shape is unchanged, and it remains advisory — prepare always re-resolves from the database.
- **Self-pay.** Handle self-pay is rejected by comparing `app_user_id` (`400 self_payment`), with no attempt created. Direct-address self-pay is unchanged.
- **Address path stays a distinct path.** `reserve()` remains address-only, performs no handle lookup, and now writes the three identity snapshot columns as explicit NULLs. Handle Pay identity is never inferred from an address — a direct address that happens to be a known account's Safe is still an address payment.
- **CallData.** After reservation, `reserved.attempt.recipient` is the sole authoritative recipient for the Safe transfer callData, for address and handle payments alike. It is re-normalized before encoding; if it is somehow unusable, prepare fails closed (the existing prepare-failed transition) rather than deriving another recipient. There is no separate recipient variable after the reserve. **Submit, reconcile, signing, and the no-blind-resend rule are unchanged**, and submit still accepts no recipient identity from the browser.
- **Server-only snapshot.** `PaymentAttempt` and the Neon row mapper carry `recipientAppUserId`, `recipientHandle`, and `recipientDisplayName`. At Slice B's closure none of them was in the prepare response, the public payment-attempt shape, history, or any browser state. **Slice D has since made the handle and the display-name snapshot — and only those two — public to the payer as `recipientIdentity`** (see "Handle Pay Slice D"); `recipientAppUserId` remains server-only, and a test still holds the snapshot's flat field names to the payment-store files.
- **Quota and one-active.** Address and handle payments share one hourly/daily quota and the one-active-payment-per-account rule, backed by the same partial unique index. The pre-existing payment concurrency model was not redesigned.
- **Evidence.**
  - *Offline:* focused Slice B tests (store 34/34, prepare and route 33/33), the inverted Slice A lookup tests, and the three deliberately changed schema-test assertions passed; before the live smoke the full Vitest suite was 2684 passed / 268 skipped / 0 failed; `tsc --noEmit`, lint, and `git diff --check` passed. The exact adapter SQL was also dry-run against an in-process PostgreSQL with the real `schema.sql` (scratchpad only, not part of the repository, and not Neon).
  - *Independent pre-smoke audit:* no Critical, High, or Medium finding; verdict "READY FOR DISPOSABLE NEON SLICE B SMOKE".
  - *Disposable Neon branch, PostgreSQL 18.6 — passed* (`test/lib/real/handle-pay-reserve.smoke.test.ts`; gated by `REAL_SMOKE_HANDLE_PAY_RESERVE`, reads only `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL`, and refuses to connect unless that endpoint differs from the real database's): **16/16** (5 offline harness checks, 11 live), one run, no ambiguous outcome. Live, through the real adapter and the Neon driver: a claimed handle reservation succeeds; `lower(a.safe_address)` is persisted as `recipient` (from a mixed-case stored Safe); the `app_user_id` / handle / display-name snapshot is correct; a NULL display name is accepted; a recipient with zero active passkeys is payable; a reserved handle, a nonexistent handle, an invalid Safe, and the payer's own handle are each refused with no attempt created; five concurrent handle reserves for one payer give exactly one success; a mixed handle/address burst leaves exactly one active attempt; address and handle payments share the same quota; the permanent pair-integrity audit stayed at 0 rows throughout; the smoke's own payment attempts were cleaned up; and the branch's pre-existing cloned data and schema were unchanged (digests identical before and after). The smoke's fixture accounts, passkeys, and handles are disposable-branch artifacts, not production facts.
  - *Final independent post-smoke audit:* no Critical, High, or Medium finding; authoritative recipient binding correct; the Neon live proof sufficient; the address path and payment state machine preserved; lookup and prepare aligned; no code or test remediation required. Verdict: **"SLICE B AUDIT PASSED — READY FOR DOCS CLOSEOUT"**.
  - *Not observed live:* which mechanism refused each concurrent loser (the count check or the partial unique index — the outcome is consistent with the index being the backstop), and non-canonical handle spellings reaching the database (covered offline only; prepare refuses them before the store).
- **Accepted residuals (non-blocking).**
  - *Balance before handle resolution — DECIDED ACCEPTABLE.* An unknown handle with insufficient balance may return `insufficient_balance` before `recipient_not_found`. Reordering would either create an attempt before balance validation or need a second, non-authoritative resolution read.
  - *Rate limiting.* Handle prepare is an existence/probing surface (a refused handle consumes no payment quota). **Before any public or mainnet release, rate limiting MUST cover both the recipient lookup and handle prepare.** This is Slice E work — **since done** ("Handle Pay Slice E", below).
  - *Safe whitespace difference (info).* The in-memory Safe validator trims surrounding whitespace while the SQL predicate is stricter. Production Safes come from the account-creation path, and the SQL fails closed.
  - *Practice test flake (unrelated).* `simulation-store.test.ts`'s "starts from createInitialState()" compares two `Date.now()` reads and fails when they straddle a millisecond under load. It pre-dates Slice B and is in Practice Mode; it is not fixed here.
- **Smoke harness rule — ONE RUN PER FRESH BRANCH (test harness only; no production correctness impact).** The Slice B smoke's fixture accounts and claimed handles remain on the branch (claimed handles can never be deleted), and its invalid-Safe fixture is not run-unique, so a second run on the same branch collides. **Permanent rule:** create a fresh disposable Neon branch for every full Slice B smoke run; never blindly rerun on the same branch; after an ambiguous execution, inspect read-only and use a NEW branch for a complete rerun.
- **Test-quality debt (non-blocking).** The smoke's final "stray" assertion contains weak/vacuous checks. The global pair-integrity audit and the final independent database inspection covered the intended invariant, so this does not block closure. The smoke test was not modified.
- **Handle Pay status.** Slice A (recipient discovery): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Recipient identity migration: COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice B (authoritative Handle Pay prepare binding): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice C (Handle Pay UI): COMPLETE / CLOSED (next section). Slice D (durable handle identity in history / restored payments): COMPLETE / CLOSED (a later section). Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening): COMPLETE / CLOSED ("Handle Pay Slice E", below). **Handle Pay A–E is now CLOSED**; mainnet/public-launch hardening is a separate future program.

### Handle Pay Slice C — Pay UI

**Status: HANDLE PAY SLICE C — COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Implemented; offline verification passed; an independent read-only audit passed; a controlled real-browser sanity run passed; **one real Base Sepolia Handle Pay completed**; a read-only post-payment identity proof passed; and the permanent pair-integrity audit stayed at 0 rows. Real Pay can now pay a person by their `@name` (test Cash on Base Sepolia only — no mainnet, no real dollars). At this slice's closure Slice D and Slice E were not started; **Slice D has since been built and closed ("Handle Pay Slice D", below); Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening) has since been built and closed too ("Handle Pay Slice E", below)**; mainnet readiness is later still. The slice is browser-side only: no change to Slice B's semantics, `app/api/real/**`, `lib/real/server/**`, `lib/real/payments/**`, `lib/real/signing/**`, `schema.sql`, payment history, Practice Mode, Turnkey, Safe ownership, or WebAuthn.

- **Files.** New: `lib/real/recipient-input.ts` (pure classifier and derived recipient view; deliberately outside `lib/real/payments/**`, which imports no handle module) and its test. Modified: `lib/stores/real-payment-store.ts`, `components/real/real-pay-form.tsx`, `components/real/real-pay-view.tsx`, their two test files, and `payment-recipient-identity-schema.test.ts` (see "Static guard changed deliberately").
- **Recipient input.** One "To" field, placeholder `@name`; no address/handle toggle, no fixed `@` adornment, and no address in the placeholder or primary copy. `classifyRecipientInput` returns `empty`, `address { recipient }`, `handle { canonicalHandle }`, or `invalid { reason }`. Anything starting `0x` is judged only as an address (a partial or mistyped one is invalid-address feedback, never a handle candidate); everything else goes through the shared `canonicalizeHandle`, so `@smit`, `smit`, `Smit`, and padded forms are all the handle `smit`. A valid full address keeps the unchanged direct-address path (supported, not the primary UX). The classifier never produces an address for a handle.
- **Lookup (advisory).** State is `idle | looking_up | found { handle, displayName, isSelf } | not_found | error`, combined with the input into one derived recipient view (`resolveRecipientView`). It runs on field blur and on Review when a handle has not been resolved — never per keystroke, never for a malformed name, a valid address, or the signed-in account's own handle. A found or not-found answer is not requested again; an error retries on the next blur or Review; Review joins a lookup the blur already started (one request). The request body is `{ handle: <canonical> }` only. The response is read field by field, never spread: only `handle`, `displayName`, and `isSelf` are kept, an answer naming a different handle than the one asked is an error, and a missing `isSelf` is treated as self (fail closed). No Safe, `app_user_id`, owner address, or passkey/Turnkey data enters the state.
- **Stale answers.** A `lookupSequence` closure counter (bumped by every recipient edit, `init`, `reset`, and a successful cancel) plus the existing store generation, following the store's closure-counter pattern. An answer applies only if both are current and the input still names the handle that was asked about. No `AbortController`.
- **Self.** When the account store already holds the user's own handle, the input is `self` immediately with no request — "That's your own @name." — and Review refuses. This is advisory UX; the server's `self_payment` (by `app_user_id`) stays authoritative. Direct-address self-pay is unchanged and left to the server.
- **Review.** Synchronous for an address or an already-found name; an unresolved name is looked up first, and review opens only after a current successful answer (and only if the amount was not edited meanwhile). The review row is "To": display name with `@handle` beneath, or `@handle` alone when there is no display name — never a display name alone. An address keeps the shortened address. No Safe, `app_user_id`, chain, gas, or wallet terms are shown.
- **Prepare.** Address: `{ recipient, amountBaseUnits }`, unchanged. Handle: `{ recipientHandle: <found.handle>, amountBaseUnits }` and nothing else. A handle payment is sent only while the lookup is `found`, its handle equals the current canonical input, and `isSelf` is false; otherwise the store returns to editing without calling prepare. The lookup is never payment authority: the browser supplies no Safe, `app_user_id`, display name, or owner, and prepare re-resolves the handle on the server (Slice B), after which the attempt's own `recipient` and `callData` come from the server as before.
- **Prepare refusals.** The store-local `ApiError` now keeps the HTTP status. A 404 (`recipient_not_found`) or 400 (e.g. `self_payment`) for a found name returns to editing with the typed text kept, the resolution cleared, and the server's existing plain message; other failures (409, 429, 502) keep the resolved name.
- **Same-session label (superseded by Slice D).** At this slice's closure the store kept `recipientLabel { attemptId, handle, displayName }` — unpersisted, set only after a successful handle prepare — for the approval, sending, and sent screens, and a reload (or an attempt this session did not start) showed the address. **Slice D removed it:** the attempt's server-supplied `recipientIdentity` is the single post-prepare source and survives reload (see "Handle Pay Slice D").
- **Accessibility.** Label-associated input with `autoCapitalize="none"`, `autoCorrect="off"`, `spellCheck={false}`; `aria-describedby` points at a `role="status"` region carrying lookup and validation feedback; `aria-invalid` for malformed, self, not-found, and lookup-failed states; focus is never moved when a lookup resolves; Enter still submits. The status region reserves one line of height. Malformed-name and own-name feedback appears after blur or Review, not while typing.
- **Copy.** Page: "Pay someone by their @name. Each payment needs a fresh passkey approval." (previously "Send test Cash to another Base Sepolia address…"). Messages: "Checking…", "We couldn't find anyone with that name.", "Couldn't check that name. Try again.", "That's your own @name.", "Enter a valid @name.", "Enter a valid account address.".
- **Static guard changed deliberately** (`payment-recipient-identity-schema.test.ts`, two assertions): the prepare wire selector name `recipientHandle` may now appear in `lib/stores/real-payment-store.ts`, and only that browser file. Every stored-snapshot name (`recipient_app_user_id`, `recipient_handle`, `recipient_display_name`, `recipientAppUserId`, `recipientDisplayName`) stays forbidden in the browser and outside the two payment-store files.
- **Evidence.**
  - *Offline:* focused tests — classifier 10/10, payment store 47/47, form 32/32; full Vitest 2743 passed / 268 skipped / 0 failed on the clean run (an earlier run's single failure was the known unrelated Practice `Date.now()` race, which passed alone and on rerun); `tsc --noEmit`, lint, and `git diff --check` passed.
  - *Independent read-only audit:* passed. Its non-blocking recommendations stay open as residuals (copy; layout shift).
  - *Controlled real-browser sanity (human-operated, localhost, Base Sepolia):* the direct-address and malformed-name checks, the direct click on Review from the focused `@smit` field (blur and Review together), the found card, the Review screen, and the Handle Pay were reported as working as expected. Captured: the lookup response (only `found`, `handle`, `displayName`, `isSelf`), the prepare response, the sent screen ("$0.01 to Smit Patel (@smit)"), and the input and status region's attributes in the DOM. Not exported: the lookup and prepare **request** bodies from the network panel — they rest on the passing contract tests and the persisted authoritative row (a stored `recipient_handle` can only come from a `recipientHandle` request). Recent activity showed the address for the same payment, as expected at this slice's closure (Slice D has since changed this).
  - *The one live Handle Pay:* attempt `aaddf0a7-2656-42c6-a675-4a57a53f9414`, 10000 base units ($0.01 test Cash), payer a different account from `@smit`, final state `confirmed`. Persisted: `recipient_handle = smit`, `recipient_app_user_id = 3f58e923-d3d8-4607-8e53-722f32ae6659`, `recipient_display_name = Smit Patel`, `recipient = 0x7dfbc62279ad8186eab494deb1b91bd29738937c`.
  - *Read-only identity proof:* the handle row is `claimed` and is `smit`; `h.app_user_id` equals the attempt's `recipient_app_user_id`; `lower(a.safe_address)` equals the stored recipient; the stored transfer callData targets that same Safe; the payer is a distinct account. The permanent pair-integrity audit returned **0 rows**. Totals afterwards: 20 payment attempts, 1 Handle Pay attempt, 10 confirmed, 0 non-terminal.
  - *Turnkey:* activity `01a12620-1c25-7329-86d4-f8a86a1d8d90`, `ACTIVITY_TYPE_SIGN_RAW_PAYLOAD_V2`, `ACTIVITY_STATUS_COMPLETED`, read back by exact id; the only signing activity in the payer's child organization since the incident began.
  - *On-chain (Base Sepolia, test USDC):* transaction `0xc6b2272fdb2db2d6a69106c35ac4d48ca9452c186129176fb5b4e56d1daf8b12`, block 47936358, status success, exactly one `UserOperationEvent` whose hash equals the stored expected UserOperation hash (`0x799c7acb…92a8`) with the payer Safe as sender, and exactly one transfer of 10000 from the payer Safe to the recipient Safe.
  - *No duplicate:* one Handle Pay attempt, one signing activity, one submitted UserOperation, one confirmed transfer, and no blind retry. The earlier attempts made during the incident (three cancelled, two expired as failed; funding payments from `@smit` to the other account's address) never received a signing activity or a transaction hash and never reached the chain. The payer had been funded beforehand by one normal, confirmed direct-address payment from `@smit`, which also exercised the address path live.
- **Turnkey quota incident (operational context — not a Slice C defect).** During the manual sanity, signing was first blocked by a local browser connectivity problem and then by Turnkey `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` once the Free account had used its initial 25-signature allowance across child organizations. The existing Turnkey account was changed to PAYG and signing resumed with the same parent organization, child organizations, Safe ownership, root quorum, and authority model. No new parent, account migration, Safe-owner rotation, or signing-architecture change was made, and this is not a new architecture milestone.
- **Accepted / open residuals (non-blocking).**
  - *History / reload — RESOLVED by Slice D.* At this slice's closure Recent activity and a reload showed the recipient address, not the `@handle`, and the same-session label was unpersisted. Slice D shows the payment's stored identity in both.
  - *Rate limiting.* Slice E must rate-limit both the recipient lookup and handle prepare before any public or mainnet release (unchanged from Slices A and B). *(Since done by Slice E, per account.)*
  - *Provider error UX — open debt, pre-existing.* Real Pay can show raw Turnkey/provider text to the user (e.g. "Failed to fetch", "Turnkey error 8: Resource exhausted…"), contrary to the goal of hiding infrastructure jargon. Not fixed here.
  - *History refresh — open debt, pre-existing.* Recent activity can stay stale (e.g. "Pending approval" for a payment that has since failed) until the manual Refresh. Not fixed here.
  - *Layout shift (LOW).* A found name with a display name takes a second line in the status region and can nudge the Review button when the card appears. The live direct click from the focused field was reported as working; the shift was not reproduced.
  - *Copy.* An audit suggested "Pay someone by their @name. You'll confirm each payment with your passkey." Non-blocking product-copy debt; the application copy was not changed in the closeout.
  - *Practice test flake (unrelated).* As in Slice B: `simulation-store.test.ts`'s `Date.now()` race; not fixed here.
- **Handle Pay status.** Slice A (recipient discovery): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Recipient identity migration: COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice B (authoritative prepare binding): COMPLETE / CLOSED. **Slice C (Pay UI): COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Slice D (durable handle identity in history / restored payments): COMPLETE / CLOSED (next section). Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening): COMPLETE / CLOSED ("Handle Pay Slice E", below). **Handle Pay A–E is now CLOSED**; mainnet/public-launch hardening is a separate future program.

### Handle Pay Slice D — Durable Handle Identity in History and Restored Payments

**Status: HANDLE PAY SLICE D — COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Implemented; offline verification passed; an independent read-only audit passed; manual browser sanity passed — against the EXISTING confirmed Handle Pay, so **no new payment, no new Turnkey signature, and no database migration** were needed (the snapshot has been stored at prepare since Slice B). **Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening) has since been built and closed (next section)**; mainnet readiness is later still. A payer now sees who a handle payment was to — the `@handle` and display name stored on the payment — on the approval, sending, and sent screens, after a reload, and in Recent activity, instead of an address. The slice is a mapper change plus client presentation: no change to `schema.sql`, `neon-store.ts`, `app/api/real/**`, `lib/real/payments/**`, `lib/real/signing/**`, Slice B's semantics, submit/reconcile/signing, Turnkey, Safe ownership, WebAuthn, or Practice Mode.

- **Public contract.** `recipientIdentity: { handle: string; displayName: string | null } | null`, nested, never flat. Handle payment: `handle` is the stored payment-time `recipient_handle` and `displayName` the stored `recipient_display_name` snapshot. Direct-address payment: always `null`. The existing `recipient` address stays on every public attempt and history entry for compatibility and as the fail-closed display fallback. **Never public:** `recipient_app_user_id` / `recipientAppUserId`, the owner address, Turnkey metadata, passkey metadata.
- **Snapshot semantics.** Historical identity is the payment's own stored snapshot. It is never joined from `real_account_handles` or `real_accounts` and never taken from the recipient's current profile, so an old payment's display name does not silently change when the recipient edits their profile. The immutable `@handle` is the durable anchor, and a history read needs no handle or account join. A payment made before Slice B, or by address, has no snapshot and is `null`.
- **Server mapping.** `toPublicRecipientIdentity` (`lib/real/server/payment-attempts.ts`, with the `PublicRecipientIdentity` type) is the explicit boundary from the server-side snapshot to public presentation data. Its parameter is `Pick<PaymentAttempt, "recipientHandle" | "recipientDisplayName">`, so it cannot read `recipientAppUserId`; it does no spread, no database read, and never infers an identity from `recipient`. The same helper feeds the existing explicit `toPublicAttempt` — so the identity appears on prepare success, submit, status by id, latest, and cancel (intentionally one mapper, not one per route) — and `toPaymentHistoryEntry`. No route file, query, or index changed.
- **Client source of truth.** Slice C's unpersisted `recipientLabel` (state, attempt-id matching, init/reset/cancel clearing) was removed entirely; no two competing post-prepare identity sources remain. **Before prepare** there is no attempt, so Review uses the advisory lookup. **After prepare** `attempt.recipientIdentity` — the server's stored snapshot — is authoritative for approval, sending, sent, reload (`init` → `latest` → attempt, with no lookup, no profile fetch, and no client persistence), and history. If the advisory name differed from the stored snapshot at prepare, the UI switches to the snapshot. A status check that replaces the attempt carries the identity from its own response. The identity is **read/presentation data only**: no mutation sends it back, and forged `recipientIdentity`, `displayName`, handle, or account fields in a request change neither the destination, the stored identity, nor signing/submit/reconcile.
- **Display rules.** One helper, `lib/real/display/payment-recipient.ts` (`describePaymentRecipient`), validates before anything is shown: `Smit Patel (@smit)`; `@smit` when the display name is null; the shortened `0x1234…abcd` for an address payment; a malformed identity falls back to the address and is never repaired or fabricated. Validation is fail-closed: an object with exactly the keys `handle` and `displayName`, a handle equal to its own canonical form (existing `canonicalizeHandle`), and a display name that is null or a non-blank string. A display name never appears without its handle.
- **Approval / resume UX (locked).** A restored Handle Pay approval shows `To: Name (@handle)` or `To: @handle` instead of the raw Safe address; the sending state shows `To Name (@handle)` and the sent state `$… to Name (@handle)`. "Only continue if you started this payment." is unchanged. A direct-address approval still shows the full address. No Safe or wallet terminology was added, and no recipient was added to the failed, unknown, or stranded states.
- **History.** Handle rows read `To Smit Patel (@smit)` or `To @smit`; address rows read `To 0x1234…abcd`. There is no reverse resolution: a direct-address payment to a Safe that belongs to a claimed handle remains an address payment. The identity is shown whatever the outcome (confirmed, failed, cancelled, pending, submitted, unknown) because it is who the payer intended to pay, not whether the transfer succeeded. A long name truncates while the `@handle` and the date do not shrink, and the full label is in the row's `title`. Amount, status, timestamps, transaction details, and the explorer link are unchanged.
- **Authorization unchanged.** By-id status and cancel stay payer-owned (another account gets `not_found`), latest is session-scoped, and history is session-scoped; no read-route authorization was weakened.
- **Static guards.** `payment-recipient-identity-schema.test.ts` holds `recipientIdentity` to exactly six files — the three server modules (`payment-attempts.ts`, `payments.ts`, `payment-history.ts`), the two stores, and the display helper (the components hand the whole attempt/entry to the helper and do not name it) — pins `toPublicRecipientIdentity`'s parameter type and its no-spread/no-read shape, and asserts neither public mapper names `recipientAppUserId` or the flat snapshot fields. Every existing restriction on `recipientAppUserId` / `recipient_app_user_id` and the snapshot names is preserved. The Slice B "no recipient identity public" assertions in `payments-history.test.ts` and `handle-pay-prepare.test.ts` were flipped deliberately to require exactly `{ handle, displayName }` and still no account id.
- **Evidence.**
  - *Offline:* focused tests — server prepare/history 56/56, guards 44/44, payment store 50/50, Pay form 36/36, history component and store 32/32, plus the display-helper tests; full Vitest 2781 passed / 268 skipped / 0 failed on the first run (the unrelated Practice `Date.now()` race did not occur); `tsc --noEmit`, lint, and `git diff --check` passed. Server tests also cover the snapshot behaviour (a recipient rename after prepare does not change latest, status, or history; a new payment takes the new name), the address-never-gets-an-identity invariant, and authorization.
  - *Independent read-only audit:* no CRITICAL, no HIGH, no MEDIUM; one LOW and three INFO findings; verdict **READY FOR MANUAL SLICE D SANITY**. Rulings: the six-file allow-list accepted; successful-submit mapper coverage sufficient (one shared mapper); restored Handle Pay identity accepted as not materially weakening the approval security affordance (the immutable handle is always shown and the warning is unchanged); exact-key client validation accepted; no new live database proof required; no new payment required.
  - *Manual browser sanity (human-operated, localhost, existing payment only — `aaddf0a7-2656-42c6-a675-4a57a53f9414`):* the restored/latest UI showed "$0.01 to Smit Patel (@smit)"; Recent activity showed "To Smit Patel (@smit)"; older direct-address rows still showed shortened addresses; transaction details expanded correctly and the Base Sepolia explorer link remained; no raw Safe address appeared in the Handle Pay history row; at 320px width the identity stayed readable. There was no existing Handle Pay approval/resume to inspect, and no payment was created just to see that state — it rests on the automated tests.
- **LOW finding — display-name confusion (deferred identity/UI hardening).** A display name can contain text that resembles another handle, for example `Alice (@alice)`, so paying `@mallory` could render `Alice (@alice) (@mallory)`.
