The authoritative immutable handle is always shown last and payment authority is unaffected. The closed display-name validator was **not** changed in this slice; track it for pre-mainnet identity hardening (Slice E did not change the validator).
- **Open residuals (non-blocking; none fixed here).**
  - *Mobile transaction hash.* At about 320px width an expanded long transaction hash can require horizontal scrolling. Technical-detail mobile layout polish only; it does not affect the identity, payment authority, transaction correctness, or primary history readability. Tracked as later UI polish.
  - *History refresh.* Recent activity can stay stale until the manual Refresh after a payment-state change (pre-existing).
  - *Provider error UX.* Raw Turnkey/provider error text can reach users (pre-existing).
  - *Copy.* The Pay-page passkey sentence is unchanged ("…Each payment needs a fresh passkey approval.").
  - *No receipt page.* There is no dedicated receipt page or modal; history rows and the sent state are the only surfaces.
  - *Rate limiting.* Slice E must still rate-limit both the recipient lookup and handle prepare before any public or mainnet release. *(Since done by Slice E, next section.)*
  - *Practice test flake (unrelated).* `simulation-store.test.ts`'s `Date.now()` race; not fixed here.
- **Handle Pay status.** Slice A (recipient discovery): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Recipient identity migration: COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice B (authoritative prepare binding): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice C (Pay UI): COMPLETE / CLOSED. **Slice D (durable handle identity in history / restored payments): COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening): COMPLETE / CLOSED ("Handle Pay Slice E", below). **Handle Pay A–E is now CLOSED**; mainnet/public-launch hardening is a separate future program.

### Handle Pay Slice E — Rate Limiting / Privacy Hardening

**Status: HANDLE PAY SLICE E — COMPLETE / CLOSED.** Implemented; offline verification passed; a disposable Neon PostgreSQL 18.6 concurrency smoke passed; the migration was applied once to the real Neon database and independently audited read-only; a final independent repository audit passed; a local Real Mode browser sanity passed. **Handle Pay A–E is now CLOSED.** This is per-account abuse control on the surfaces that can be used to probe who has a handle, and on payment prepare. It is **not** mainnet or public-launch readiness (see "Public-launch limitation" below), and it takes no part in payment authority: it only decides whether a request may go on to do its protected work. No change to Slice B's binding, submit, reconcile, signing, Turnkey, Safe ownership, WebAuthn, payment history, the display-name or handle validators, `next.config.ts`, or Practice.

- **Budgets.** Four buckets, each per authenticated account:

  | Bucket | Limit | Window |
  |---|---|---|
  | `recipient_probe_short` | 20 | 10 minutes |
  | `recipient_probe_day` | 100 | 24 hours |
  | `pay_prepare_short` | 20 | 10 minutes |
  | `pay_prepare_day` | 100 | 24 hours |

- **Subject.** Always the caller's authenticated `app_user_id`. The limiter is keyed by the caller, never by the target: the handle being looked up or paid is never limiter state, and no target Safe, recipient identity, display name, IP address, or session id is stored or passed to it. Forwarded IP headers are not trusted or read.
- **Endpoint coverage.** Slice E protects three surfaces:

  | Request | Buckets charged |
  |---|---|
  | Recipient lookup (`POST /api/real/recipients/lookup`) | both probe buckets |
  | Handle availability / claim options (`POST /api/real/account/handle/options`) | both probe buckets |
  | Handle Pay prepare | all four |
  | Direct-address prepare | both prepare buckets |

  Handle Pay prepare shares the recipient-probe budget because its refusals tell found from not-found; without that, prepare would remain an unbounded second recipient-existence oracle. Direct-address prepare uses only the generic prepare budget. The charge happens after the request's canonical validation and before any protected work — the recipient or availability read, the balance and block RPCs, the reservation, Pimlico, or challenge creation. The limiter is a required parameter of each service, not an optional one.
- **Backend.** Neon/PostgreSQL only: table `real_rate_limits` (`bucket TEXT COLLATE "C"`, `subject TEXT`, `window_start TIMESTAMPTZ`, `hits INTEGER`; primary key `(bucket, subject)`; CHECKs hold `bucket` to the four names and `hits >= 1`). No Redis, no Upstash, and no process-local limiter in production: `runtime.ts` selects the Neon store whenever a database is configured and allows the in-memory store only outside production (unit tests and local development without a database).
- **Algorithm.** A first-request-anchored fixed window per `(bucket, subject)`, on database time (`now()`), never the application clock. Every bucket a request touches is charged in **one atomic SQL statement** (a multi-row `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, rows ordered by bucket name in `COLLATE "C"` byte order so concurrent requests lock identically). A request is allowed only if every charged bucket is within its limit. Stored hits cap at limit + 1. A denied request does not move or extend the window, and it still charges its other buckets. A fixed window can pass up to about twice the limit around a boundary — accepted.
- **Denial contract.** HTTP 429 with `{ error, code: "rate_limited" }` and an integer `Retry-After` of at least 1 second: the longest remaining wait among the exceeded buckets. No bucket name, count, or subject leaves the server. The pre-existing payment-quota 429 is unchanged and keeps its own message.
- **Fail closed.** A limiter store that throws, or that reports anything other than exactly one usable state per charged bucket, fails the request with the route's generic 500. A request is never let through because the limiter could not be consulted, and there is no production in-memory fallback. `real_rate_limits` must therefore exist before this code runs against a database.
- **User-facing copy.** Lookup and handle options: "Too many tries. Try again later." Prepare: "You're going a bit fast. Try again later." No countdown and no automatic retry: in the Pay form a rate-limited name check is not retried on blur, and each Review press asks once.
- **Code.** `lib/real/server/rate-limit.ts` (buckets, policies, decision, the in-memory store); `createNeonRateLimitStore` in `neon-store.ts`; `getRateLimiter()` in `runtime.ts`; `rateLimitedResponse` in `http.ts`; the three routes and `handle-recipient.ts`, `handle-claim.ts`, `payments.ts`. Client: `recipient-input.ts`, the payment store, and the Pay form.
- **Migration evidence.**
  - *Schema block.* `-- BEGIN Handle Pay Rate Limits … -- END Handle Pay Rate Limits` in `schema.sql`, hand-applied, idempotent, and fail-closed (it proves the table's exact shape against the catalog). The applied DO statement's SHA-256 is `78c5557d5b615805f0eb8c63e62d09eccffc792c95f9e2782f9af48d92d1513d` (11680 characters). It is evidence-bearing — don't change it without re-running the migration smoke. The comments above it may change; the statement may not.
  - *Disposable Neon smoke (2026-10-10, PostgreSQL 18.6):* the committed smoke (`rate-limit.smoke.test.ts`, gates `REAL_SMOKE_RATE_LIMIT` and `REAL_SMOKE_RATE_LIMIT_PUBLIC`, run with `--testTimeout=30000 --hookTimeout=30000`) passed 17/17 through the real Neon driver: the JSONB/casting path, the concurrency tests with no deadlock observed, and exact allowance and counter behavior. Additional scratch checks on the same branch proved that a denied request does not move `window_start` and that the longest exceeded `Retry-After` wins.
  - *Real Neon migration (2026-10-10, PostgreSQL 18.6):* the endpoint's identity was positively verified before the write; one exact statement was sent once and succeeded cleanly, with no ambiguous outcome and no rerun; the table was initially empty; unrelated database invariants were unchanged.
  - *Independent read-only real-Neon audit:* passed with no Critical, High, or Medium finding.
- **Final repository audit.** Independent: no Critical, no High, no Medium, no Low. The only full-suite failure seen in that audit was the already-known, unrelated Practice `Date.now()` millisecond race in `simulation-store.test.ts` — not a Slice E defect. Offline verification before it: full Vitest 2861 passed / 280 skipped / 0 failed, `tsc --noEmit`, lint, and `git diff --check` clean.
- **Browser sanity (human-operated, localhost against the real database).** The `@smit` lookup succeeded: two recipient lookup requests, both HTTP 200, no retry loop. Review displayed "Smit Patel" / "@smit" with no raw recipient Safe. A direct `0x` address stayed an address. The identity page loaded normally. No unexpected 429 or 500. The server log showed no payment prepare request, no payment submit request, and no handle-options request, so there was no Turnkey signing, no Pimlico or UserOperation submission, and no new payment. **A forced live 429 was intentionally skipped** because there was no disposable authenticated account; that behavior rests on the unit and integration tests, the final audit, and the disposable Neon smoke.
- **Public-launch limitation (preserved).** Slice E is **per-account** abuse control. It does not solve multi-account (Sybil) enumeration, abusive account creation, or registration farming, and it adds no per-IP or global limit. Registration is still unauthenticated and unlimited, so S5's M2 stays open, and registration/account-creation abuse controls remain a prerequisite before any public or mainnet release. Closing Slice E does not make Handle Pay or the product mainnet-ready.
- **Residuals (non-blocking).**
  - *Optional test hardening.* Eleven additional schema-tamper variants were not added to the committed disposable smoke harness. The exact-shape guard was independently reviewed and the real table was catalog-audited directly; the slice is not reopened for this.
  - *Client.* The Pay form treats any lookup 429 as rate-limited without reading `code`.
  - *Carried forward, unchanged:* the display-name confusion LOW from Slice D; raw Turnkey/provider error text reaching users; Recent activity staying stale until Refresh; the mobile transaction-hash overflow; and the unrelated Practice `Date.now()` race.
- **Handle Pay status.** Slice A (recipient discovery): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Recipient identity migration: COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice B (authoritative prepare binding): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice C (Pay UI): COMPLETE / CLOSED. Slice D (durable handle identity in history / restored payments): COMPLETE / CLOSED. **Slice E (rate limiting / privacy hardening): COMPLETE / CLOSED.** **Handle Pay A–E is now CLOSED.** Mainnet/public-launch hardening is a separate future program, scoped separately.

### Public Readiness Milestone 0 — Live Database Test Isolation

Test infrastructure and operator tooling only; no application, schema, or runtime change. **Pass A is VERIFIED. Pass B is VERIFIED. The admin-runner safeguards are IMPLEMENTED and offline-verified only: their independent audits found no Critical, High, or Medium; the Low findings are remediated (below; the last two pending re-audit) except one accepted procedural limitation. No admin runner has been run live in this form, not even as a dry run. The milestone remains OPEN.**

**What the smoke target check proves.** The URL about to be used is the endpoint the operator named, and is not the configured real one. The expected endpoint id is typed by the operator, who must read it from the Neon dashboard for the branch created for that run. Nothing derived from a connection URL can prove a branch is disposable, and differing from `.env.local` does not prove it either.

**Pass A — the adapter smoke (`test/lib/real/neon-smoke.test.ts`). VERIFIED.**

- **The defect.** Four of its five live blocks were enabled by `DATABASE_URL` alone, so an ordinary `pnpm test` in a shell that exported the application's database URL would have written to that database. The fifth block and the two L2 post-index cases needed their own flag but used the same target.
- **Now.** Every live block needs `REAL_SMOKE_NEON_ADAPTER=1`. Its only target is `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL`; `DATABASE_URL` is never read and never a fallback. The first hook of each block refuses, before any connection, unless `NEON_BRANCH_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID` equals the branch URL's endpoint id exactly, that endpoint is not the real one, `DATABASE_URL` is not exported, and no admin gate or other smoke gate is set (`REAL_SMOKE_PROVISIONING_DISPATCH` and `REAL_SMOKE_L2_INDEXES` are this file's sub-gates). Earlier sections that call this suite "`DATABASE_URL`-gated", or say its post-index cases run on the pooled `DATABASE_URL`, describe how it was run then, not how it runs now.
- **Live evidence (disposable Neon branches, PostgreSQL 18.6, 2026-10-10).** First run: 110 passed, 10 failed, 0 skipped. All ten failures were one pre-existing harness mismatch, not the gate: the S5 L3/L2 finalize fixtures used a `smokeL3A…` credential id that the seed helper's `smoke-<runId>-` ownership guard refused. Cleanup had completed and the branch was unchanged from its baseline. The fixture id was then generated inside the ownership contract (`test/lib/real/fixtures/smoke-run-ids.ts`; the guard was not widened) and audited independently. Rerun on a fresh branch: **120 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped**; every table's row count and content digest, and the schema fingerprint, matched the pre-run baseline, with no fixture rows left.

**Pass B — every other database-writing smoke. VERIFIED.**

- **Migration smokes** (`l2-identity-migration.smoke.test.ts`, `provisioning-dispatch-migration.smoke.test.ts`). They keep their gates (`REAL_SMOKE_L2_MIGRATION`, `REAL_SMOKE_PROVISIONING_MIGRATION`) but no longer use `DATABASE_URL` as a target: the target is `NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL` through the same confirmed check, the refusal is the block's first hook, and cleanup connects to nothing after a refusal. Their migration SQL and assertions are unchanged. The run commands recorded in earlier sections (flag plus `DATABASE_URL`) are historical.
- **Race smoke** (`l2-identity-race.smoke.test.ts`). Its gate is its own, `REAL_SMOKE_L2_RACE`. `REAL_SMOKE_L2_INDEXES` no longer enables it and is only the adapter smoke's sub-gate. It keeps `REAL_SMOKE_L2_DIRECT_DATABASE_URL`, because it needs one real Postgres session per client, and `prepareRaceSuite` also requires the confirmed endpoint id, refuses the real endpoint, and refuses an exported `DATABASE_URL`, all before its session probe. The probe and every client receive the one URL the provenance check validated.
- **The five disposable-branch smokes** (Handles migration and runtime, payment recipient identity migration, Handle Pay reserve, rate limit). The shared helper they already call, `requireDisposableTargetUrl`, enforces the confirmed check, so each also requires `NEON_BRANCH_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID`. Their live blocks are enabled by their gate alone, so a set gate with no branch URL fails the run instead of skipping it green. Their substantive tests, cleanup, and one-run-per-fresh-branch rules are unchanged.
- **Shared check** (`test/lib/real/fixtures/handles-smoke-db.ts`), for every suite including Pass A's:
  - `new URL` does not lower-case a `postgres://` hostname, so a target with a mixed-case hostname is refused, and the real hostname is lower-cased before comparison.
  - A URL that could reach a different endpoint than its hostname says is refused: any query parameter other than `sslmode`, `channel_binding`, or `connect_timeout`; any value outside that parameter's standard set, or a query string containing anything but plain `name=value` pairs (no `;`, percent-encoding, `+`, whitespace, or second `=`); a repeated parameter; a non-default port; more than one `@`; a routing override embedded in the credentials. Which parameters Neon actually honors as overrides was not verified against Neon; the rule is an allow-list so it does not depend on that.
  - The target URL is canonicalized once (surrounding whitespace and one balanced pair of quotes removed; unbalanced or stray quotes refuse), and that exact string is both what is validated and what is handed to the connector.
- **The real endpoint is read under a strict subset of dotenv, not a reimplementation of it.** Refusing an unusual file is acceptable; reading one differently from Next.js is not. For `.env.local` and every other env file Next.js loads (`.env`, `.env.development[.local]`, `.env.production[.local]`, `.env.test[.local]`; never `.env.example`):
  - *Line terminators (N1).* Only LF and CRLF. A bare carriage return, U+2028, or U+2029 anywhere in the file is a refusal — Next.js's loader breaks lines on them, so one can hide the effective definition.
  - *One complete single-line assignment (N2).* Only `DATABASE_URL=value` or `DATABASE_URL: value` on one line counts (with or without `export` and indentation). Any other non-comment line that names `DATABASE_URL` as a whole token is a refusal, never skipped: the loader treats `DATABASE_URL` on one line and `=value` on a later line as one definition. Look-alike names (`MY_DATABASE_URL`, `DATABASE_URL_OLD`) are not affected.
  - *A literal value (N3).* Any `$` in the value is a refusal: the loader expands `$NAME` / `${NAME}` after parsing, in every quoting style, which can move the host. A `#`, a backslash, whitespace inside the value, or unbalanced quotes also refuse.
  - *Exactly one, everywhere.* `.env.local` must hold exactly one definition; any other active file may only repeat the same endpoint.
  - A differential test (`smoke-target.test.ts`) runs a generated corpus through the installed Next.js loader and asserts that no endpoint the loader would use is ever accepted as a target. It found no acceptance; it is evidence over a corpus tied to the installed Next.js version, not a proof.
- **Gate collisions.** Every live suite has its own primary gate and refuses when any admin gate, or any smoke gate outside its own list, is set, so two suite families cannot run in one process. No test reads `DATABASE_URL` from the environment itself.
- **Process-level proof (offline).** `test/lib/real/smoke-gate-process.test.ts` runs each of the nine live suites in a child Vitest process with synthetic values: a set gate with no target exits nonzero with a refusal and no connection attempt, and with no gate every live test stays skipped even when `DATABASE_URL`, a branch URL, and a direct URL are all exported.
- **Live evidence (four fresh disposable Neon branches, PostgreSQL 18.6, 2026-10-11; each run once, behind a pre-connection provenance check and a read-only baseline).** L2 identity migration **20/20**; provisioning-dispatch migration **35/35**; L2 direct-session race **10/10**; adapter smoke on the hardened shared check **120/120**; none failed or skipped. After every run the branch matched its baseline: all table row counts and content digests, and the schema, index, constraint, column, trigger, function, type, view, and policy fingerprints, with no scratch schema and no fixture row left. *Not run live:* the five disposable-branch smokes through their own entry point (the adapter run exercised the same check), and the refusal paths, which rest on the offline and process-level tests.

**Admin runners (`test/admin/`). IMPLEMENTED, offline-verified only; never run live in this form.**

The three runners — authenticator-id backfill, provisioning-activity poll, passkey-revocation resolve — are operator tools, not tests. They may legitimately target the real database, so none of the disposable-branch rules above apply to them: nothing refuses a target for being real, reads `.env.local`, or falls back to application configuration. `test/admin/admin-target.ts` requires instead that the operator names what they mean to touch:

- **Explicit endpoint and database confirmation.** With the runner's primary gate exactly `1`, `DATABASE_URL` must be given explicitly and pass the same strict URL grammar as the smokes (one parser), and `REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID` and `REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_DATABASE_NAME` must equal that URL's endpoint id and database name exactly. Read both from the Neon dashboard. The code checks that the URL matches what was typed; it cannot check that anyone looked at the dashboard. Every connector receives the one validated URL.
- **No silent skip.** A runner is enabled by its primary gate alone. With the gate set, a missing target or confirmation fails the run before any database or Turnkey call.
- **Gate isolation.** Any `REAL_SMOKE_*` or `NEON_BRANCH_*` variable, and any `REAL_ADMIN_*` variable other than the ones that runner documents (another runner's gate or commit flag included), is a refusal. The list is explicit per runner, so a future flag is refused until it is added.
- **Turnkey host pinned.** Requests go only to the repository's approved host (`lib/real/constants.ts`, `https://api.turnkey.com`, also the server config's default). A `TURNKEY_API_BASE_URL` naming anything else is refused before any request.
- **Dry run first, everywhere.** All three default to a dry run. A commit flag must be exactly `1`; any other value is a refusal, not a silent dry run.
- **Commit confirmation (poll, resolve).** The commit flag is never honored alone. `REAL_ADMIN_COMMIT_CONFIRMATION` must equal, exactly, a string naming the runner, the endpoint and database, and the one operation — `poll-provisioning-activity:<endpoint>/<database>:<credential id>:<dispatch seq>:quiescent`, or `resolve-passkey-revocation:<endpoint>/<database>:<app user id>:<revocation attempt id>` — so a flag left over from an earlier run, or meant for another target, commits nothing. The runners never print it.
- **A requested commit that commits nothing is a failed run (poll, resolve).** If the commit flag and confirmation were accepted but the report says `committed=false`, the runner still prints the full redacted report and then exits nonzero — it no longer exits 0. The failure names the report's own outcome and reason codes and says which of three it was: *previously processed* (poll: the dispatch already has its terminal observation; resolve: the attempt already has its resolution) — a no-op by this run, distinguishable from newly committed work, and not a data error; *state uncertain or changed* (a provider read failed or was incomplete, or the row changed underneath the run) — read again before any new attempt; or *declined* (the evidence or input did not allow a commit; nothing changed). Dry runs are unchanged, nothing is retried, and no compensating write is made.
- **Poll commits name one explicit dispatch (audit finding L-1).** `REAL_ADMIN_DISPATCH_SEQ` is required for a commit, and the confirmation binds to that exact sequence and credential. Without a sequence the runner looks at the attempt's newest dispatch ("latest"); that stays available for a dry run only. A newer dispatch can appear between a dry run and a commit, and "latest" would then mean a different one — so a commit with no sequence is refused even when the confirmation says "latest", a malformed sequence is refused in either mode, and a dry run of "latest" is not a preview for a commit. The sequence is validated exactly as given, in plain decimal (`1`): whitespace padding (`" 1 "`) or a leading zero (`01`) is refused, never trimmed into a valid one. Operator sequence: (1) identify the exact dispatch sequence; (2) dry run with that sequence; (3) review its evidence; (4) separately authorize the commit against the same explicit sequence. An explicit sequence fixes which dispatch is recorded; it does not show that provisioning is quiescent (below).
- **Backfill: dry run, then a bounded commit bound to that dry run** (`test/admin/backfill-run.ts`). The dry run reads the database and Turnkey as a real run would, reports `would_map` per row, and writes nothing; it ends with a candidate count and a sha256 preview digest over the endpoint, database, and exact (credential, authenticator) pairs. A commit needs `REAL_ADMIN_BACKFILL_COMMIT=1`, `REAL_ADMIN_BACKFILL_EXPECTED_COUNT`, and `REAL_ADMIN_BACKFILL_PREVIEW_DIGEST`; it recomputes the preview and refuses, writing nothing, if the count or digest differs or the count exceeds 25. It then writes only those candidates, each at most once, reporting each as it happens. Both passes run the production helper unchanged, so the exact-byte match, the no-overwrite `UPDATE`, and the uniqueness guard still decide every row; a candidate is a write that will be attempted, not a promised result.
- **An incomplete backfill is not a successful completion (audit finding L-2).** A commit reports `completed` only when every approved mapping was confirmed written. Anything less is `incomplete` and fails the run with a nonzero exit: an approved candidate that is no longer unmapped, evidence that could not be re-read or that changed, a write the database declined, or a write whose outcome is unknown. It stops at the first of these — once the approved count cannot be reached, no further write is attempted — with no retry and no overwrite. The output separates, with redacted ids: `written` (confirmed), `notWritten` (the database confirmed no write), `uncertain` (unknown; never counted as written), and `remaining` (never attempted).
- **Partial writes may remain after a failed run.** This is not one transaction and nothing is rolled back: rows listed as `written` are modified and stay modified, and an `uncertain` row may be. Do not run the same commit again. Once at least one write is confirmed the candidate set has changed, so the old count and digest are refused — but that is not a general safeguard: an incomplete run with ZERO confirmed writes can leave the candidate set, and therefore the digest, unchanged, and the code would then accept the same count and digest again. The required procedure is the same in every case and does not depend on that refusal: reconcile read-only first (read the database), then run a new dry run, and commit only its new count and digest.
- **More than 25 candidates cannot be committed in one operation.** The limit is per run and there is no automatic batching: a dry run that reports more than 25 candidates cannot be committed as it stands.
- **Poller in-flight restriction (operational, not enforced).** A committed poll observation can be recorded ahead of the application's own in-process terminal observation for the same dispatch. Commit only in a known operator-recovery situation where no in-process provisioning request for that attempt can still be running. The trailing `quiescent` in the confirmation is the operator's statement of that; nothing verifies it, no automatic quiescence check exists, and naming an explicit dispatch sequence does not establish it — quiescence remains operator-assessed. Uncertainty means no commit, and a dry run never authorizes one. The registration state machine was not changed.
- **File-scoped commands; no ambient gates.** Pass every variable inline on one command that names only that runner's file, for example:
  - `REAL_ADMIN_BACKFILL_TURNKEY_AUTHENTICATOR_IDS=1 DATABASE_URL=... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID=ep-... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_DATABASE_NAME=... <turnkey/app env> pnpm exec vitest run test/admin/backfill-turnkey-authenticator-ids.admin.test.ts`
  - `REAL_ADMIN_POLL_PROVISIONING_ACTIVITY=1 REAL_ADMIN_CREDENTIAL_ID=... DATABASE_URL=... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID=ep-... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_DATABASE_NAME=... <turnkey/app env> pnpm exec vitest run test/admin/poll-provisioning-activity.admin.test.ts`
  - `REAL_ADMIN_RESOLVE_PASSKEY_REVOCATION=1 REAL_ADMIN_APP_USER_ID=... REAL_ADMIN_REVOCATION_ATTEMPT_ID=... DATABASE_URL=... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID=ep-... REAL_ADMIN_EXPECTED_DATABASE_NAME=... <turnkey/app env> pnpm exec vitest run test/admin/resolve-passkey-revocation.admin.test.ts`

  Never `export` a gate, a commit flag, or a confirmation, and never run a general `pnpm test` in a shell armed for an admin run. **Accepted procedural limitation (audit finding L-3; not eliminated, no code change):** a gate does not by itself keep a runner out of a whole-suite run. If a primary gate and its full confirmation are exported in the shell, an unrelated `pnpm test` runs that runner too, and nothing in the code can tell the difference or guarantee protection from an intentionally armed whole-suite execution. The exact file-scoped command with inline, non-exported variables is the control.
- **Verification so far.** Offline only: pure tests of the target check, host pin, and commit decision; the poll selection through the production poller with a mocked store and Turnkey port; the backfill over an in-memory database and a mocked Turnkey read; and child-process runs of each runner file with synthetic values, every one ending in a refusal before any database or provider call. That a refused or incomplete backfill commit exits nonzero rests on the runner's success rule being unit-tested and pinned as its last step, not on an executed commit. No runner has been executed against Neon or Turnkey in this form, including as a dry run.

### Public Readiness Milestone 1 — Registration Abuse Protection (CLOSED at `7d73711`)

**Status: CLOSED at commit `7d73711`** (on `main` and `origin/main`). Implementation complete; offline checks previously passed; both controlled disposable-Neon verifications are complete (the dedicated concurrency run `a630d8ff` and the legacy adapter-smoke regression run, both below); independent component audits have passed. **The final independent whole-milestone audit has PASSED** — verdict GO for the operator's Git checkpoint, with 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 2 Low, and 5 Info findings. **The operator's manual Git checkpoint followed: the work is committed and pushed, and the milestone is closed.** Closing it is not a deployment or a launch approval. Nothing was run against the real database and no provider call was made in verifying it. None of this makes the product public- or mainnet-ready: these ceilings cap provider dispatches, they do not stop passkey/attempt creation, and there is still no per-IP limit, invitation gate, or CAPTCHA.

**Slice 1 — dispatch ceilings.** Three code constants (`registration-attempts.ts`), with no runtime override: 5 `CREATE_SUB_ORGANIZATION` dispatches per rolling hour and 20 per rolling 24 hours across all registrations, and 2 per registration attempt, ever (the first, plus one after a proven definitive failure).

- **The ledger is `registration_provisioning_dispatches` itself.** No new table, no migration, no use of `real_rate_limits`. A dispatch consumes capacity when its claim commits, before anything is sent, and is never refunded — whatever the provider answers, including a timeout, an unknown outcome, or a crash. Every row counts regardless of terminal status. A rolled-back claim consumes nothing.
- **Admission is part of the claim transaction** (`beginProvisioningDispatch`, the only INSERT into the ledger). *(Amended by Slice 3C-3a, which does not change this claim: the claim still performs its own authoritative admission after taking its locks, but at the call-path level an advisory, read-only refusal check, `peekProvisioningDispatchRefusal`, may now run BEFORE it — see "Slice 3C-3a". Older statements here or in code comments that no read precedes the claim describe the claim itself, not the call path.)* One READ COMMITTED batch, in this order: lock the attempt's row; take one dedicated transaction-scoped advisory lock (`PROVISIONING_DISPATCH_ADVISORY_LOCK_KEY`, a fixed 64-bit key outside the 32-bit range of the `hashtext()` payment locks); then ONE statement that reads the clock, counts, decides, claims, and inserts. Each lock is its own statement, so the counting statement starts — and takes its snapshot — after both are held. Lock order is always row, then global; nothing else takes the global lock.
- **`created_at` is the post-lock admission time.** The claim reads `clock_timestamp()` once, after both locks, uses that one reading for both window counts, and writes it as the new row's `created_at` (and `updated_at`). Before this slice `created_at` defaulted to `now()`, the transaction's start; a claim that waited on the lock would have dated its row before it was admitted. The column, its type, its default, and its immutability are unchanged, and no existing row was touched. Accepted by the Slice 1 audit.
- **Window boundary.** A dispatch counts toward a window while `created_at > admission − window`: one millisecond short of the window still counts, exactly one window old does not, and a row dated in the future still counts (it can only refuse more).
- **Outcomes.** The claim returns `claimed`, `claim_lost`, `attempt_budget_exhausted`, or `global_capacity_reached`; a database error throws and is never read as a refusal. When an attempt is both out of budget and behind a full ceiling it gets the permanent answer. An attempt whose sequence numbers are not exactly 1..n is an error, never spare capacity. A refusal writes nothing: no row, no sequence number, no state or `external_outcome` change, and no provider call.
- **API.** Both verify routes answer a full ceiling with 503 and `code: "registration_capacity_reached"` ("We can't open new accounts right now. Your passkey is saved. Please try again later using \"I already have an account.\""), and an exhausted attempt with 409 and `code: "registration_setup_exhausted"` ("We couldn't finish setting up this account. Trying again with this passkey won't work."). No count, limit, window, or `Retry-After` is sent; nothing retries or re-registers automatically. Needs-review (Option 3) is unchanged.
- **Offline evidence** covers the limit boundaries, refusal purity, the pipeline and routes, and the exact text and order of the Neon statements. It uses the in-memory store with an injected clock and the real driver with `fetch` intercepted. **None of that is evidence of PostgreSQL concurrency** — the one live run recorded below is.
- **Accepted residual — global registration availability.** The 5-per-hour and 20-per-24-hour ceilings are *global*: every registration draws on the same shared capacity. A malicious actor can therefore exhaust it and temporarily keep legitimate users from completing registration, and nothing in this slice distinguishes them. This is accepted as an availability residual for controlled testnet use. It is not a complete public-registration abuse defense: the ceilings protect the provider and the parent organization, not the people trying to sign up. Invitation-based, per-IP, or equivalent admission controls remain necessary before unrestricted public registration.

**Slice 2A — live-smoke preparation (offline only).**

- **Adapter smoke.** Its Provisioning Evidence Capture block makes 13 committed claims, more than the hourly ceiling allows. It now removes its own fixtures after each case instead of once at the end — the same exact-id, run-prefix-checked deletes as before — so no case makes more than two claims against an empty ledger. Nothing is aged or re-dated there. A read-only preflight refuses the block if the branch already holds a dispatch from the last 25 hours, and the three claim calls whose result was unchecked now assert it.
- **Dedicated concurrency smoke** (`test/lib/real/provisioning-limits.smoke.test.ts`, gate `REAL_SMOKE_PROVISIONING_LIMITS`). It drives the production claim through the real Neon adapter with truly concurrent requests and makes no provider call. It uses the Milestone 0 confirmed-target check unchanged (`NEON_BRANCH_DATABASE_URL` + `NEON_BRANCH_EXPECTED_ENDPOINT_ID`, never `DATABASE_URL`, no other gate), and additionally refuses unless Vitest was started as exactly `vitest run <that file>` with at most the two timeout flags — `vitest.config.mts` provides the main process's command line to tests for this one check. Its cases (since run once — see "Live verification" below): six racing attempts with four dispatches already in the hour; an empty window; one attempt claimed six times at once; the per-attempt budget; the daily ceiling; a claim forced to wait behind the advisory lock that must see the holder's committed dispatch; a rolled-back claim; refusal purity; and the admission timestamp for both windows.
- **Test-only re-dating.** To stand in for dispatches committed earlier, that smoke moves the `created_at` of rows it created through the production claim — by exact row id and exact owner, a bounded number of times — and removes its fixtures by exact id after each case. It never re-dates or deletes a row it did not create. Production never changes `created_at`.
- **Pooled connection.** That smoke — and only that smoke — requires the branch's pooled connection string (`<endpoint id>-pooler…`), the form production uses, and refuses a direct one before connecting. It reads the mode from the shared parser's result for the already-confirmed URL; the shared target check and the other smokes are unchanged. It reports only the label `connection_mode: pooled`.
- **Baseline contract.** Before its first write the smoke checks, read-only, that the connected database is the one the confirmed URL names, that there is no smoke residue and no dispatch from the last 25 hours, and that nothing holds the provisioning lock; it records every public table's row count and content digest and a catalog fingerprint.
- **A clean run restores the branch; a failed run keeps its evidence.**
  - *Clean.* Each case's fixtures are removed by exact id when that case ends, and at the end the database must match the pre-run baseline. Only then — and only if the evidence file was saved — does the run pass, as `completed_clean` with the database restored.
  - *Poisoned.* The run is poisoned, permanently, the moment a case throws, a case or a cleanup outlives its own deadline (each shorter than the test runner's timeout for it), a case is still running when its cleanup is due, the runner reports the test failed, a write fails, a claim's answer never arrives, or a cleanup is not confirmed. From then on no later case starts (it refuses before its first database read), every write is refused at one gate, and no cleanup is started — not by that case's hook, not by a later one, not at the end.
  - *Nothing abandoned is assumed cancelled.* A case body or a cleanup whose await timed out may still be working in the database, so it stays "in flight" until it really settles. No case starts while either is in flight, or before the previous case's cleanup was confirmed.
  - *Cleanup is gated per statement.* Cleanup reaches the database only through a gate the lifecycle can switch off, checked — with the id's ownership — before every statement. Once the run is poisoned or the cleanup's deadline has passed, a cleanup loop that is still running sends nothing further: if a statement it had already issued later answers, that outcome is recorded and the loop stops there. It is never sent again.
  - *Registry.* A fixture id is forgotten only by the one cleanup that confirmed both of its deletes and then found no residue; never wholesale, and never after a cleanup that was interrupted.
  - *Reporting is about what is known.* Every cleanup keeps a per-statement account: confirmed, refused by the database, sent with no known outcome, in flight, or never sent. After an interrupted cleanup the report says the database state is unknown — some run-owned rows may have been deleted and some may remain — with those counts, and lists the run's registered ids as *minted and not confirmed removed, presence not verified*, never as retained. The harness does not read the database again after poison to improve the report. A poisoned run is `poisoned_evidence_preserved`, never restored; the branch must be investigated read-only and not reused or repaired.
  - *Evidence file.* The final report is printed and saved under the git-ignored `evidence/` directory: a name built from the validated run id alone, created exclusively (an existing file is never overwritten), owner-only, and refused outright if its content holds anything shaped like a connection string, an endpoint id or hostname, or a credential. It carries outcomes, counts, digests, run-owned ids, and SQLSTATEs — never a driver's error text. A clean run whose evidence could not be saved fails; that failure triggers no cleanup and no retry.
  - *How this is known.* The lifecycle is exercised offline against an in-memory stand-in, and under the real test runner in child processes: an assertion failure, a failure only the runner sees, a runner timeout with the body still running, the run's own case deadline, a DELETE and a residue read stalled past the cleanup deadline and past the runner's hook timeout, a stalled DELETE that then fails, and an evidence-file collision. That is evidence about the harness, not about Neon.

**Live verification — the dedicated concurrency smoke, run once (run `a630d8ff`).**

- **What ran.** One controlled, separately authorized execution of `test/lib/real/provisioning-limits.smoke.test.ts` against one disposable Neon branch: database `neondb`, the branch's pooled connection (`connection_mode: pooled`), PostgreSQL 18.6, default isolation READ COMMITTED. It drove the production claim through the real Neon adapter and made no provider call.
- **Result.** 19/19 Vitest tests passed (the file's offline harness checks, the ten live cases, and the completeness check). All ten decisive cases passed:
  - A — six racing attempts with four dispatches already in the hour: 1 admitted, 5 refused for capacity.
  - B — empty window, seven racing attempts: 5 admitted, 2 refused for capacity.
  - C — one attempt claimed six times at once: 1 admitted, 5 `claim_lost`.
  - D — per-attempt budget: the third dispatch was refused.
  - E — the daily ceiling was enforced.
  - F — a claim queued behind the advisory lock and then saw the holder's committed dispatch.
  - G — a rolled-back claim consumed no capacity.
  - H — refusals left the attempt and the ledger unchanged.
  - I — the post-lock admission timestamp held for both the hourly and the daily window.
- **End state.** `completed_clean`: `databaseRestored: true`, `baselineCompared: true`, `poisoned: null`, no unconfirmed cleanup. All 178 cleanup DELETE statements were confirmed, and the after-run table counts, content digests, and catalog fingerprint equal the pre-run baseline.
- **Evidence.** `evidence/provisioning-limits-smoke-a630d8ff.json` (local, git-ignored; SHA-256 `57a0e1c68aff3a0a53b686aeb691d5a96c4998a1565178543b1eb3184a1b98b4`). An independent review of the terminal summary and that file found no Critical, High, or Medium.
- **What this evidence is, and is not.**
  - It is one run on one disposable branch whose application tables were empty at the start. It does not prove production readiness.
  - Calls issued together are not, by themselves, proof that they overlapped inside the database in every case. Actual lock contention was observed only in the waiter cases (F and I), where a claim was seen queued behind the held advisory lock.
  - The daily-ceiling history was constructed: dispatches the run itself created through the production claim and then re-dated, not 24 hours of real traffic.
  - No Turnkey sub-organization creation was run, and the registration route's path through to the provider was not live-verified; only the database claim was.
  - The schema/catalog baseline covers the objects the harness checks (public tables' columns, constraints, and indexes), not everything in the database.
  - The endpoint was confirmed by the operator and checked by the target guards; by design it is not in the evidence file.
  - Vitest's process exit code was not captured, because an interactive Zsh comment-parsing error interfered with the command that would have recorded it. The passing terminal summary and the saved evidence are what was reviewed.
- **No rerun.** That branch's ledger has been used; the smoke must not be run on it again. Any further live execution needs separate authorization and a fresh disposable branch.

**Live verification — the legacy adapter smoke, run once after it was adapted to the ceilings (a separate run from `a630d8ff`).**

The adapter smoke (`test/lib/real/neon-smoke.test.ts`) is the older, broader suite; its Provisioning Evidence Capture block had to be adapted so its 13 committed claims coexist with the production ceilings (above). This run checked that the adaptation did not break it. It is a different test, on a different branch, from the dedicated concurrency smoke above, and has no connection to that run's evidence file or hash.

- **Target.** Disposable Neon branch `test/m1-adapter-regression-20261012`, endpoint `ep-patient-cell-b53jcnyv` (confirmed by the operator and checked by the target guards), database `neondb`, the branch's pooled connection. An endpoint id is not a credential; no connection string or secret is recorded here.
- **Result (operator-provided terminal output).** 1 test file passed; **118 passed, 2 skipped, 120 total**; exit code 0; duration 36.05 s. All ten Provisioning Evidence Capture tests passed. The two skipped tests are the pre-existing L2 post-index checks, which have their own separate gate and were not enabled.
- **After the run (operator-provided).** The operator ran a read-only check in the Neon SQL Editor: all 11 application tables contained zero rows.
- **Independent adapter audit.** 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 0 Low, and five INFO findings. Three INFO findings were stale comments in the adapter smoke (the claim count — 13, not 12; the cleanup description — per case in `afterEach`, with `afterAll` as the remaining safeguard; and a late claim's result — `{ outcome: "claim_lost" }`, not `null`); they were corrected by a comment-only edit, with no executable change. The two remaining INFO findings — optional extra hardening and wider secret-content coverage — were deliberately not implemented in that pass and remain open as optional test debt.
- **What this evidence is, and is not.**
  - The terminal output and the row counts were provided by the operator, not captured by the harness.
  - This run produced no local evidence JSON and so has no file hash; there is nothing comparable to run `a630d8ff`'s file.
  - Zero rows in the 11 application tables is not a complete comparison of the catalog, grants, roles, or settings.
  - Branch identity rests on the operator's confirmation and the target guards.
  - No provider-side registration was run, and no test was run against the production database.
  - No public-launch approval is implied.

### Public Readiness Milestone 2 — Invitation-Only / Beta Registration Admission Controls (DEFERRED until closer to public launch)

Milestone 2 is **invitation-only / beta registration admission controls**: deciding who may open an account, which is the admission-control residual that Milestone 1 records. It is deliberately **deferred until closer to public launch**. No work on it has started and nothing in this repository implements it — there is no invitation or beta gate today — and its detailed design is recorded here when it is taken up. The numbering is unchanged. Deferring it does not close any residual listed elsewhere in this document.

### Public Readiness Milestone 3 — API Abuse Protection: Slice 3A-0 (Provider Budget Foundation, CLOSED at `423f00b`), Slice 3A-1 (Authenticated Chain-Read Budgets, CLOSED at `59e93eb`; NOT deployable) and Slice 3A-2 (Authenticated Payment-Submit Budgets, independently accepted offline; CLOSED at `48d673a`; NOT deployable) and Slice 3A-3 (Authenticated Turnkey Reconciliation Budgets, CLOSED at `78be1ad`; NOT deployable)

**Status.** Milestone 3 — API Abuse Protection — is **IN PROGRESS**. Slice 3A-0 built a durable table and a server-only limiter for per-account budgets on requests that make the server call a provider; it is implemented, independently audited, **live-verified once on one disposable Neon branch (run `6d32fdf8`, 2026-10-13; independent live-evidence audit: PASS)**, and **CLOSED at `423f00b`**. Slice 3A-1 is the first consumer (see "Slice 3A-1" below); it was independently accepted offline and is **CLOSED at `59e93eb`**. Slice 3A-2 (see "Slice 3A-2" below) is the second consumer: implemented, both stage audits and the final whole-slice audit passed, independently accepted offline, and **CLOSED at `48d673a`**. Neither is deployed, and the migration has not been applied to the real database. Slice 3A-3 (the `turnkey_reconcile` consumer) is the third consumer, described in its own section below; it passed every stage audit and its final independent whole-slice audit, and is **CLOSED at `78be1ad`** (still NOT deployable). With it, Milestone 3A (per-account provider budgets) is complete; Milestone 3B (request-size limits) is recorded in its own section below, and Milestone 3 as a whole is not production-ready.

**What 3A-0 itself did and did not do.**

- **3A-0 added no consumer.** The foundation shipped with `getProviderBudgetLimiter()` in `runtime.ts` and no caller; Slice 3A-1 (below) is the slice that gave it callers. No request was limited by 3A-0 alone.
- **3A-0 approved no numeric limit.** The limiter module still exports no provider-budget policy; a caller passes each bucket's limit and window. The only policy table that exists is 3A-1's (development values), kept in its own module.
- **The real application database has NOT received the migration.** `real_provider_budgets` exists only on the one disposable branch used for verification. Applying the block to the real database is a separate, separately authorized step.
- **From the moment a route consults a budget, the table must be applied *before* that code is deployed**, because the limiter fails closed without it. That is now true of 3A-1's code.

**Design.**

- **A sibling table, not an extension of `real_rate_limits`.** `real_provider_budgets` is a second table so the Slice E block, and its four-bucket CHECK, stay exactly as executed on the real database. Nothing in 3A-0 reads, alters, or copies from `real_rate_limits`; there is no backfill. The Slice E limits, the payment quota, and the registration provisioning ceilings are unchanged.
- **Three families, six buckets.** `chain_read`, `turnkey_reconcile`, and `pay_submit`, each with a short and a daily bucket (`chain_read_short`, `chain_read_day`, and so on). The table's CHECK lists exactly those six names; the limiter accepts exactly those six and refuses the four Slice E names, as the Slice E limiter refuses these.
- **Subject.** The authenticated account's `app_user_id`, and nothing else — never a session id, a cookie, an IP address, or an account id the client supplied.
- **Same statement, second table.** `createNeonProviderBudgetStore` sends the Slice E statement against the sibling table: one multi-row `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` per request, rows in byte order of bucket name, database time, a first-request-anchored fixed window, the count capped at limit + 1. It is written out a second time rather than sharing a table-name parameter, so neither statement can be pointed at the other's table; a test asserts the two texts differ only in the table name.
- **Rules carried over, and one added.** One decision per request across all its buckets; a denied request is still charged to every bucket it named; nothing is ever refunded; a store that throws or mis-reports makes `consume()` throw (fail closed); production never falls back to an in-memory budget. Added: a family is always charged on both its short and its daily bucket — a lone one is refused.
- **Schema block.** `schema.sql`'s "Provider Budgets" block is the Slice E block's statement with only the table / constraint / reference names, the refusal prefix, and the bucket list changed, so it carries the same fail-closed catalog proofs. Its single DO statement is 11820 characters, SHA-256 `8213b46c2f859fb3c8c5457d34d3dff682508d401fcf82b9169b84333c67a5f5`. (One audit brief carried a typo in its expected digest; the evidence file and an independent computation both give the value recorded here.) The Slice E statement is untouched: 11680 characters, SHA-256 `78c5557d5b615805f0eb8c63e62d09eccffc792c95f9e2782f9af48d92d1513d`.

**Smoke harness** (`test/lib/real/provider-budget.smoke.test.ts`, gate `REAL_SMOKE_PROVIDER_BUDGET`; helpers in `fixtures/provider-budget-smoke.ts`).

- **Target safety.** The Milestone 0 confirmed-target check, unchanged, then: pooled connection only; exactly `vitest run <that file>`; and no runner-timeout flag below 120000 ms. All four refuse before a connector is built.
- **Timeouts.** Every test and hook carries a 120 s runner timeout explicitly; the run's own deadlines are shorter (90 s for the preflight and for each case, 60 s for the cleanup and for the post-run comparison), so the run poisons itself before the runner could abandon a database operation.
- **Lifecycle.** Read-only preflight, start evidence saved, then armed. Every database call goes through a tracked wrapper. Poison is permanent, keeps its first reason, and stops every later case, read, write, and cleanup. An operation stays "in flight" until it really settles, whoever stopped waiting for it. A database error or a lost answer is never counted as a budget denial; an expected SQL refusal passes only with the exact SQLSTATE its case names.
- **Cleanup.** None per case. One cleanup at the end, only after every case completed cleanly: a read-only check that each scratch schema holds exactly what was registered and that no dependency crosses its boundary, then exact-subject deletes, then `RESTRICT` drops of registered objects and schemas (never `CASCADE`, never a pattern), then exact-name residue reads. It stops at the first step that is not confirmed and is never retried.
- **Verdict.** A clean run is **baseline plus expected delta**, never "restored": the new table remains, empty. It also needs both evidence records saved.
- **How this is known.** Offline, against an in-memory stand-in, and under the real test runner in child processes (runner and lifecycle timeouts for a case, the cleanup, the preflight, and the comparison; late settlement; assertion failures; evidence collisions; every refused gate configuration). That is evidence about the harness, not about PostgreSQL.

**Live verification — run once (run `6d32fdf8`, 2026-10-13).**

- **What ran.** One controlled, separately authorized execution of the smoke against one disposable Neon branch: database `neondb`, the branch's pooled connection, PostgreSQL 18.6, default isolation READ COMMITTED. It applied the exact Provider Budgets statement, drove the production limiter over the production Neon store, and made no provider call. No route was involved.
- **Result.** Vitest: 32 passed, 0 skipped, 0 failed, exit code 0. All 20 live cases completed, in order:
  - *Schema (7).* The limiter fails closed with no table; scratch-schema first apply, idempotent rerun, and rerun over existing data, with the exact catalog descriptor; the bucket CHECK, the hits CHECK, the primary key, and NOT NULL enforced; an exact hand-built copy accepted and 13 wrong-shape look-alikes refused and left unchanged; 11 further look-alikes refused and left unchanged; a missing target schema refused and not created; and, on that branch's `public`, first apply plus idempotent rerun matching the descriptor.
  - *Limiter (10).* Exactly-at-limit admission then one denial; 16 concurrent requests admitting exactly the limit; daily exhaustion independent of the short window; both windows exhausted with the longest wait deciding; window expiry by the database clock (a real 4-second test window, nothing re-dated); denials not extending the window; counters saturating at limit + 1; two subjects independent under simultaneous load; overlapping 2-, 4- and 6-bucket sets in mixed caller order with exact counts; and overlapping four-bucket combinations across families.
  - *Errors (3).* A foreign bucket refused by the CHECK with the whole multi-bucket statement rolled back; an oversized window refused with nothing charged; an oversized limit failing closed on the second request with the rows unchanged.
- **Counts.** 40 expected SQL refusals, each with the exact SQLSTATE its case named; 67 budget requests admitted and 59 denied across the cases; 406 tracked database operations; no unknown and no unsettled operation; not poisoned.
- **Cleanup.** 125 of 125 steps confirmed: 15 test subjects and 26 scratch schemas removed (with their 30 registered objects), and both residue reads zero.
- **End state.** `COMPLETED_CLEAN`, comparison verdict `baseline_plus_expected_delta`: all 11 pre-existing public tables unchanged in row count, content digest, and catalog shape (including `real_rate_limits`); the schema set unchanged; `public.real_provider_budgets` present on that branch with the expected structure and 0 rows.
- **Evidence.** `evidence/provider-budget-smoke-6d32fdf8-start.json` (SHA-256 `a602fc5edcacaa1dd33f99d484299692f4218ef1809d22905e625dceac752670`) and `evidence/provider-budget-smoke-6d32fdf8-final.json` (SHA-256 `88322e1b3e01a7e56e38c9d38f118b8b6f3b00264b502a6a0d437635586dc9d2`), both local and git-ignored; both record the migration digest above. The independent live-evidence audit returned **PASS — 3A-0 live verification clean and evidenced**.
- **The file that ran.** The smoke file's SHA-256 at the time of the run was `e8c9acf91656ecdcb1a66b2bb5e110642e9b16953102e3d6d2d2122d86a3ff8b`. Afterwards three files received edits to record the run, none of them runtime. (1) The Provider Budgets block's header in `schema.sql` was edited to record the disposable-branch apply: **comment-only**, and the DO statement is byte-identical to the executed migration (digest above). (2) `provider-budget.smoke.test.ts` received header-comment changes **and one offline harness assertion change** — the ungated test that had required the pre-run "never run live" sentence now checks the recorded run instead. (3) `provider-budget.test.ts` received **offline test assertion changes** — its schema-header assertions (J2 and its obsolete-wording guards) now check the recorded disposable-branch verification instead of the pre-run state. The gated live block, beginning at `describe.skipIf(!enabled)`, is byte-identical to the version that ran. The smoke file is therefore **not** byte-identical to the one that ran: its SHA-256 was `e8c9acf91656ecdcb1a66b2bb5e110642e9b16953102e3d6d2d2122d86a3ff8b` at the run and was `da56f541b62573f902529b578d74c5e2cedd4aea12f3d08f8d8add1569431e9d` after these edits. No runtime application behavior changed.
- **What this evidence is, and is not.**
  - It is one run on one disposable branch. It does not prove production readiness, and it says nothing about the real database, which was not touched.
  - Requests issued together are not, by themselves, proof that they overlapped inside the database in every case; no case held a row lock and observed a request queue behind it.
  - The expiry case used a deliberately short test window; no production-length window was waited out.
  - The descriptor comparison is structural. That the CHECK lists exactly the six buckets rests on the insert cases, not on the comparison.
  - The baseline covers what the harness measures (public tables' rows, contents, and shape; schema names), not functions, sequences, roles, grants, or settings.
  - The endpoint was confirmed by the operator and checked by the target guards; by design it is not in the evidence files. The Vitest totals and the audit verdict were reported by the operator.
- **No rerun.** That branch now holds `public.real_provider_budgets`, which the smoke requires to be absent. **The branch must not be reused.** Any further live execution needs separate authorization and a fresh disposable branch.

**Offline state at closeout preparation.** `tsc`, lint, and the full suite pass; the provider-budget tests, the harness tests, the child-process probes, and the smoke's own offline checks are part of that suite, and every live case is skipped without its gate.

**Open after 3A-0 — each a separate decision or piece of work** (the route-integration item is now partly answered by 3A-1 below).

- **Production numbers.** Limits and windows for all six buckets; provider pricing and quotas, which should inform them, have not been gathered or decided. 3A-1's two chain-read values are development values only.
- **Other families.** At 3A-0's closure `pay_submit` and `turnkey_reconcile` had no consumer and no policy (historical: 3A-2 and 3A-3 have since added them, each with development-only values in its own module).
- **The real-database migration.** Not applied; separately authorized when a route is about to depend on it.
- **Low findings from the 3A-0 audit, accepted for the foundation:** a denied request is charged to every bucket it names; the pair rule does not check that the short window is shorter than the daily one; limits and windows have no upper bound (the live run showed an integer-sized limit and an over-integer window both fail closed).
- **Not part of this milestone's work (later resolved):** a separate read-only audit reported a Medium finding concerning replay/recovery in passkey revocation that depends on provider behavior. Nothing in 3A-0 or 3A-1 investigated or changed it. Its automatic-replay half has since been resolved by the separately authorized, independently audited R0 (see "Security Remediation R0"); the recovery-liveness items it names remain tracked there.
- **Unchanged:** every public-launch prerequisite already recorded — registration admission control, uncertain-provisioning recovery, script CSP, a host-specific RP ID/origin, the platform duration limit. Neither slice makes the product public- or mainnet-ready.

#### Slice 3A-1 — Authenticated Chain-Read Budgets (CLOSED at `59e93eb`; independently accepted offline; NOT deployable)

**Scope.** Exactly two authenticated GET endpoints that make the server call a provider on a signed-in user's behalf: `GET /api/real/account/balance` and `GET /api/real/payments/[id]/status`. Both consume the `chain_read` family (`chain_read_short`, `chain_read_day`) of the 3A-0 foundation; no new bucket, no SQL change.

**Policy (controlled-development values, not production limits).** `chain_read_short`: 30 per 600 s. `chain_read_day`: 200 per 86 400 s. They live in one module, `lib/real/server/chain-read-budget.ts` (frozen), and both resolvers call `consumeChainReadBudget(limiter, appUserId)` with no policy parameter — so the two endpoints cannot hold different limits for one bucket. That matters beyond tidiness: the accounting statement stores the counter against the limit it is handed. A source guard and an object-identity test pin it; the static-boundary tests pin which files may name `chain_read`, `getProviderBudgetLimiter`, or the limiter types.

**What one unit is.** One provider-bound endpoint request, charged to the authenticated `app_user_id` (taken from the session-resolved account, never the request) on both buckets in one atomic limiter operation, immediately before the first provider call and after authentication, ownership, and Safe validation. Database-only responses consume nothing.

**Balance.** Charge before `readCashBalance`, outside its `try`. Denial → HTTP 429, `Retry-After` (validated whole seconds), `{ error, code: "rate_limited" }` through the shared `rateLimitedResponse` helper; no RPC call, no balance. 401 / 409 / 502 / 500 are unchanged.

**Payment status.** Only branches that actually reach a provider are charged: a reconcilable state (`submitting` / `submitted` / `unknown`) with a stored UserOperation hash (charged before the bundler read, and the same charge covers the EntryPoint fallback), and a pre-dispatch attempt past `validUntil` with complete stored proof evidence and a usable Safe (charged before the EntryPoint read; `now == validUntil` is still free). `entryPointProofTarget()` is the single test for "would this read happen", shared by the charge decision and the reconciliation itself. Everything else — terminal rows, `prepared`, unexpired pre-dispatch, legacy or incomplete rows — is free and stays readable for an exhausted account. Denial → HTTP 200 `{ attempt, serverNowSeconds, reconciliationDeferred: true, retryAfterSeconds }` plus `Retry-After`; the attempt object is the stored attempt, byte-identical to an ordinary answer (no metadata inside it), and nothing is transitioned, read, resent, or implied. Ordinary answers carry `reconciliationDeferred: false`.

**Failure semantics.** A limiter that throws, answers a non-decision, or reports retry metadata that is not a whole number of seconds in 1…86 400 raises; the routes answer the generic 500 and no provider is contacted. There is no in-memory production fallback and no catch-as-allow.

**Cross-site protection (Stage 1).** `lib/real/server/request-gate.ts` refuses the two GETs (HEAD included, raw and decoded paths) unless `Sec-Fetch-Site` is exactly `same-origin` or, absent that, the Referer's origin exactly matches a configured Real origin; a present `Origin` must match too. Trusted origins come only from `NEXT_PUBLIC_REAL_ORIGIN`. This runs before any charge, so a hostile page cannot spend a victim's budget.

**Client (Stages 2A–2C.3, closeout).**

- *Polling is bounded.* An expired pre-dispatch attempt gets one initial check and at most eight follow-ups at 75 s doubling to 10 min; none while the tab is hidden; timers are cleaned up on unmount; a refused cancel gets one reconciliation read per attempt per 30 s window (a repeat press does not discard it).
- *Reads are fresh or discarded.* A `statusEpoch` marks every local transition (submit, prepare, refused cancel); a read begun under an older epoch is never applied and never joined by a caller that needs current state. Waiting behind an older read is bounded by the request deadline and re-checks lifecycle, account, attempt, and the server deadline; a single in-flight slot means waiters share one current-epoch read.
- *Every read has a deadline.* 45 s, with an `AbortController` where fetch honors it and a bounded logical settlement where it does not; a timeout ends only the client's wait and is never evidence about the server.
- *Server delays are honored.* A deferral's `retryAfterSeconds` (validated) becomes an absolute deadline bound to the `appUserId` and attempt; nothing provider-bound is sent before it, automatic follow-ups use the later of it and their schedule, and it survives navigation for that account and attempt. It can never shorten the 30 s manual cooldown. Malformed deferral metadata is not trusted — not even its attempt — and applies a 60 s local back-off.
- *Honest copy.* "Check available later" (a server pause) is distinct from "Checked just now" (the ordinary cooldown); the balance line says "unavailable" rather than "checking" or zero; no provider vocabulary reaches the user. The balance store keeps no stale balance (account isolation) and keeps its cooldown only for the same `appUserId`.
- *Never authority over payments.* The client schedules and displays reads; it never marks a payment finished, failed, or cancelled on a timeout or a deferral, never resends, and never releases a payment slot.

**Verification.** Each stage was independently audited (Stage 1, 2A, 2A.1, 2B, 2C, 2C.3: no Critical, High, or Medium findings; accepted Lows recorded below), and the final independent whole-slice audit PASSED with 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 0 Low and 4 Info, accepting the offline implementation. That audit re-ran 725 focused tests, `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint, and `git diff --check`, all passing. The Builder's last full Vitest run was 4169 passed / 322 skipped / **1 failed**: the failure was the previously observed, unrelated `simulation-store.test.ts` "starts from createInitialState()" (a Practice `Date.now()` race), which passed 28/28 when run alone; its root cause is not conclusively established, and it is recorded as a separate intermittent failure, as is the revocation test noted under the open items. The new behaviors are pinned by deterministic fake-timer / deferred-promise tests and were mutation-checked. No live Neon or provider test was run for 3A-1, and none is authorized: the server contracts are proven against fakes, not against the real table. Acceptance is of the offline code only — not a deployment authorization, not a migration, not approval of production limits, and not a statement that the milestone is production-ready.

**Accepted limitations (controlled testnet development only).** Rapid visibility changes can consume bounded follow-up checks. Browser timer suspension can delay cooldown UI recovery. The balance wait copy can overstate the remaining time. **Balance and payment-status reads share one chain-read budget, so heavy balance polling can delay payment reconciliation; this is accepted only for controlled testnet development and is NOT approved as a public-launch recovery guarantee** (a background reconciler or operator recovery tooling would be separate work, and neither exists). A rare 86 401-second limiter result fails closed. A hard reload loses client-only retry deadlines, so the first read afterwards may be deferred again. An aborted browser request may continue server-side. Cross-tab stale-account display limitations remain. Per-account control only: no per-IP, global, or registration-time control.

**Deployment prerequisites (none done).** (1) Explicit migration authorization; (2) controlled migration of `real_provider_budgets` to the real database; (3) independent verification of that migration; (4) approval of production provider policies and windows; (5) separate deployment authorization. Until then this code must not be deployed — against the unmigrated real database both routes would answer a generic 500. Milestone 2 remains deferred.

#### Slice 3A-2 — Authenticated Payment-Submit Budgets (independently accepted offline; CLOSED at `48d673a`; NOT deployable)

**Audit status.** Stage 1 (server) independent audit: **PASSED** — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low, 4 Info. Stage 2 (client) independent audit: **PASSED** — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low, 6 Info. **Final whole-slice independent audit: PASSED** — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 0 Low, 5 Info (nonblocking; no production correction was required and none of the Info observations was acted on). 3A-2 is independently accepted for OFFLINE engineering. That is an acceptance of the offline code only — not a deployment authorization, not a migration, not approval of production limits, and not a statement that the slice or Milestone 3 is production-ready. The slice's Git checkpoint is complete: it is **CLOSED at `48d673a`** (verified against `git log`).

**Scope.** `POST /api/real/payments/submit` — the one authenticated request that makes the server read Turnkey (the attribution proof: `getActivity`, `getUsers`) and may then dispatch through the bundler. Without a budget a signed-in account could drive unlimited Turnkey reads by repeating submits. It consumes the `pay_submit` family (`pay_submit_short`, `pay_submit_day`) of the 3A-0 foundation; no new bucket, no SQL change.

**Policy (controlled-development values, not production limits).** `pay_submit_short`: 12 per 600 s. `pay_submit_day`: 90 per 86 400 s. Defined once, frozen, in `lib/real/server/pay-submit-budget.ts`; `resolveSubmitPayment` calls `consumePaySubmitBudget(limiter, appUserId)` and takes no policy parameter (the accounting statement stores the counter against the limit it is handed, so one bucket must have one limit). Static guards allow `pay_submit` to be named only by the limiter module and the policy module.

**What one unit is.** One provider-bound submit request, charged to the session-resolved `appUserId` (never the request body) on both buckets in one atomic operation. The charge sits after authentication, request-shape validation, ownership, state, bound-passkey and the offline digest/signature-shape checks, and immediately before the first Turnkey read. It covers every Turnkey read and the permitted dispatch; the compare-and-set transitions, `beginDispatch`, and the Pimlico send are not charged separately. Every database-only refusal (including the local `failBeforeClaim` branches) consumes nothing.

**Denial.** HTTP 429, `Retry-After` (validated whole seconds), `{ error, code: "rate_limited", retryable: true }`. `retryable: true` is security-relevant: the payment client treats a non-retryable submit error as a possibly-uncertain dispatch, but a denial is decided before any provider work, so it must say "nothing changed". The helper is separate from `rateLimitedResponse`, whose body is unchanged for the probe and prepare routes. A denied submit makes no Turnkey and no Pimlico call, writes no transition, records no activity id, does not release the payment slot, and implies no uncertainty or finality; the same signature and activity id may be sent again after the wait. An expired-but-unclaimed row is not failed by a denied submit (expiry is judged only after the budget is spent; status reconciliation handles it).

**Failure semantics.** A limiter that throws, answers a non-decision, or reports a delay that is not a whole number of seconds in 1…86 400 raises; the route answers the generic 500 with zero provider calls and an untouched row. No in-memory production fallback, no catch-as-allow.

**Client (Stage 2).**

- *A validated wait.* `Retry-After` is read only from a response that is a 429 with `code === "rate_limited"` and `retryable === true`; whole seconds 1…86 400 as plain digits. A missing or malformed value applies a 60 s local fallback — never an immediate retry. Later denials can only extend the deadline.
- *Bound and memory-only.* The deadline is `{ appUserId, attemptId, untilMs }` in the store closure. It survives a same-account, same-attempt remount (including Strict Mode's double mount) and is dropped by reset, a successful cancel, a new prepare, or a different account or attempt. Nothing is stored in browser storage.
- *Gated on the action.* Continue is refused before `signPreparedPayment` and again before the request, not merely disabled; overlapping presses during a passkey ceremony open no second ceremony. When the wait ends only the control is re-enabled — nothing is signed or sent automatically.
- *Honest wording.* The server sentence ("…tap Continue") is never shown. The card says "Too many attempts right now. You can try again shortly." or an approximate "about N minutes/hours", and, when the wait outlasts the approval's dispatch margin, "This payment approval will expire before you can try again. You can cancel this payment and start a new one later." with no Continue. No provider vocabulary reaches the user.
- *Late answers are isolated.* `authorizeAndSubmit` and `confirmAndSend` capture generation, attempt and account and re-check them after every await: a prepare, signing, or submit answer from a replaced lifecycle is dropped, starts no request, and keeps no signature; within one lifecycle a late answer cannot regress a terminal payment or resurrect a cancelled one. Dropping an answer is never treated as evidence about the payment.
- *Unchanged.* The server's state machine and at-most-once dispatch; the existing 503 (retryable) and generic-500 (conservative, `unknown`) client handling; the 3A-1 status polling budget.

**Verification (offline only).** Stage 2 audit's focused run: 727 passed / 0 failed; `tsc --noEmit` and `git diff --check` passed; ESLint passed with one test-only warning (fixed in the closeout). The closeout correction was re-verified offline with the focused suites, `tsc`, ESLint and `git diff --check`. The final whole-slice audit independently re-ran verification: focused 550 passed / 0 failed; broader regression 3665 passed / 319 skipped / 0 failed; `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` PASSED; and both provider-budget SQL digests were independently confirmed unchanged. The Builder's earlier complete offline Vitest run: 4244 passed / 322 skipped / 0 failed. The new behaviors are pinned by fake-limiter, fake-Turnkey/Pimlico, fake-timer and deferred-promise tests and were mutation-checked. **No live-browser, Neon, or provider test has been run for this slice, and none is authorized**; these results are not live-provider validation.

**Accepted limitations (controlled testnet development only).** The wait is memory-only: a hard reload or another tab does not keep it, so the first retry afterwards may be refused again. A remount discards `pendingSubmission`, so a later manual retry may need a new passkey approval — a new Turnkey signing activity that this budget does not cap. The wait message is approximate and never counts down, and an idle page can overstate the remaining time. The "approval will expire" judgment uses the advisory clock offset at the moment the denial arrives (or is restored) and stays set after the wait ends until the context changes. Client checks supplement the server, which stays authoritative. Per-account control only: no per-IP, global, or registration-time limit. **Closeout Low — fixed and regression-tested:** if the attempt was cancelled elsewhere and a status read showed it, then a new payment was prepared without a reset, the published wait flags (`isSubmitCoolingDown`, `submitCooldownSeconds`, `isSubmitRetryBeyondApproval`) were not cleared by prepare (only the closure deadline was) and could leave Continue disabled on the new payment until the page reinitialized. `confirmAndSend`'s successful-prepare transition now clears all three with the deadline, after the lifecycle/account guard (so an obsolete prepare answer changes nothing); the same attempt keeps its wait across a remount. A store test and a form test pin it and fail without the fix. Unrelated flaky tests (`simulation-store.test.ts`, `backup-step-up-and-pending-removal.test.ts`) remain separately tracked.

**Deployment prerequisites (none done).** The same five as 3A-1: explicit migration authorization; controlled migration of `real_provider_budgets` to the real database (which has not received it — against it the submit route would answer a generic 500); independent verification; approval of production provider policies and windows (Turnkey read pricing and quotas are unresearched); a separate deployment authorization. ON Chain Finance remains localhost-only with no Vercel project; GitHub source synchronization is not deployment. The automatic passkey-removal DELETE replay (the Medium finding) is resolved for offline engineering by independently audited R0; its recovery-liveness residuals stay tracked separately; Milestone 2 stays deferred.

## Security Remediation R0 — No Automatic Passkey-Removal Replay

A prerequisite to Milestone 3A-3 (Turnkey reconciliation budgets). It resolves the Medium finding about repeated forwarding of a stored signed Turnkey DELETE during passkey-removal reconciliation.

**Status: CLOSED — implemented; independently audited — PASSED (0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 0 Low, 5 Info; no production correction required); accepted for OFFLINE engineering only. Committed and pushed at `c1919c4` (on `main` and `origin/main`), so R0 is synchronized with GitHub and no longer awaits a checkpoint.** (The general rule stands for later work: Git operations are the operator's own manual step, so don't assume a commit or push without evidence from `git log`.) Not a deployment, migration or launch approval.
