- **The defect.** Before R0, `reconcileRevocation` forwarded the stored signed DELETE whenever an attempt was `dispatch_in_flight` with no activity id, a fresh request timestamp and a stored body and stamp. Concurrent or repeated reconciles could forward the same DELETE several times, creating duplicate provider activities and muddying recovery.
- **The invariant.** At most **one** signed DELETE forward per successfully claimed removal attempt. It is made in `submitRevocation`, after the `beginDispatch` claim (which admits one claimant per attempt). Neither the internal reconcile that follows a submit nor any external reconcile request forwards a DELETE. There is no alternative resend path, and a transport error is never treated as proof the DELETE was not sent.
- **Behavior with no activity id.** Inside the existing grace window (the unchanged 2-minute freshness window): `pending`, with no provider call and no resend. After it: the existing CAS clears the stored stamp and transitions to `blocked` / `no_activity_receipt`. An unknown original outcome stays uncertain; it is never described as "not forwarded".
- **Behavior with a known activity id.** Unchanged: read-only `getActivity` and `getUsers`; `confirmed` still requires BOTH a COMPLETED delete activity naming exactly the target AND a later read showing it absent. Authenticator absence alone is never success, and a dispatched target never returns to `active`.
- **S3 unchanged.** Operator-only evidence-based recovery of a `blocked` removal (see S3 above) is untouched; the existing resolver suite resolves genuine `no_activity_receipt` rows produced under R0. It is never invoked automatically and no recovery endpoint was added.
- **Not the backup flow.** Backup-enrollment create keeps its own separately controlled, claimed replay (`claimReplay` CAS, at most one byte-identical replay while fresh). R0 did not touch it, and "signed requests are never replayed" would be wrong.
- **Stamp persistence unchanged.** The optional "R0-s" variant (stop persisting the signed stamp) was not authorized or implemented. The body and stamp are stored as before and cleared by the existing rules — when an activity id is recorded or the window closes. No schema, store, serialization, or signed-request validation changed; the only executable change is in `lib/real/server/passkey-revocation.ts`.
- **Tests.** `test/lib/real/passkey-revocation.test.ts` (52 tests): three replay-dependent tests were rewritten to enforce R0, and defect-proof tests were added (repeated and concurrent reconciles, a reconcile racing submit, a lost response with dedupe off, a legacy in-flight row with a stored stamp, concurrent submits, and a source guard allowing exactly one `forwardSignedRequest` call site in the revocation module, after `beginDispatch`). The Builder confirmed the new and rewritten tests fail against the original code.
- **Verification record (offline only).** Auditor's focused run: 634 passed / 0 failed; passkey-revocation 52 passed; `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` PASSED. The Builder's earlier full offline run: 4256 passed / 322 skipped / 1 failed — the unrelated Practice `simulation-store.test.ts` race; that run is not a clean pass. The intermittent `backup-step-up-and-pending-removal.test.ts` "FIX 3" failure (a same-millisecond `latestForTarget` ordering tie) occurred once in the Builder's first focused run and is a separate open reliability item. **No live-provider test was performed for R0.**
- **Residuals — preserved, not new vulnerabilities:**
  - The original DELETE's response can be lost after Turnkey applied it; R0 never resends, so such an attempt can end `blocked` / `no_activity_receipt`.
  - A `revoking` credential stays unusable for app sign-in, and Turnkey authority can remain uncertain, while reconciliation or operator review is pending.
  - S3 recovery is operator-only, not user self-service.
  - The raw forward in `forwardSignedRequest` has no explicit timeout.
  - Provider activity retention, duplicate-request behavior and the fingerprint assumptions are not verified enough for production guarantees.
  - Operator capacity, alerting, recovery SLAs and further public-launch recovery work remain necessary. Public-launch recovery readiness is not claimed.
  - The "FIX 3" ordering issue and the Practice `simulation-store` timing flake remain open and separate.
- **Deployment boundary.** ON Chain Finance remains localhost-only with no Vercel project; no deployment, migration or production budget limit is authorized; `real_provider_budgets` has not been applied to the real database; Turnkey read pricing and quotas remain unresearched; GitHub synchronization is not deployment.
- **3A-3.** At R0's closure the Turnkey reconciliation budgets (`turnkey_reconcile`) were NOT implemented; R0 removed the replay-design blocker without itself authorizing 3A-3. (Historical: 3A-3 was then separately authorized and built — see "Slice 3A-3" below.)

### Public Readiness Milestone 3 — Slice 3A-3: Authenticated Turnkey Reconciliation Budgets (CLOSED at `78be1ad`; every stage audit and the FINAL WHOLE-SLICE AUDIT PASSED; NOT deployable)

**Status.** Server (Stage 1) and client (Stages 2A and 2B, plus the Stage 2 follow-up corrections) are implemented. Every stage has passed its own independent audit, and the **final integrated whole-slice independent security audit PASSED** (record below; verdict "ready for manual 3A-3 Git checkpoint"). The operator's manual Git checkpoint is **complete**: 3A-3 is **CLOSED at `78be1ad`** (R0's `c1919c4` is a separate, earlier checkpoint). Milestone 3A is therefore complete; Milestone 3 as a whole is not production-ready (request-size limits are recorded in "Public Readiness Milestone 3B"), and Milestone 2 stays DEFERRED. Acceptance covers the offline code only — not a migration, not a deployment authorization, not approval of production limits, and not a statement that anything is synchronized with GitHub.

**Scope: three budgeted routes.** `POST …/account/passkeys/backup/authorize/reconcile`, `POST …/account/passkeys/backup/verify-signing/confirm`, and `POST …/account/passkeys/[credentialId]/revoke/reconcile`. They are the externally reachable requests that make the server read Turnkey (`getActivity`, `getUsers`) to settle a backup-passkey enrollment or a passkey removal. They consume the `turnkey_reconcile` family (`turnkey_reconcile_short`, `turnkey_reconcile_day`) that 3A-0 already provisioned; no new bucket, table, or SQL. The subject is the session-resolved `appUserId`, never the request body, and the same-origin request gate still refuses cross-site POSTs before any charge.

**Excluded on purpose.** `backup/authorize/submit` and `[credentialId]/revoke/submit` are not charged: each keeps its WebAuthn authorization and its durable state-machine claim (`transition` to in-flight, `beginDispatch`). Their own post-send reconcile calls a private, non-exported core with an already-admitted callback, so a submit never pays a second unit. No exported function takes an optional "skip the budget" parameter — the budgeted entrypoints require a limiter, and static guards assert the cores are unexported and unnamed elsewhere.

**Policy (development values, not production limits).** `turnkey_reconcile_short`: 20 per 600 s. `turnkey_reconcile_day`: 100 per 86 400 s. Defined once, frozen, in `lib/real/server/turnkey-reconcile-budget.ts` (`consumeTurnkeyReconcileBudget(limiter, appUserId)`); no route or pipeline carries a number or bucket name. The helper accepts exactly an allow or a denial with a whole-second delay of 1–86 400 and throws on anything else (so a malformed limiter answer is a failure, never an allow). These figures are not approved quotas and say nothing about Turnkey's pricing or limits.

**What one unit is.** One provider-bound request, charged on both buckets in one atomic operation. It is **not** one Turnkey HTTP read: a single reconcile can poll an activity several times and read the authenticator list. Database-only exits cost nothing:
- unknown, foreign or malformed ids; terminal, removed or wrong states; a missing account;
- a malformed signing-confirmation activity id (refused before the charge, using the existing `isWellFormedActivityId`);
- a removal reconcile with no recorded activity id (see R0 below);
- the stale-stamp cleanup and the legacy `definitive_failure` → `blocked` move (both database-only; the blocked read that follows in the same request is the one charged unit).

**Charge placement.** Backup reconcile: after those exits and the stale-stamp cleanup, before `claimReplay`, before any forward, `getActivity` or `getUsers`. Signing confirm: before `readTurnkeyActivity`. Removal reconcile: only when an activity id exists, before the first Turnkey read. A denial makes no Turnkey call, claims nothing, forwards nothing, writes no status, and implies no finality.

**Backup-create replay (distinct from removal).** The backup flow keeps its own controlled replay: at most one byte-identical replay of the stored signed create, claimed durably by `claimReplay`, only while the request is fresh. Because the charge precedes the claim, a denial cannot consume that one replay (tested, including a denial that takes time). **Stage 1 LOW-1 (found by the independent audit; corrected; follow-up audit accepted):** freshness used to be evaluated before the asynchronous admission, so admission time was not counted. It is now evaluated again after admission and immediately before `claimReplay` (the earlier value only gates the stale-stamp cleanup); a request that expired during admission claims and forwards nothing and falls through to the existing read-only discovery. The window and tolerances are unchanged. **Accepted residual:** one asynchronous `claimReplay` round trip between that final check and the claim.

**R0 (CLOSED at `c1919c4`) is intact.** Removal reconcile never forwards a signed DELETE; the only forward is the original submit's, after its `beginDispatch` claim (a source guard still allows exactly one `forwardSignedRequest` call site in the revocation module). With no activity id: inside the grace window `pending`, no provider call, no charge; after it the existing `blocked` / `no_activity_receipt`; S3 operator recovery is unchanged and never automatic. With an activity id: the existing evidence rules (`revoked` needs a COMPLETED delete naming exactly the target AND a later read showing it absent) after one charge. A deferred reconcile does not mean the DELETE was never sent, and no document or UI may claim removal without that evidence.

**Server denial contracts (frozen).**
- Backup reconcile: HTTP 200, the truthful stored `pending` (in flight) or `blocked` (with its stored reason) outcome, plus `reconciliationDeferred: true` and `retryAfterSeconds` (validated whole seconds).
- Removal reconcile: HTTP 200, plain `pending` with its existing continued-authorization warning, plus the same two fields. Only activity-id-bearing requests can defer.
- Signing confirmation: HTTP 429, `Retry-After`, `{ error, code: "rate_limited", retryable: true }` (the existing helper from 3A-2). The server's stored signing challenge is left untouched by a denial.
- A limiter that throws or returns something malformed: generic HTTP 500, no Turnkey call. Ordinary responses carry no deferral fields.

**Client, Stage 2A (`lib/stores/real-passkeys-store.ts`, `lib/real/display/passkey-status.ts`, `components/real/real-passkeys-manager.tsx`).**
- *Typed errors.* `api()` throws a local `ApiError` carrying status, `code`, `retryable` and the raw `Retry-After`, tolerates non-JSON bodies, and never treats an HTTP error as success. The payment store is not imported.
- *One door.* `chargedPost` is the only way to call the three charged endpoints. It refuses locally (no fetch) while a wait is active, rechecked immediately before sending, and rejects any other URL. Gating is in the store action, not just in disabled buttons.
- *One shared wait.* A private record `{ generation, appUserId, untilMs }`, memory-only (nothing in localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB or cookies), shared by backup reconcile, signing confirm and removal reconcile. Recorded only for a still-live originating scope; a later denial can only extend it (longer deadline wins).
- *Parsing.* A 200 deferral is `reconciliationDeferred === true` with a whole 1–86 400 `retryAfterSeconds`; absent means an ordinary answer; present but invalid waits the 60 s fallback and invents no outcome. A confirmation denial requires `429` and `code === "rate_limited"` and `retryable === true`; its `Retry-After` follows the payment store's strict convention (ASCII digits, no leading zero, 1–86 400, else 60 s). Any other 429, 500, 503 or network failure is an ordinary error and starts no wait.
- *Polling.* `reconcileUntilSettled` keeps its five-attempt cap and 1.5 s spacing; the first deferral (or a local refusal) ends it immediately. A deferred result is never reported as confirmed or failed.
- *Gating and non-gating.* During a wait, the three charged requests are refused, and no signing ceremony is started whose confirmation would be refused. Never gated: `revoke/options`, the original revoke submit, backup `authorize/options` and `authorize/submit`, step-up, registration, verify-login, status reads, refresh, cancel, abandon, rename. If a removal submits during a wait, only its follow-up reconcile is skipped and the pending result is shown.
- *Timer.* One timer exists only to publish the end of the wait (`isTurnkeyCheckWaiting`, `turnkeyCheckWaitSeconds`); it never fetches, signs, reconciles or invokes an action, and a stale timer cannot clear a newer deadline. Account switch and `reset()` clear the record and the timer; a late response from an old account installs nothing; a same-account remount keeps the wait; a reload loses it.

**Client, Stage 2B (retained signing proof).**
- *Record.* A private, memory-only `{ appUserId, generation, enrollmentId, activityId, sequence, failures }` — no digest, signature or secret. Installed after `signDigestViaTurnkeyRaw` returns and the scope check passes, **before** the confirmation is attempted, so a wait that starts while signing is in flight still keeps the returned id.
- *Reuse.* At `login_verified`, after the status read, a matching proof is confirmed again through `chargedPost` with the original `activityId` — no `verify-signing/options`, no new challenge, no new signing activity. During a wait nothing is sent and the proof stays. Only a user action retries.
- *Three-400 policy (control-room decision).* The first two HTTP 400s keep the proof; the third clears it and shows the existing error; nothing is re-signed automatically, and a later user action may begin a fresh approval. Classification is by status only. 429, 500, 503 and network errors keep the proof and neither add to nor reset the count; a new proof starts at zero. Rationale: `readTurnkeyActivity` can turn a transient provider error into a 400, so one 400 does not prove the proof invalid, but an invalid proof is not kept forever.
- *Clearing.* Success; `reset()` or an account switch; a status read (`fetchStatus` or `refresh`) showing no enrollment, a different enrollment id, or a state other than `login_verified`; abandon. A status read that began before the proof existed cannot discard it (sequence ordering); a stale 400 cannot count against or clear a newer proof (identity check).
- *Overlap.* `continueBackupSetup` is a no-op while a setup action is running, so a second Resume cannot mint a second signing challenge that would invalidate the first.

**Stage 2 follow-up (LOW-1 and LOW-2: corrected; independently accepted).**
- *LOW-1.* A blocked backup setup refused by the wait used to lose its stored review reason to generic "unconfirmed" copy. It now keeps the stored `blockReason` (or the existing default) in `setupError` with the review-wait line, and a setup in flight keeps its in-flight copy.
- *LOW-2.* A removal-check button disabled by a wait that started elsewhere now has an adjacent explanation, linked with `aria-describedby` (skipped when the row's own message already carries it). Remove, Rename and the backup controls stay enabled.
- *Stale wait copy.* Temporary wait copy carries explicit ownership (`setupMessageIsWait`, and `waitBase` on a removal message); when the wait ends only that copy is dropped (a removal message reverts to the server's own base text). Review reasons, errors and pending/revoking states are untouched, nothing is fetched, and there is no live countdown.

**Audit and verification record (offline only; distinct runs, not to be added together).**
- Stage 1 initial independent audit: PASSED — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low (the freshness-after-admission finding above), 8 Info. LOW-1 correction independently accepted. Stage 1 LOW-1 follow-up: 0 / 0 / 0 / 0 Low, 4 Info.
- Stage 2 initial independent audit: PASSED — 0 / 0 / 0 / 2 Low (the two corrections above), 6 Info. Both Lows corrected and independently accepted. Stage 2 follow-up: 0 / 0 / 0 / 0 Low, 6 Info; verdict "ready for post-Stage-2 documentation closeout".
- Latest targeted verification: 862 focused tests passed, 0 failed; `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` passed. An earlier, broader Stage 1 run: 3553 passed, 322 gated smoke tests skipped, 0 failed. The new behaviors are pinned by fake-limiter, fake-Turnkey, fake-fetch and fake-timer tests, several mutation-checked against the earlier code. **No live-provider, Neon or browser test has been run, and none is authorized.**
- Final integrated whole-slice independent security audit: **PASSED** — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low, 4 Info; verdict "ready for manual 3A-3 Git checkpoint". The Low was a documentation inconsistency (a stale R0 Git-checkpoint status in the roadmap and project notes), since corrected; the Info items were not acted on. The audit added no test run to the figures above, and it was offline like everything else. The operator then committed and pushed the slice as `78be1ad`.

**Accepted residuals (controlled-development only; kept visible, none claimed resolved).**
- The wait and the proof are memory-only: a reload or another tab may need another passkey approval and signing activity; the busy guard is per tab.
- Per-account control only: no per-IP, global or registration-time limit. Shared-family exhaustion can delay backup and removal confirmation, and a long denial can let an eligible backup replay window expire.
- The `claimReplay` round-trip freshness gap above.
- A transient Turnkey activity-read error can surface as HTTP 400 (and spend one of the three). Turnkey activity retention, pricing and quotas are unverified; the figures above are not provider-informed.
- No automatic provider retry is promised. A removal can remain pending while provider authority is uncertain; S3 recovery stays operator-only; R0's own residuals stand (lost original response, no explicit forward timeout, operator capacity and alerting).
- Presentation limits: a displayed wait length can go stale when a later denial extends the shared deadline; a daily wait can read awkwardly ("for a moment" beside "about 24 hours"); browser timer throttling can delay re-enabling (a lazy settle on the next charged action narrows this); some removal wait text is not linked to the disabled button when the row already owns it; minor message-ownership and detached-row presentation limits.
- The "FIX 3" same-millisecond ordering flake and the Practice `simulation-store` `Date.now()` race remain separate; neither was fixed here.

**Deployment prerequisites (none done).** ON Chain Finance remains localhost-only. `real_provider_budgets` has not been applied to the real Neon database, so against it all three routes would answer a generic 500 (the limiter fails closed) — do not deploy or add a bypass. Needed, in order: explicit migration authorization; controlled migration; independent verification; approval of production provider policies and windows (informed by Turnkey pricing and quotas, which are unresearched); a separate deployment authorization. GitHub source synchronization after the final audit is not deployment.

## Public Readiness Milestone 3B — Request-Size Limits (technical implementation COMPLETE at `8206fde`; closes with the operator's documentation checkpoint; NOT deployable)

**Status wording.** Slices 3B-1 through 3B-4 and the handle-canonicalization correction are each **CLOSED** at the commits below, and every slice passed an independent security review. The 3B technical implementation is **complete at `8206fde`**. Final milestone closeout occurs with the operator's manually committed and pushed **documentation checkpoint** — the Git commit that contains this documentation update, identifiable in Git history after `8206fde`. The order is: documentation Builder, independent read-only audit, any separately authorized correction, an independent follow-up review when needed, the operator's own review of the final staged diff, then the operator's commit and push; Milestone 3B is formally closed once that checkpoint is committed and pushed. A document cannot know its own commit hash, so this record names none. 3B is **not** a deployment authorization, not a statement that Real Mode is public- or mainnet-ready, and not a migration. Milestone 2 (invitation-only registration) stays DEFERRED and keeps its numbering.

**What it is.** Milestone 3A gave each account a *budget* for requests that make the server call a provider. Milestone 3B bounds *how large a request body* any Real Mode route will read, so a hostile or buggy client cannot make a route buffer, decode or parse an arbitrarily large body. It is per-route size policy plus one shared gate; it is not rate limiting and not per-IP control.

**Completed commits (verified against `git log`).**

| Slice | Commit | What it did |
|---|---|---|
| Handle canonicalization correction | `8078ced` | Replaced a quadratic whitespace-trimming regular expression with a linear scan. |
| 3B-1 | `ba2e5c4` | Added `readBoundedJsonBody()` and the 4 KiB control policy; migrated 14 control routes. |
| 3B-2 | `1ad58d1` | Made the Next.js proxy body-copy limit explicit and added the mandatory `Content-Length` gate. |
| 3B-3 | `9a27be9` | Bounded the 4 WebAuthn-assertion routes (16 KiB) and the 2 signed-Turnkey routes (64 KiB). |
| 3B-4 | `8206fde` | Bounded the 2 WebAuthn-registration routes (64 KiB); removed the legacy `readJsonBody()`. |

**Final request-body policy matrix** (constants in `lib/real/server/http.ts`; each measures actual UTF-8 transport bytes, never string length):

| Constant | Bytes | Routes |
|---|---|---|
| `CONTROL_BODY_MAX_BYTES` | 4096 | 14 — payments `prepare` and `submit`; `recipients/lookup`; `handle/options`; `profile`; passkey rename; removal `cancel` and `reconcile`; backup `abandon`, `authorize/options`, `authorize/reconcile`, `verify-login/options`, `verify-signing/options`, `verify-signing/confirm` |
| `WEBAUTHN_ASSERTION_BODY_MAX_BYTES` | 16384 | 4 — `login/verify`, `handle/claim`, backup `options` (step-up assertion), `verify-login/confirm` |
| `SIGNED_TURNKEY_REQUEST_BODY_MAX_BYTES` | 65536 | 2 — removal `submit`, backup `authorize/submit` |
| `WEBAUTHN_REGISTRATION_BODY_MAX_BYTES` | 65536 | 2 — `register/verify`, backup `register` |

The two 64 KiB policies are **separate named constants** that happen to be equal; nothing requires them to differ, and either may change independently. The hierarchy that tests pin is control < assertion < registration < global gate, and signed < global gate. A route names exactly one family constant and never an inline number.

**Route inventory (verified against the repository).** 34 route files export 35 handlers (`session` exports both `GET` and `DELETE`). **22** handlers read a body and **all 22** use `readBoundedJsonBody()` (14 + 4 + 2 + 2). **13** are body-less: seven `GET`s (balance, payment status, payment history, latest payment, passkey list, backup status, session) and the six exempt unsafe-method routes (`register/options`, `login/options`, `backup/step-up/options`, `[credentialId]/revoke/options`, `payments/[id]/cancel`, `DELETE session`). Do not confuse route *files* with *handlers*. The production helper `readJsonBody()` was removed in 3B-4; source guards fail if it reappears anywhere in `app/`, `lib/` or `components/`, and fail if any body-reading route is added without being counted.

**What the bounded reader does** (`readBoundedJsonBody(request, maxBytes)`). `maxBytes` is a required positive safe integer — there is no default and no fallback to `request.json()`. If `Content-Length` is present it must be plain decimal digits that form a safe integer (anything else, including a duplicated or list value, is *invalid*); a declared length over the limit is *too large* at once and the stream is cancelled; a declared length within the limit must equal the bytes actually received (a mismatch is *invalid*). Bytes are counted per chunk **before** the chunk is kept, so the chunk that crosses the limit is never retained, an oversized stream is cancelled, and a reader error or abort is *invalid* (a partial body is never parsed). The complete body is decoded with fatal UTF-8 (no replacement characters), parsed with `JSON.parse` with no reviver, and returned untouched — no trimming, normalization or re-serialization. An absent `Content-Length` is accepted by the reader (the gate below requires it earlier). Each route maps *too large* to HTTP 413 `{ "error": "This request is too large.", "code": "request_too_large" }` (no `retryable`, no `Retry-After`, no echo of the request) immediately after the read, and maps *invalid* to its own existing HTTP 400, so no success path or 400 message changed. **What it is not:** it is not an early network-level limit and does not stop a slow upload — it runs inside the route handler, after the framework has handled the request.

**The request gate (3B-2).** `REAL_API_MAX_BODY_BYTES`, exported from `lib/real/server/request-gate.ts`, was `10_485_760` (10 MiB) when 3B closed and is **`262_144` (256 KiB) since the separate post-3B global-ceiling change** (see "Post-3B Global Body Ceiling" below; the figures in the rest of this paragraph and in the 3B-2 verification record below that are the 3B-time 10 MiB ones unless they say otherwise). `next.config.ts` sets `experimental.proxyClientMaxBodySize` to that same imported constant (at 3B-2 the installed Next.js 16.3.4 default was the same 10 MiB, so the copy limit was then made explicit, not changed; it is now deliberately below that default). The reason is that Next.js copies a proxied request body only up to that limit and, past it, silently keeps just the prefix while continuing normally; a route could otherwise see a truncated body that parses. The gate therefore refuses any larger declared length before a route runs, and a parity test loads the configuration with Next.js's own loader to keep the two values equal. Precedence for an unsafe method on `/api/real/**`: cross-site Fetch Metadata or a foreign `Origin` → 403; a known body-less route → passes (no content-type or length needed); a non-JSON content type → 415; missing `Content-Length` → 411 (`{ "error": "Unsupported request.", "code": "length_required" }`); malformed, duplicated or unsafe-integer `Content-Length` → 400; declared length above the ceiling (then 10 MiB, now 256 KiB) → 413 (the same body as above); otherwise the request proceeds. A body at exactly the ceiling passes the gate and is then refused by the route's own smaller limit. `GET`, `HEAD` and `OPTIONS` are unchanged (apart from the existing protected-read rules). The gate grants no authentication and runs before any route authenticates, so it can answer its own 403, 415, 411, 400 or 413 (a declared length above the ceiling) to an unauthenticated caller. Each route still authenticates as before: on the session-first routes the route's own bounded-reader 413 is never reached unauthenticated, while payments `prepare` and `submit`, `login/verify` and `register/verify` read their body without a prior session read, as they did before.

**Handle-canonicalization correction (`8078ced`).** The handle canonicalizer trimmed ASCII whitespace with a regular expression whose trailing alternative rescanned a long whitespace run from every offset, so input shaped like `"a"` + many spaces + `"a"` took quadratic time (about 15 ms at 5 000 characters, 233 ms at 20 000, 932 ms at 40 000 and roughly 4.5 s at 80 000 in local measurement). That parsing was reachable on the payment-prepare path before authentication. It is replaced by a linear scan over exactly the same six ASCII whitespace characters; accepted and rejected handles, results and error text are identical (checked against the original expression as a reference oracle). This removes one specific superlinear parser; it does not claim that API denial of service is solved.

**Key behavior preserved.** Signed Turnkey request strings (`signedRequest.body`, the stamp value, the URL) reach the services byte-for-byte whatever the outer JSON's whitespace or escaping, and the parser's own limits (16 384 and 8 192 UTF-16 units, depth 32) are unchanged. WebAuthn data reaches the resolvers equal to what `request.json()` produced. A 413 happens before challenge consumption, registration or enrollment work, provisioning, signing, replay or dispatch claims, provider-budget charging and every durable mutation. Safe is still the user account; Turnkey is still signer infrastructure; R0's no-signed-DELETE-replay rule, the no-blind-UserOperation-retry rule, exact identity comparison, and the finalize-only activation path are untouched.

**Verification record — keep these distinct.**
- *Last independent 3B-4 security audit (offline):* focused regression **1 792 tests passed**; full offline regression **4 880 passed, 1 failed, 322 skipped**. The single failure was the previously known intermittent "FIX 3" revocation-ordering test (`backup-step-up-and-pending-removal.test.ts`, a same-millisecond `latestForTarget` tie), assessed as unrelated to the 3B-4 changes. It has **not** been fixed, and this is not a clean full-suite pass. `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` passed.
- *Builder runs during the slices* used narrower scopes and are not additive with the audit's: for example the 3B-4 Builder's run of `test/lib/real`, `test/lib/stores` and `test/components/real` was 4 296 passed / 319 skipped / 0 failed.
- *Final read-only 3B closeout audit:* "ready for 3B documentation closeout". It did not rerun the test suite.
- *This documentation pass* changed no code and ran no tests.
- 3B-2 also ran a bounded localhost integration probe against a scratch copy of the built application (dummy configuration, no providers or database): a body at exactly 10 MiB reached the route whole, 10 MiB + 1 was refused by the gate, and — in a control copy with the gate disabled — a valid-JSON prefix followed by padding past 10 MiB *was* delivered truncated to an unbounded route and parsed. Headless Chrome and Safari on that machine sent a `Content-Length` on every JSON string body. This is evidence for that environment only (see the prerequisites below).

**Accepted residuals — controlled local development only (none is claimed eliminated).**
- The global 10 MiB ceiling was retained at 3B's closeout (decision below); it was lowered afterwards by the separate post-3B change recorded in "Post-3B Global Body Ceiling".
- Next.js buffers a proxied request body (up to the ceiling) and drains the whole upload; a 411/413 does not stop that, and for a slow upload the refusal is delayed until the framework has read the body. Bodyless routes can still cause framework-level buffering.
- Route parsing may happen before authentication where it already did (payments `prepare` and `submit`, `login/verify`, `register/verify`); the session-first routes authenticate first.
- Node's HTTP parser answers malformed, duplicated or mismatched framing with its own empty 400 before the gate sees it, so the gate's own 400 branch is defense in depth for other front ends.
- The 64 KiB registration policy rests mainly on offline, synthetic models (see below).

**Remaining public-deployment prerequisites (not done).** Some controls already exist — the Milestone 1 registration provisioning dispatch ceilings, and a session cookie that is already `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` and `Secure` in production — but they do not make the product public-ready; what remains: Hosting and edge upload limits; connection-concurrency controls; header and body timeouts; per-IP traffic protection; Firefox and wider browser testing; HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 behavior; actual hosting and CDN behavior, including that `Content-Length` is forwarded; real-device WebAuthn registration payload measurements and passkey compatibility; production provider-budget migration and policy (3A); remaining registration admission and abuse controls (Milestone 2 and the S5 register; the Milestone 1 dispatch ceilings already exist); and the existing deployment tasks — script CSP, a host-specific RP ID and origin, the platform duration limit, and HSTS and the host-only (`__Host-`) cookie decisions.

**Separate hardening work (recorded, not confirmed exploitable, severities not assigned here).**
- A possible pre-existing Turnkey stamp-header forwarding edge case, noted by the 3B-3 scope audit; deliberately not investigated or changed in 3B.
- The "FIX 3" revocation `latestForTarget` same-millisecond ordering flake, and the unrelated Practice `simulation-store` `Date.now()` race — both still open.
- `experimental.proxyClientMaxBodySize` is an experimental Next.js option; its copy-and-truncate behavior must be re-verified on every Next.js upgrade.
- A false-positive 413 on a registration could leave an **orphan passkey**: the browser creates the credential before the server sees the response, so a legitimate response over 64 KiB would be refused after the credential exists on the device. The orphan would sit unused in the user's password manager and could make a retry confusing, but the server never registered it: the 413 comes before challenge consumption and before any registration or backup enrollment, so no account provisioning or credential enrollment happens for that request and the orphan gains no account authority. The risk is not fixed, and this does not claim it can never occur with a real authenticator.

**Registration-limit compatibility (stated plainly).** The 65 536-byte WebAuthn registration limit is an approved controlled-development policy. It has **not** been validated against a comprehensive real-authenticator matrix: the implementation was checked with offline fixtures and synthetic registration models (modeled envelopes ran from about 1.3 KiB for a typical ES256 response to roughly 42 KiB for a deliberately extreme synthetic). One historical real Turnkey body measured 1 319 characters, which is not a directly measured browser registration envelope. Future validation should cover representative Apple platform passkeys, Google Password Manager, Windows Hello and roaming FIDO2 keys across relevant browsers and attestation formats. No live registration measurement was authorized or made.

**Global ceiling decision.** Milestone 3B can close with the 10 MiB global ceiling unchanged: all 22 individual route limits are enforced, and the ceiling only bounds what the framework will copy and what the gate admits. Lowering the ceiling (128 KiB was an earlier proposal only, **not approved and not an agreed future limit**) requires a separate policy review (see "Explicitly Not Yet Decided"), and the gate's constant and `next.config.ts` must change together. Neither is changed now, and no new milestone number is assigned to that review. *(Historical: this was the decision at 3B's closeout, `d9a0564`. It was later superseded by the separate policy decision recorded next.)*

### Post-3B Global Body Ceiling (256 KiB; controlled development only; NOT deployable)

A separate post-3B hardening change, **not** a reopening of Milestone 3B and not Milestone 3C, 3D or 3E. Milestone 3B closed at `d9a0564` with the 10 MiB ceiling; those 3B records are historical and are not rewritten. After an independent read-only policy audit of the existing ceiling, the control room approved lowering it for controlled local development.

- **Policy.** `REAL_API_MAX_BODY_BYTES` is now `262_144` (256 KiB), down from `10_485_760` (10 MiB): the most the framework may copy for one proxied request, and the most the gate admits, drops 40-fold. Exactly 262 144 is admitted by the gate; 262 145 is the gate's 413. A leading-zero `Content-Length` is read as the number it spells. The 128 KiB proposal was not chosen. Changing the ceiling again needs a separate policy decision.
- **Parity (mandatory, unchanged architecture).** `next.config.ts` still imports the gate's constant for `experimental.proxyClientMaxBodySize`; there is no second literal. The parity test now pins the approved value, requires strict EQUALITY (not `>=`) between the Next.js-loaded limit in the development, build and production-server phases and the gate constant, and its source guard forbids the 256 KiB value in any spelling inside `next.config.ts`. The route source guard likewise forbids the 256 KiB spellings inside any route.
- **Unchanged.** All 22 route limits (4 KiB × 14, 16 KiB × 4, 64 KiB × 2 signed Turnkey, 64 KiB × 2 registration); `readBoundedJsonBody()` in all 22; the gate precedence (403, 415, 411, 400, 413); the 413 body `{ "error": "This request is too large.", "code": "request_too_large" }` with no `retryable`, no `Retry-After` and no echo; the body-less exemptions; safe-method handling; authentication, signing, provider budgets, registration, recovery, payments and Practice Mode. The hierarchy tests now read control < assertion < registration < 256 KiB gate, and signed < gate.
- **Authentication precedence.** The gate still runs before any route authenticates, so a declared length above 256 KiB now receives the gate's 413 even from a caller whose route would otherwise answer 401. This is a known, accepted consequence of the lower threshold; session ordering was not changed.
- **Offline framework check (installed Next.js 16.3.4, `getCloneableBody`, synthetic streams in a scratch directory outside the repository; no server, network, database or provider).** Exactly 262 144 and 262 143 bytes were copied intact, one chunk or several. 262 145 bytes crossed the limit: the copy was cut at a chunk boundary (the crossing chunk is dropped whole, so a single 262 145-byte chunk yielded an empty copy and four 64 KiB chunks plus one more byte yielded the first 262 144 bytes), one console warning was written, and the source stream was still drained to its end. A complete valid-JSON prefix followed by an oversized later chunk produced a truncated copy that *still parses* — which is exactly why the gate must refuse every declared length above the copy limit before a route runs. This is the behavior of that installed version only, and it was NOT re-measured through a running server in this pass; no early transport rejection is claimed.
- **Accepted residuals (controlled localhost only; none is claimed eliminated).**
  - Next.js still buffers and drains an oversized upload before a 411/413 can be sent; slow-upload and connection-exhaustion attacks are unresolved. The lower ceiling shrinks per-request copy retention (at most 256 KiB instead of 10 MiB); it does not bound concurrent or slow uploads.
  - **Log volume.** Next.js writes a console warning whenever a body exceeds the copy limit. A lower limit makes that warning cheaper to trigger (any body over 256 KiB, not 10 MiB). No workaround was added and no Next.js internals were touched; production log-volume protection does not exist.
  - The 64 KiB registration limit still rests mainly on offline synthetic models. Real-device registration measurements remain incomplete, and a false-positive 413 could still orphan a passkey (see above).
  - The gate trusts the declared `Content-Length` and the server's HTTP framing; behavior behind a CDN or proxy is unverified.
  - `experimental.proxyClientMaxBodySize` is experimental and version-dependent; this check must be repeated on every Next.js upgrade.
- **Deployment prerequisites unchanged and still not done:** edge body-size controls; header and body timeouts; connection concurrency; per-IP rate limits; CDN behavior including that `Content-Length` is forwarded; HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 and Firefox testing; platform-hosted proxy behavior; real-authenticator registration measurements; provider-budget production migration and policies; a host-specific RP ID and origin; script CSP; cookie/HSTS decisions; the platform duration limit. Public deployment remains unauthorized.
- **Verification (offline; separate from the 3B records above and not additive with them).** The three directly affected test files: 561 passed / 0 failed. One broader offline run: 4 948 passed / 1 failed / 322 skipped; the single failure was the known unrelated Practice `simulation-store` "starts from createInitialState()" `Date.now()` race, which passed (28/28) when its file was run alone — so this is not a clean full-suite pass. `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` passed. The "FIX 3" ordering flake did not fail in that run; both flakes remain open and untouched. No live-browser, database, provider or deployment test was run.

### Public Readiness Milestone 3C — Slice 3C-1: Global Registration Admission (CLOSED at `46d1cc2`; live-verified on a disposable branch ONLY; NOT applied to the application database; NOT deployable)

Milestone 3C is "Unauthenticated Rate Limits"; **3C-1 is its first slice** (CLOSED). 3C-2 (stateless login challenges) was authorized and built afterwards — see "Slice 3C-2" below (CLOSED at `7d66abe`); 3C-3a (an advisory provisioning pre-check) followed — see "Slice 3C-3a" below (CLOSED at `beac686`); 3C-3b (canonical registration-challenge shape validation) followed — see "Slice 3C-3b" below (CLOSED at `48a98d9`); 3C-3c is not authorized, 3D (provider timeouts) and the broader 3E (authenticated write limits) are not started (a narrow preparatory challenge-purge slice exists — see "Bounded Shared WebAuthn Challenge Purge" below), Milestone 2 stays DEFERRED, no milestone is renumbered, and **Milestone 3 as a whole is not closed**. Keep these statuses distinct:

- *Technical implementation:* COMPLETE. *Independent offline implementation audit:* passed. *Offline regression:* passed.
- *Disposable-Neon live verification:* the first run (`ced714ee`) was **POISONED by a test-fixture defect** and is not a success; the second run (`c20ffc87`), on a different brand-new branch, **COMPLETED_CLEAN** (`clean_and_evidenced`). Details below. *Final independent live-evidence audit:* passed (0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low — outdated closeout documentation, which this update addresses — and 5 Informational).
- *Documentation closeout and formal milestone closure:* **COMPLETE.** The operator's manual Git checkpoint is `46d1cc2` ("feat: add global registration-start admission limits", verified against `git log`), which contains the 3C-1 implementation and this documentation as first written. Wording in this section, or elsewhere in that commit, that calls closure PENDING is historical and superseded by this line. Closure is not a deployment approval, a migration of the application database, or a statement about public readiness.
- *Where the migration has run:* ONLY in scratch schemas and in the `public` schema of the disposable branch used by run `c20ffc87`. It has **not** been authorized or applied, through this process, to the normal application database. This is not a production migration, and **public deployment remains NOT AUTHORIZED**.

**Policy and behavior**

- **What it does.** `POST /api/real/account/register/options` — the unauthenticated step that mints a registration challenge — asks one GLOBAL admission pool whether a registration may start. The pool is **30 admitted starts per 3600 s and 100 per 86400 s**, shared by everyone: two first-admission-anchored fixed windows with independent rollover, on PostgreSQL database time. They count registration **starts**, not completed accounts. An admitted request charges both windows; a refused request changes neither counter nor window. The numbers are frozen code constants (`lib/real/server/registration-admission.ts`) with no environment override. A fixed window can admit up to about twice its limit around a boundary (accepted). The Milestone 1 provisioning dispatch ceilings (5/hour, 20/day, 2 per attempt) are a different mechanism and are unchanged — do not confuse the two.
- **Route order (`app/api/real/account/register/options/route.ts`).** Real Mode disabled → the existing 404 (the limiter is not called) → server configuration (a failure is the generic 500 and charges nothing) → global admission → refused: HTTP **503** `{ "error": "We can't open new accounts right now. Please try again later.", "code": "registration_unavailable" }` with an advisory whole-second `Retry-After`, and **no** challenge, passkey ceremony, attempt, or provider dispatch → admitted: `beginRegistration` mints the one challenge. A limiter or database failure is the generic 500 with no challenge — a storage fault is never disguised as an exhausted pool. A challenge that fails after admission is not refunded. The route takes no request (it reads no header, cookie, or body), stays body-less in the gate, and does not reuse the verify routes' `registration_capacity_reached` response. No limiter was added to registration verify or login, no provisioning or recovery policy changed, there is no IP attribution, and Practice Mode is untouched. The client needed no change: `register()` shows the server's `error` text, starts no ceremony after a non-OK options answer, and never retries (tested).
- **Table.** `real_registration_admission` (`schema.sql`, "Registration Admission" block, between Provider Budgets and Provisioning Evidence, which stays last): `scope` (`TEXT COLLATE "C"`, primary key, CHECK `= 'register_options'`), `short_window_start`, `short_hits`, `day_window_start`, `day_hits`, with `0..100000` CHECKs on the counters (a sanity/overflow bound, not the policy). One singleton row; no IP, account, session, or client-controlled column; no foreign key, secondary index, trigger, or default. Window lengths and limits are parameters, not schema, so a policy change needs no migration.
- **Admission SQL — two statements, never a transaction.** **E1** is one autocommit conditional `UPDATE … WHERE … RETURNING` on the singleton row: it admits only if each window has elapsed or is under its limit, and its `SET` charges both windows (resetting an elapsed one to `now()`). **Exactly one returned row is the only way to be admitted.** Under READ COMMITTED a concurrent `UPDATE` waits on the row's tuple lock, then re-checks the `WHERE` clause and recomputes the `SET` values from the newest committed version, so counters are neither lost nor oversubscribed; the `WHERE` clause reads only this row's own columns. **E2** runs only when E1 returned zero rows: a separate read-only `SELECT` with a fresh snapshot returning both counters and each window's remaining whole seconds (NULL when elapsed). It only classifies a refusal and times Retry-After; **it is never an alternative admission path**. Zero rows from E1 is not "missing" — it can be an exhausted window, a lost race at a boundary, or an absent row. The limiter is **READ COMMITTED** (the level in force on the pooled endpoint; nothing sets it), not serializable: under REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE a concurrent `UPDATE` would raise a serialization failure, which throws (fail closed) but would hurt availability. The adapter is `createNeonRegistrationAdmissionStore` in `neon-store.ts`; policy, validation, classification, the limiter wrapper, and the in-memory model are in `registration-admission.ts`, and `runtime.ts` selects Neon when `DATABASE_URL` is set and the in-memory model only past the existing production fail-closed throw.
- **Fail closed.** Every policy value is validated before any statement (whole numbers; windows 1..86400 s; limits 1..100000; short window strictly shorter than the daily one; exact keys). Exactly one E1 row with counters in `1..limit` is an admission; more than one row, a missing row (E1 and E2 both empty), malformed fields, or any database error throws. If E2 sees neither window exhausted after E1 refused, the answer is still a **refusal** (advice 1 s) — never an admission. A counter above a lowered limit counts as exhausted.
- **Retry-After (advisory).** The longest exhausted window's remaining time, rounded up, minimum 1, maximum 86400; each reported wait is validated to `1..window + 60 s` and clamped to the window; anything else throws. A later request is not guaranteed admission. No count, limit, window start, or key is ever exposed.
- **Denial, stated accurately.** A refusal changes no counter and no window and (as asserted by the smoke) writes no new row version. A refused `UPDATE` that raced a writer may take a transient tuple lock and generate a lock WAL record, and every refusal still costs one or two queries. This is **not** "zero database work".

**Migration evidence identity.** The Registration Admission statement is **13101 characters, SHA-256 `b269ebcc0953efce79c7504ec451237eca3dc1bc4d67996d7179b5eba26ac91f`** (pinned in tests and recorded by both runs' evidence). Unchanged, recomputed with the same extraction rule: Handle Pay Rate Limits (Slice E) 11680 / `78c5557d5b615805f0eb8c63e62d09eccffc792c95f9e2782f9af48d92d1513d`; Provider Budgets 11820 / `8213b46c2f859fb3c8c5457d34d3dff682508d401fcf82b9169b84333c67a5f5`. The new statement is now evidence-bearing: do not change it without a fresh smoke on a brand-new disposable branch. Its header comment still says "NOT APPLIED ANYWHERE … never been parsed or run by PostgreSQL"; that wording predates the live runs and is **superseded by this section** — comments above the statement do not change its digest, so a later, separately authorized comment-only correction is possible.

**First disposable-Neon run — `ced714ee` — POISONED (not a success).** PostgreSQL 18.6, pooled connection, READ COMMITTED, one run. S1 (missing table fails closed), S2 (scratch apply, seed, idempotent rerun, counter preservation, catalog descriptor) and S3 (CHECK, primary key, NOT NULL enforcement) passed; in S4 the exact control copy was accepted and the extra-column look-alike (`m01`) was refused with P0001. S4 then failed while **building** the `m02` "missing column" fixture with SQLSTATE **42703**: the fixture dropped the `day_hits` column but kept the CHECK that names it, so PostgreSQL refused the `CREATE TABLE` before the migration's guard ever ran. This was a **test-fixture defect; no defect in the production migration, admission SQL, or runtime was established.** The harness poisoned the run permanently and retried nothing: 92 operations issued, no admission tallies, no cleanup attempted, no final fingerprint; S5–S7 (so the public-schema migration), I1–I7, C1–C4, R1–R2 and F1–F4 never executed. Five scratch schemas were registered; **their final existence was never verified, and the branch may still hold scratch objects. Do not claim it was cleaned up or that its final state is known.**

**The S4 correction.** The `m02` fixture was changed from `exact({ dayHits: null })` to `exact({ dayStart: null })` (`day_window_start` has no dependent constraint), giving a constructible table with four of the five intended columns that the migration's exact-column guard refuses with P0001. Five offline fixture-consistency tests were added to the smoke file's always-run block: every one of the 28 look-alikes' DDL is checked so no constraint, index, or added constraint names a column its table lacks; removing each intended column is flagged exactly when a surviving constraint names it; the check fails closed on anything it does not recognize; `m02` genuinely lacks exactly one intended column; and the 15 mandatory plus 13 further variants are pinned by name. The original defect was temporarily reintroduced offline and two of those tests failed as intended, after which the file was restored. The check is a purpose-built splitter, not a SQL parser (it covers only references to the five intended column names). The correction and the remaining unexecuted smoke SQL were independently audited before the second run.

**Second disposable-Neon run — `c20ffc87` — COMPLETED_CLEAN, verdict `clean_and_evidenced`.** A brand-new, operator-confirmed disposable branch (not the first run's). PostgreSQL 18.6, pooled, READ COMMITTED read through the same single-statement path production admission uses. All 24 decisive cases ran; nothing was skipped, poisoned, or unexpected. The operator-reported console result was 44 passed / 0 failed (18 offline harness tests and 26 live: the 24 cases plus two closing assertions) in 41.86 s — those console figures are **not** in the evidence files. Verified outcomes:

- **Schema (S1–S7).** Missing table fails closed (42P01). The migration created and seeded the singleton, reran idempotently, preserved a live counter across reruns, and enforced CHECK, PRIMARY KEY and NOT NULL (42 recorded database refusals in total). **All 28 look-alikes (15 mandatory, 13 further) were constructible and each was refused by the migration's own guard with P0001** — including the previously uncertain `a07` foreign-key fixture, which constructed. The block applied to the disposable branch's `public` schema, and reran.
- **Sequential (I1–I7).** The first admission anchored both windows; 30 admitted then the 31st denied; 100 admitted across hourly windows then the 101st denied; hourly rollover; daily and independent rollover (a refusal charged neither window); Retry-After for short-only, daily-only, both (the longest), and a lowered limit; 25 further refusals left counters, window starts, `xmin` and `ctid` unchanged (the case asserts it; the saved evidence records the counters but not every intermediate `xmin`/`ctid`).
- **Concurrency (C1–C4).** 60 simultaneous requests at limit 30 → 30 admitted, 30 denied; 50 at a daily limit of 20 → 20 admitted, 30 denied, both counters 20/20; 10 with both counters one below their limits → 1 admitted, 9 denied; 40 over elapsed windows → 30 admitted, 10 denied, counters 30/30.
- **READ COMMITTED races (R1–R2).** R1: with a second session holding the row lock, the racing admission was observed waiting on the lock, then was denied after the holder committed; the row ended 30/30 (no over-admission). R2: a racer whose snapshot saw the exhausted window while another session rolled it was denied (it settled before the holder, recorded as an observation), and a fresh request afterwards was admitted (counters 1/41). The harness establishes the interleaving by polling `pg_stat_activity`; the exact server-side degree of overlap was **not** directly measured.
- **Failure cases (F1–F4).** A missing singleton row failed closed (11 fail-closed refusals, none admitted), was not recreated by any request, and an explicit rerun of the migration reseeded it; far-future window starts failed closed (`malformed_state`) while a small future start was clamped within the tolerance; a database error (table renamed away) propagated as an error — neither an admission nor an ordinary denial; the row was returned to the seed state.
- **Resources.** 720 tracked operations (budget 1500), 35 counted fixture row writes (budget 60), 4 activity polls (budget 80), at most 60 simultaneous requests, two bounded lock holders, no unknown or in-flight operations. Known limits: the two lock-holder row writes are not in the 35-write tally (they count toward the operation budget), and a timed-out HTTP request is not necessarily cancellable (none occurred).
- **Cleanup.** 129 of 129 steps confirmed (31 inventory reads, 31 dependency reads, 35 object drops, 31 schema drops, one residue read), 0 unexpected, 0 refused, 0 uncertain, 0 in flight, 0 unattempted; all 31 registered scratch schemas were removed; the residue check returned 0; drops were RESTRICT, never CASCADE; no public table was dropped.
- **Final database delta.** Baseline **plus one intended addition**, not a restoration: `public.real_registration_admission` now exists with exactly the one seed row (`short_hits` 0, `day_hits` 0, both window starts at the epoch). The 11 previously measured public tables kept matching row counts, content digests and shape digests. The branch was deliberately left in place for evidence review.

**Evidence files (private, mode 0600, git-ignored under `/evidence/`, not to be modified or committed; their contents are not copied here).** First run: `evidence/registration-admission-smoke-ced714ee-start.json`, `evidence/registration-admission-smoke-ced714ee-final.json`. Second run: `evidence/registration-admission-smoke-c20ffc87-start.json`, `evidence/registration-admission-smoke-c20ffc87-final.json`.

**Executed source identity (must stay byte-identical).** `test/lib/real/registration-admission.smoke.test.ts` SHA-256 `f344ad058afa580b87e7174d8dbff7449334f9a3d8917b92ce767e244d03783f`; `test/lib/real/fixtures/registration-admission-smoke.ts` `9a2956a792df5f22c2546d4879ef685fcf2eee126fdb51b85fadcdadd6d0b97d`; `test/lib/real/registration-admission-smoke-harness.test.ts` `168bbcf06a3ed24a0672bc9f18d58b5842597079d53ef7f1306ae4e8d12a30a6`. Those files' headers say "IT HAS NEVER BEEN RUN LIVE" (and the fixture calls its SQL unexecuted). That wording is **historical and superseded by the verified execution evidence above**; it was deliberately not edited, because editing would break byte equality with the source that ran (an offline test also pins the header). Production comments in `registration-admission.ts` and `neon-store.ts` ("it has not been run", "has not been asserted live", "has not been created anywhere yet") are likewise outdated and untouched in this docs-only pass.

**What the evidence does NOT establish.** The saved evidence does not independently prove the Neon endpoint's identity (that rests on the operator's independent Neon Console confirmation and the harness's target safeguards), the state of the normal application database, the state of every database object, the absence of transient changes by another session, the exact degree of concurrent overlap, every R1/R2 timing detail, every I7 `xmin`/`ctid` value, or the console-only pass counts and duration. The final comparison measures the listed public tables and the schema names — it is not an object-by-object inventory. Nothing here claims more than the audit supports.

**Branch rules (lasting).** The branch behind run `ced714ee` is **poisoned and must never be reused** for any smoke; it may still hold scratch schemas, and no automatic cleanup or repair was authorized. The branch behind `c20ffc87` completed its registered cleanup and retains the expected admission table. **Neither branch is authorized for deletion, and no further SQL is authorized against either.** Branches are referred to by run id only. A further live run needs a new explicit decision and a brand-new branch.

**Accepted trade-off and residuals (read this).** Anonymous callers can exhaust the shared pool — 30 requests in an hour, 100 in a day — which protects the database but can keep legitimate users from starting to register for as long as the attacker persists. It does not guarantee registration availability and **is not sufficient for public registration by itself**; trusted per-source/edge protection is required before public launch. It bounds the **rate** of permanent registration-attempt growth, not the lifetime count, and permanent registration attempts still have no retention cleanup. Also still open and outside this slice: trusted ingress attribution (no header is read; none exists); existing-user login is unlimited (the separate residual of `login/options` minting unauthenticated challenges into `webauthn_challenges` was removed by Slice 3C-2, below; `login/verify` itself is still unlimited); login-driven provisioning advisory-lock contention (historical wording; Slice 3C-3a, below, only reduces re-entry into the claim on exhausted refusal paths and does not remove it); further unauthenticated abuse protections (3C-3); provider timeouts (3D); authenticated write limits (3E); the production provider-budget migration (Milestone 3A's `real_provider_budgets` is still unapplied on the real database); real-device registration compatibility, including a possible orphaned passkey from a false-positive limit; slow-upload and transport-level denial of service; and the unresolved deployment prerequisites (script CSP, a host-specific RP ID and origin, the platform duration limit). Milestone 3C-1 does not solve API abuse in general, and **public deployment remains NOT AUTHORIZED**.

**Deployment prerequisite (not done).** Applying the migration to the normal application database is a separate operator checkpoint with its own explicit authorization, and it must precede deployment of this code: without the table and its seed row every registration start answers the generic 500. A deliberate operator rerun that re-seeds a deleted row resets registration capacity to a full allowance.

**Offline verification history.** Before the live runs: 157 new tests; a full offline run of 5105 passed / 4 failed / 322 skipped (the known unrelated Practice `Date.now()` race plus three tests that hit a 5000 ms timeout only under full-suite load, each passing alone). Later offline work: 14 exact-SQL regression tests (the complete E1/E2 statements pinned against an independent reference, with 33 in-memory SQL mutations all rejected), the smoke harness and its 56 offline fixture tests, and the five S4 fixture-consistency tests; the last full offline run was 5200 passed / 0 failed / 348 skipped, with `tsc --noEmit`, ESLint and `git diff --check` clean. Two existing test files needed necessary edits (`provider-budget.test.ts`'s adjacency assertion and `session-security.test.ts`'s mocked runtime) and two shared gate tests gained one registration line each (`smoke-target.test.ts`, `smoke-gate-process.test.ts`).

### Public Readiness Milestone 3C — Slice 3C-2: Stateless Login Challenges (CLOSED at `7d66abe`; live-verified on ONE disposable branch ONLY; migration NOT applied to the application database; NOT deployable)

`POST /api/real/account/login/options` used to write one `webauthn_challenges` row per anonymous request (plus a cleanup `DELETE`): unbounded anonymous database write amplification. 3C-2 replaces that with a **stateless, signed, expiring challenge** and moves replay protection to a **used-marker table written only after a successful WebAuthn verification**. Milestone 3C is "Unauthenticated Rate Limits"; 3C-1 (global registration admission) is CLOSED at `46d1cc2`; 3C-3a (CLOSED at `beac686`) and 3C-3b (CLOSED at `48a98d9`) have since been built (see "Slice 3C-3a" and "Slice 3C-3b" below), 3C-3c is not authorized, and 3D (provider timeouts) and 3E (authenticated write limits) are not started; Milestone 2 stays DEFERRED; no milestone is renumbered; **Milestone 3 as a whole is not closed**. Keep these statuses distinct:

- *Technical implementation:* COMPLETE and independently audited. *Smoke harness:* independently audited; the first harness audit returned **HOLD** for a confirmed High harness defect (below), which was corrected offline before any live run; a final offline source preflight then passed.
- *Live verification:* ONE authorized disposable-Neon run, **`14751f64`, COMPLETED_CLEAN** (2026-10-21 UTC). *Independent live-evidence audit:* **PASSED** — 0 Critical, 0 High, 0 Medium, 1 Low, 5 Informational. *Technical closeout:* control-room GO.
- *Formal milestone closure:* **COMPLETE.** The operator's manual Git checkpoint is `7d66abe` ("feat: add replay-safe stateless WebAuthn login challenges", verified against `git log`), which contains the 3C-2 implementation and the documentation as first written. Wording in that commit's text — in this section or elsewhere — that calls closure pending, or the work uncommitted, is historical and superseded by this line. Closure is not a deployment approval, a migration of the application database, or a statement about public readiness.
- *Where the migration has run:* only on disposable branches (the 3C-2 smoke's scratch schemas and the `public` schema of its branch). It has **not** been applied to the normal application database, and applying it is a separate operator checkpoint that must precede deploying this code. **Public deployment remains NOT AUTHORIZED**, and a clean smoke is not a deployment approval.

**Challenge token** (`lib/real/server/login-challenge.ts`; no database access). 58 bytes, wire form its canonical **unpadded base64url, exactly 78 characters**: version `0x01`, purpose `0x01` (login only), an 8-byte big-endian expiration in milliseconds, a 16-byte random nonce, and a 32-byte HMAC-SHA256 (never truncated). Lifetime **five minutes**. The MAC covers a fixed domain string, the first 26 token bytes, and the RP ID (length-prefixed), so a token is valid for one ceremony kind and one relying party. The key is **HKDF-SHA256 over the trimmed `REAL_SESSION_SECRET`** with an empty salt and a login-challenge-specific info string — never the raw session secret, so it is independent of the session-cookie HMAC. There is no new environment variable and **no fallback to database login challenges or to an old key**: rotating `REAL_SESSION_SECRET` invalidates every outstanding login challenge (as it already ends every session). SimpleWebAuthn is handed the **raw 58 `Uint8Array` bytes** (a string would be re-encoded as UTF-8); `beginLogin` refuses to continue unless the library's `optionsJSON.challenge` equals the canonical encoding exactly. The application-clock expiry check is an **advisory** pre-filter (not expired, and not more than five minutes plus a 120 s skew ahead); PostgreSQL's clock is authoritative where the marker is written.

**Route and flow.** `login/options` performs **no database I/O**: it mints the token and builds the options. `login/verify` (`completeLogin`) first checks the token as signed, unexpired and for this RP ID (a forged, malformed, expired or wrong-RP token costs no database access and is refused), then verifies the WebAuthn assertion exactly as before (active passkey, `userHandle`, user verification), and only then **consumes the marker** — before the authenticator-counter update, before the account lookup, before any session is minted, and before a pending registration enters the provisioning pipeline (both branches). A replayed or expired challenge is refused; a thrown consumption (database error, malformed answer) propagates as the generic failure with **no session and no retry** of an uncertain write. A request that fails verification writes **no** marker. Registration and the other WebAuthn ceremonies are unchanged.

**Marker table** (`public.real_login_challenge_uses`, `schema.sql` "Login Challenge Uses" block, between Registration Admission and Provisioning Evidence): `nonce BYTEA` (primary key `real_login_challenge_uses_pkey`; CHECK `real_login_challenge_uses_nonce_length_check` = 16 bytes), `expires_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL`, `used_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL`, no defaults, and one non-unique btree index `real_login_challenge_uses_expires_at_idx` on `expires_at`; permanent, logged, no foreign key, trigger, rule, policy, inheritance or partitioning. No credential id, user id, IP, token body or purpose column — nothing identifies who used a challenge. The migration is idempotent and **fail-closed** (catalog proofs that an existing table and index under those names are exactly the intended ones, else it raises and rolls back; nothing is dropped, rebuilt or repaired).

**Marker statement** (`createNeonLoginChallengeUseStore`, `neon-store.ts`). **One autocommit SQL statement — never `sql.transaction`, never retried:** a bounded purge of markers whose challenge expired **more than one hour ago** (oldest first, at most **64** rows, `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`), and a conditional `INSERT` that runs only if the expiration is in the future and at most 10 minutes ahead by the **database** clock (`clock_timestamp()`), with `ON CONFLICT (nonce) DO NOTHING` and `RETURNING` a boolean `live` that re-checks expiry **after** any lock wait. Exactly one row with `live = true` is the only admission; zero rows (already used, expired, or pre-filtered) or `live = false` is a rejection; several rows, a non-boolean `live`, or a database error throws. The one-hour margin plus the post-insert clock check are what stop a stalled replay — started before expiry with a stale transaction-start `now()` while another session purged the expired marker — from being re-admitted. The earlier candidate (`DELETE … WHERE expires_at < now()` with `INSERT … WHERE expires_at > now()`) had exactly that stale-clock flaw and was rejected; the smoke reproduces the hazard against it (M8) only inside an owned scratch schema. Not claimed: that PostgreSQL's clock cannot step backwards by about an hour.

**Migration and statement identity (evidence-bearing — do not change without a fresh smoke on a brand-new branch).** Login Challenge Uses: **15070 characters, SHA-256 `00ff39691a33436ce75d2ff33534983bd408c95574ee4ad8ad9ff6bcdcbf1d1e`**. Unchanged and re-verified: Registration Admission 13101 / `b269ebcc…ac91f`, Handle Pay Rate Limits 11680 / `78c5557d…1513d`, Provider Budgets 11820 / `8213b46c…a5f5` (full digests above and in CLAUDE.md). The production marker statement was captured from the real adapter and compared with an independently written reference; the run recorded that it matched.

**Harness defect found by the independent harness audit (corrected before live).** The live run registers **54** scratch schemas; the shared ownership registry (`provider-budget-smoke.ts`) accepted only a fixed **40**, so the 41st registration (S5 variant `b10`) would have poisoned the run and left 40 schemas on the branch. `createRunOwnership(runId, { maxScratchSchemas })` now accepts an explicit capacity — a whole number 1 to 60, rejected rather than clamped; the **shared default stays 40**, the hard cap is **60**, and the object (120) and marker (600) caps did not change. The 3C-2 harness asks for **56**. An offline replay of the exact registration order fails at `b10` against the old behavior and passes against the new, and a temporary reintroduction of the defect failed four tests. The same correction pass also metered every cleanup statement against the 340 ceiling, removed an unenforced per-operation timeout declaration, split S4 into two 90-second cases, bound the shared helpers into the source digest, registered the suite in the shared gate tests, and labelled the race timing evidence by how it is known.

**Source identity.** The authorized live baseline is the digest `681b7c4ebc0a714ac80d7d3a5d38b4c894d4258e3e53ddb5b85d301a6c871edc`, recomputed offline before and after the run. It is a hash over: the migration block, the pinned marker-statement reference, the production adapter function, the Neon client constant/factory/store wiring, `login-challenge.ts`, `login-challenge-uses.ts`, the smoke file and its fixture, and five shared helper files (provider-budget, registration-admission and provisioning-limits smoke fixtures, `handles-smoke-db.ts`, `smoke-run-ids.ts`). **It is not a digest of the repository.** It does not cover `login.ts`, `webauthn.ts`, the routes, `runtime.ts`, the rest of `neon-store.ts` or `schema.sql`, the Vitest configuration, or dependencies, and it says nothing about the database: it is not evidence that the target is a fresh disposable branch. The run's confirmation is bound to source digest, endpoint and run id. Executed file hashes: smoke `7f0c395f2daf2af9a2a824daa1286608f03ce86bef302554013ead49e062d8ef`; fixture `1613c7d200c653b774a3b6531a608039e89a641eb11581eb15b64634b3a51a17`; harness test `9cb388a562e91d033ee3c86d0c14365a9b93773f702e7c7c13653193aa553ead`.

**Live run `14751f64` (2026-10-21 UTC).** Disposable branch `ocf-m3c2-login-smoke-02` (parent `production`; the branch and endpoint identifiers are held by the operator and deliberately not recorded here); one pooled connection, PostgreSQL 18.6, READ COMMITTED on the single-statement path production uses. **Branch identity and parentage are operator-confirmed facts; the independent auditor did not connect to Neon to verify them, and the saved evidence deliberately omits the endpoint and confirmation.** One live invocation was reported; the evidence supports one execution but cannot exclude an invocation that refused before writing evidence. The Builder reported exit code 0 and about 162 s; the structured evidence and source were independently verified, but the console exit code and the offline pass count come from the Builder's report. Result: **24 tests passed, 0 failures** (3 offline, 19 live scenario cases, 1 completeness check, 1 final cleanup check); outcome `COMPLETED_CLEAN`, database classification `baseline_plus_expected_delta`, not poisoned, no unknown or in-flight operations, no automatic retries.

- *S1–S7 (schema).* Missing table fails closed (42P01). The migration applied in a scratch schema, reran idempotently, preserved a recorded marker across reruns, and matched the exact catalog descriptor; the primary key, the 16-byte CHECK and NOT NULL rejected bad rows (23505, 23514, 23502). Exact hand-built copies, with and without the expiry index, were accepted (the missing index was created, rows kept). **All 49 look-alikes were constructible and refused without modification** — 47 by the migration's own guard (P0001), two by PostgreSQL itself before that guard reached them (a missing `expires_at` column, 42703; a JSON `expires_at` with no btree operator class, 42704, both fail-closed); a nonexistent target schema was refused and not created; the block then applied to `public`, reran, and preserved an owned marker. 57 database refusals were recorded; 111 fixtures constructed, none failed.
- *M1–M3.* READ COMMITTED confirmed on the production path; inside one transaction `now()` stays frozen while `clock_timestamp()` moves; `live` arrives as a JavaScript boolean; first use consumed and replays rejected; expiry rejected/accepted correctly at the tested margins (−10 s, −1 s, +20 s, +9.5 min, +10.5 min, +1 year). **Boundaries were tested with margins, not exact-millisecond equality.**
- *M4 / M5.* 60 simultaneous requests for one nonce: **1 consumed, 59 rejected**, with 60 sessions observed waiting together. 60 simultaneous requests for 60 distinct nonces: **60 consumed**, 60 observed waiting.
- *M6 / M7 / M8 (stale-clock and blocked-insert races).* **M6:** a replay started before expiry was observed blocked behind another session's uncommitted removal of its marker and was **rejected** after that session committed; its marker row remained. The holder's start and release instants were measured on the database clock inside its batch, and the release was after the expiry. **M7:** a request blocked behind an uncommitted insert that rolled back after expiry was rejected, its marker present. A rollback holder's own batch ends in an error that discards its results, so its start and release instants were **NOT OBSERVED** — M7's protection rests on the observed blocking, the rollback, the marker state and the rejection (and the request's own post-insert check), not on a measured release timestamp; do not cite one. **M8:** the deliberately unsafe historical candidate **did re-admit** in the same interleaving, restricted to an owned scratch schema. Together M6 and M8 are meaningful live evidence for the approved correction.
- *M9 / M10 (purge).* **M9:** of 100 markers expired more than an hour ago the first statement removed exactly the oldest 64 (36 remained), the second (even though its own insert was refused by the pre-filter) removed the rest, and the 20 markers inside the margin and the 3 live ones were untouched. **M10:** ten row-locked expired markers were **skipped, not waited for** (the purge returned while the holder still held them); an eight-statement burst released together neither deadlocked nor lost any marker the run knew to be present (**111 identities survived**). **Limitation:** M10's sequential drain loop executed **zero rounds** because the burst had already removed the remaining aged markers, so M10 did **not** demonstrate repeated one-statement-at-a-time draining; the 64-rows-per-statement bound was demonstrated by M9. The loop's source comment overstates this; it is an accepted Low and was deliberately left (the source is part of the audited digest).
- *M11.* The production statement text, qualified onto a scratch table with no unique key, failed closed with 42P10 and wrote nothing.

**Resources (actual / ceiling) and cleanup.** Operations 685 / 2000; fixture statements 15 / 90; fixture rows 335 / 700; polls 24 / 400; peak concurrency 62 / 64; scratch schemas 54 / 60 (registry 56); cleanup statements 230 / 340 — all reconciled, none exceeded. **Cleanup:** 224 / 224 registered schema steps confirmed (inventory and dependency reads, object and schema drops, residue read), 111 known-present markers deleted by exact name, final marker residue 0, drops RESTRICT only, no registered schema left; the final schema list is `public` only. **Final database delta:** the 11 measured baseline public tables kept their row counts, content digests and shape digests; one expected table was added — `public.real_login_challenge_uses`, **empty**. This is the baseline **plus** that delta, **not** a restoration, and the comparison covered the measured public tables and schema names, **not every PostgreSQL object category**.

**Evidence files** (private, mode 0600, git-ignored under `/evidence/`, verified free of credentials; never modify, move, copy, commit or paste their contents): `evidence/login-challenge-uses-smoke-14751f64-start.json`, `evidence/login-challenge-uses-smoke-14751f64-final.json`.

**Lasting branch and run rules.** The disposable branch `ocf-m3c2-login-smoke-02` is **spent and must not be reused** for any smoke (it holds the expected marker table, and a rerun on it is not a fresh-branch run). **Run `14751f64` and its confirmation must never be reused or re-executed.** A further live run needs a new explicit decision, a brand-new branch with independent provenance evidence, and a fresh run id. The earlier spent runs (`ced714ee`, `c20ffc87`, `6d32fdf8`, `a630d8ff`) stay spent. No further SQL is authorized against any of these branches.

**What the evidence does NOT establish.** External branch provenance (operator-confirmed only); the normal application database's state (the migration is not applied there); every PostgreSQL object category; exact-millisecond expiry equality; M7's holder instants; repeated sequential draining (M10); real-device passkey compatibility of the 58-byte challenge, real browsers, or the routes end to end (no WebAuthn ceremony was performed); PITR/restore or clock-anomaly behavior (a backward clock step near an hour is outside the design); or behavior behind a proxy or CDN. Source-header wording that predates the live run — the smoke file's "never been run live", the `schema.sql` block's "NOT APPLIED ANYWHERE", and comments in `login-challenge-uses.ts` / `neon-store.ts` ("not deployable yet", "needs disposable-Neon verification; none has been run") — is **historical and superseded by this section**; it was deliberately not edited, because the files are part of the audited digest and the migration statement is evidence-bearing.
